Forcing with Language Fragments, Extending Namba Forcing, and Models of Theories with Constraints in Interpretation
Desmond Lau

Abstract
We develop a forcing framework based on the idea of amalgamating language fragments into a theory with a canonical term model. We then demonstrate the usefulness of this framework by applying it to variants of the extended Namba problem, as well as to the analysis of models of certain theories with constraints in interpretation ( TCIs ). The foundations for a theory of TCIs and their models are laid in parallel to the development of our framework, and are of independent interest.



1
Introduction

Forcing is a technique in mathematical logic, whereby a set is proven to exist through a non-constructive but coherent assembly of known components. It is most often used in relative consistency proofs, and has been so ever since Cohen invented syntactic forcing in 1963, when he proved that the continuum hypothesis is independent of
\mathsf{ZFC}
in
[
1
]
and
[
2
]
.

An application of forcing involves coming up with a partial order (called a
forcing notion
) and analysing a filter (called a
generic filter
) intersecting suitably many dense subsets of the aforementioned partial order. A set of which existence we want to show (called a
generic object
) typically manifests as an amalgamation of members of the generic filter. In set theoretic applications, a generic object generates an extension (called a
generic extension
) of the original universe.

Over the years, set theorists have discovered important relationships between forcing notions and their generic objects/extensions. These relationships usually associate higher-order properties of a partial order with its forcing consequences, to the extent that the study of such properties has become a good description of forcing theory. Now, in order to derive a generic extension we desire, we need to construct a forcing notion drawing from forcing theory with our generic object in mind, at the same time easing tensions between requirements. This balancing act can be extremely tough, in part because it sees little in the way of systematic support.

In this paper we develop a framework in which certain desiderata of a generic object can be naturally realised. Using this framework, all we need to do is to translate requirements on the generic extension into requirements on the generic object. We see via two non-trivial examples, how this structured approach can make things more convenient and intuitive in practice.

It turns out that the bare-bones framework we initially envisioned can be adorned with additional layers of syntactic sugar to capture analogous properties of a specific type of first-order structures. These structures are incidentally models of what we term first-order
theories with constraints in interpretation
(
TCIs
). The concept of a first-order TCI, in some sense, generalises that of a first-order theory in logic, and can be useful in expressing different kinds of objects logicians care about. We devote some space in this paper to the basic theory of TCIs and their models, before relating forcing to generic models of TCIs in a variety of ways that traverses set theory and computability theory. As an aside, we argue for a way to characterise the expressiveness of forcing as a technique, by the kind of truths it is able to impose on generic objects.

1.1
Sectional Content and Dependencies

Section
2
lays the technical foundation for the rest of the paper. Potential philosophical and meta-theoretic concerns are addressed, prerequisite knowledge highlighted, and background readings recommended. Important definitions and conventions are made explicit, especially those that are more niched, and those that lack community consensus. Consequentially, the topics related to forcing and generic iterations (Subsections
2.4
to
2.7
) tend to get more comprehensive treatments than the others. All subsequent sections depend on Subsections
2.1
to
2.4
, whereas the materials in Subsections
2.5
to
2.7
are only referenced in Subsection
4.3
.

Section
3
concerns itself with the development of our central framework for forcing. The technical machinery of this section concentrates around Lemma
3.42
, which itself is a generalisation of Lemma
3.56
. On the other hand, Lemma
3.56
is the accessible and more applicable backbone of the paper, a throughline tying subsequent sections together. Subsection
5.1
is notable for not depending on Lemma
3.56
, nor in fact, on any of Section
3
’s technology.

The entirety of Section
4
is devoted to applying the forcing framework developed in Section
3
to variants of the extended Namba problem. Said framework is used to construct specific forcing notions that give rise to generic extensions satisfying various sets of requirements. We start with a relatively simple construction in Theorem
4.11
, before extending it to solve a more difficult problem in Theorem
4.41
. No other section is dependent on what transpires here. (N.B. The proof of Lemma
3.57
references Remark
4.14
in parentheses, but there is no strict dependency — the low level subtleties in the remark are unnecessary and might detract from the clarity of the proof.)

Section
5
introduces the notions of (first-order) TCIs and models of TCIs, before relating them to forcing and genericity. In particular, Subsection
5.1
develops the basic theory of TCIs and their models, and can be read right after Section
2
. The other subsections depend in part on the results of Section
3
, and give applications of Section
3
’s forcing framework to more general contexts of genericity. Here, genericity is investigated in both set-theoretic and computability-theoretic senses of the word. Like Section
4
, this section is not the object of any dependency.

2
Preliminaries

2.1
The Meta-theory

At the meta-level, it suffices to assume
\mathsf{ZFC}
. We frame relative consistency proofs involving additional assumptions as proofs of statements of the form

\text{``}\mathrm{Con}(\mathsf{ZFC}+\phi)\implies\mathrm{Con}(\mathsf{ZFC}+\psi)\text{''}

over
\mathsf{ZFC}
, where
\phi
is the conjunction of the relevant assumptions. Implicit are the invocations of Gödel’s completeness theorem at the meta-level, whenever we argue using models (a.k.a.
universes
) of set theory.

Our meta-theoretic approach to forcing is only slightly more complicated. Conventionally we start with a countable transitive model of
\mathsf{ZFC}
, called a
ground model
, which is not guaranteed to exist by our meta-theory. There are many ways to sidestep this problem and treat the statement “countable transitive ground model” as a convenient abuse of notation, a few of which are noted by Kunen in IV.5 of
[
14
]
. We adopt the first approach detailed in IV.5.1 of
[
14
]
, an approach that is pretty much standard in the community, and one we feel is most immediately and formally accessible.

2.2
Basic Mathematical Logic

Unless specified otherwise, we follow the standard definitions of concepts related to the syntax and semantics of first-order logic, as seen in e.g.
[
10
]
.

Convention 2.1
.

(1)

We call any set of first order formulas a
first-order language
.

(2)

We assume the first-order languages we consider to contain only
\neg,\wedge,\vee,\implies,\iff
as their zeroth-order logical symbols, interpreted semantically in the usual sense.

(3)

Each first-order logical symbol is identified with a unique member of
H(\omega)\setminus\omega
.

(4)

Given a first-order language
\mathcal{L}
, let
\mathrm{Ter}(\mathcal{L})
denote the set of all terms occurring in (some formula in)
\mathcal{L}
.

(5)

A first-order structure
\mathfrak{A}
is presented in the form
(A;\mathcal{I})
, where
A
is the base set of
\mathfrak{A}
and
\mathcal{I}
is the interpretation of the signature of
\mathfrak{A}
. In this presentation, the signature of
\mathfrak{A}
is simply
dom(\mathcal{I})
.

Sometimes, when the correspondence between a signature and its interpretation is clear, we might write
(A;\mathcal{I})
as
(A;\vec{S})
, where
\vec{S}
is some ordering of
ran(\mathcal{I})
.

(6)

The signature of a first-order language or structure can contain only relation symbols of a non-zero finite arity, function symbols of a non-zero finite arity, and constant symbols, identified as follows:

•

a
n
-ary relation symbol is a triple of the form
(X,0,n)
,

•

a
n
-ary function symbol is a triple of the form
(X,1,n)
, and

•

a constant symbol is a pair of the form
(X,2)
.

We call any such symbol a
signature-related symbol
.

(7)

A
form-preserving signature embedding
is an injective function from a set of signature-related symbols into the class of signature-related symbols, such that

•

n
-ary relation symbols are mapped to
n
-ary relation symbols,

•

n
-ary function symbols are mapped to
n
-ary function symbols, and

•

constant symbols are mapped to constant symbols.

(8)

We will assume that the class of signature-related symbols is disjoint from the set of first-order logical symbols.

(9)

The variables occurring in any first-order formula must come from a fixed countably infinite set
\mathrm{Var}
. We will assume that
\mathrm{Var}
is disjoint from both the set of first-order logical symbols and the class of signature-related symbols.

(10)

A string over a vocabulary set
\Sigma
is a member of
\Sigma^{<\omega}
.

(11)

We sometimes define procedures in which subformulas of a first-order formula are replaced by other formulas. In these cases, for convenience’s sake, what follows will be adopted.

Let

•

\phi
be a first-order formula,

•

\varphi
be a subformula of
\phi
, and

•

\ulcorner x\urcorner
be a variable occurring in
\phi
.

Suppose we are to replace
\varphi
in
\phi
with some formula
\varphi^{\prime}
. Let
\ulcorner y\urcorner
be a bound variable in
\varphi^{\prime}
. Unless otherwise stated, we always assume

\ulcorner x\urcorner\neq\ulcorner y\urcorner\text{.}

We write “structure(s)” as the abbreviation of “first-order structure(s)” henceforth. There should be no confusion as these are the only type of structures we will be dealing with.

Definition 2.2
.

Given any set
X
and any signature
\sigma
, the
language associated with

(X;\sigma)
is the set of first-order formulas over
\sigma
with parameters from
X
. Similarly, given any structure
\mathfrak{A}=(A;\mathcal{I})
, the
language associated with

\mathfrak{A}
is the set of first-order formulas over the signature of
\mathfrak{A}
with parameters from
A
.

Definition 2.3
.

For any structure
\mathfrak{A}=(A;\mathcal{I})
, a
\mathfrak{A}
-valuation
is a function from
\mathrm{Var}
into
A
.

Definition 2.4
.

The
size of a structure

\mathfrak{A}=(A;\mathcal{I})
is equal to

max\{|A|,|\mathcal{I}|\}.

We say
\mathfrak{A}
is a
countable structure
iff its size is a countable cardinal.

Definition 2.5
.

Let
\phi
be a first-order formula over a signature
\sigma
. We inductively define what it means for
\phi
to be
\Pi_{n}
or
\Sigma_{n}
as
n
ranges over the natural numbers.

(1)

If
n=0
, then
\phi
is
\Pi_{0}
iff
\phi
is
\Sigma_{0}
iff
\phi
is quantifier-free.

(2)

If
n=m+1
for some
m<\omega
, then

(a)

\phi
is
\Pi_{n}
iff there is a
\Sigma_{m}
formula
\varphi
, a number
k<\omega
, and variable symbols
x_{1},\dots,x_{k}
not bound in
\varphi
, such that

\phi=\ulcorner\forall x_{1}\dots\forall x_{k}\ \varphi\urcorner\text{, and}

(b)

\phi
is
\Sigma_{n}
iff there is a
\Pi_{m}
formula
\varphi
, a number
k<\omega
, and variable symbols
x_{1},\dots,x_{k}
not bound in
\varphi
, such that

\phi=\ulcorner\exists x_{1}\dots\exists x_{k}\ \varphi\urcorner\text{.}

Note that if
k=0
in
(2)
(2)()
and
(2)
(2)()
, then
\phi
is
\Sigma_{m}
and
\Pi_{m}
respectively.

2.3
Basic Set Theory

Unless specified otherwise, we follow the standard definitions of concepts typically encountered in a foundational set theory course, following e.g.
[
9
]
.

Convention 2.6
.

(1)

Unless otherwise specified,
V
always refers to the universe we are currently working in. For all practical purposes, we can assume it is a countable transitive model of
\mathsf{ZFC}
, so that it doubles as a ground model in case forcing arguments are to be run.

(2)

We adopt the set-theoretic interpretation of functions as sets of ordered pairs satisfying certain properties. So when we say a function is definable, we actually mean its graph is definable as a set — usually a subset of an ambient structure that should be clear in context, if not explicitly mentioned.

(3)

We fix in
V
, a distinguished first-order relation symbol
\ulcorner\in\urcorner
.

(4)

A first-order formula is
in the language of set theory
iff it is a formula over the signature
\{\ulcorner\in\urcorner\}
.

(5)

We say a structure
\mathfrak{A}
is
a structure in the language of set theory
iff

•

the signature of
\mathfrak{A}
is the singleton set
\{\ulcorner\in\urcorner\}
, and

•

\mathfrak{A}
interprets
\ulcorner\in\urcorner
as the membership relation on
V
restricted to the base set of
\mathfrak{A}
.

More formally,

\mathfrak{A}=(A;\mathcal{I})\text{ and }\mathcal{I}=\{(\ulcorner\in\urcorner,\in\cap A)\},

where
\in
is the membership relation on
V
. In this case, we can just write
\mathfrak{A}=(A;\in)
.

(6)

A first-order formula is
in a possibly expanded language of set theory
iff it is a formula over some signature
\sigma
satisfying
\ulcorner\in\urcorner\in\sigma
.

(7)

We say a structure
\mathfrak{A}
is
a structure in a possibly expanded language of set theory
iff we omit the cardinality requirement on the signature of
\mathfrak{A}
in
(5)
. More formally,

\mathfrak{A}=(A;\mathcal{I})\text{ and }\mathcal{I}=\{(\ulcorner\in\urcorner,\in\cap A)\}\cup\vec{X},

where
\vec{X}
is some function and
\in
is the membership relation on
V
. In this case, we can just write
\mathfrak{A}=(A;\in,\vec{X})
.

(8)

We identify a universe of set theory
W
with the structure
(W;\in)
. This should not cause confusion in the circumstances we find ourselves in.

(9)

A real is a subset of
\omega
. We identify a real with its characteristic function on
\omega
, as is standard in computability theory. As in
(8)
, this ambiguity should not cause any confusion.

We overload and expand on Definition
2.5
when dealing with the special case of set-theoretic languages.

Definition 2.7
.

Let
\phi
be a first-order formula over a possibly expanded language of set theory. We inductively define what it means for
\phi
to be
\Delta_{n}
,
\Pi_{n}
or
\Sigma_{n}
as
n
ranges over the natural numbers.

(1)

If
n=0
, then
\phi
is
\Delta_{n}
iff
\phi
is
\Pi_{n}
iff
\phi
is
\Sigma_{n}
iff every quantifier occurring in
\phi
is bounded by
\in
.

(2)

If
n=m+1
for some
m<\omega
, then

(a)

\phi
is
\Pi_{n}
iff there is a
\Sigma_{m}
formula
\varphi
, a number
k<\omega
, and variable symbols
x_{1},\dots,x_{k}
not bound in
\varphi
, such that

\phi=\ulcorner\forall x_{1}\dots\forall x_{k}\ \varphi\urcorner\text{, and}

(b)

\phi
is
\Sigma_{n}
iff there is a
\Pi_{m}
formula
\varphi
, a number
k<\omega
, and variable symbols
x_{1},\dots,x_{k}
not bound in
\varphi
, such that

\phi=\ulcorner\exists x_{1}\dots\exists x_{k}\ \varphi\urcorner\text{.}

(c)

\phi
is
\Delta_{n}
iff

\circ

\phi
is
\Pi_{n}
, and

\circ

for some
\Sigma_{n}
formula
\varphi
,

\mathsf{ZFC}\vdash\phi\iff\varphi\text{.}

Note that if
k=0
respectively in
(2)
(2)(b)
and
(2)
(2)(b)
, then
\phi
is
\Sigma_{m}
and
\Pi_{m}
respectively.

Most of the time, the context should indicate clearly which interpretation to adopt. Nevertheless, we shall try as much as possible to disambiguate things in this respect, and highlight each time Definition
2.7
is used instead of Definition
2.5
.

Definition 2.8
.

Let
X
be a set and
\mathfrak{A}
be a structure in a possibly expanded language of set theory with base set
A
. We say
X
is
definable in the language associated with

\mathfrak{A}
iff

X=\{y\in A:\mathfrak{A}\models\phi(y)\}

for some formula
\phi
with one free variable in the language associated with
\mathfrak{A}
.

Definition 2.9
.

Let

•

X
be a set,

•

\mathfrak{A}
be a structure in a possibly expanded language of set theory with base set
A
, and

•

n<\omega
.

We say
X
is
\Sigma_{n}
-definable
(resp.
\Pi_{n}
-definable
and
\Delta_{n}
-definable
)
in the language associated with

\mathfrak{A}
iff

X=\{y\in A:\mathfrak{A}\models\phi(y)\}

for some
\Sigma_{n}
(resp.
\Pi_{n}
and
\Delta_{n}
) formula (in accordance with Definition
2.7
)
\phi
with one free variable in the language associated with
\mathfrak{A}
.

We can generalise Definition
2.9
by relaxing the language requirement.

Definition 2.10
.

Let

•

X
be a class,

•

\mathfrak{A}
be a structure in a possibly expanded language of set theory with base class
A
and signature
\sigma
, such that

\circ

A
is transitive, and

\circ

\mathfrak{A}\models\mathsf{ZFC}
, and

•

n<\omega
.

We say
X
is
\Sigma_{n}
-definable
(resp.
\Pi_{n}
-definable
and
\Delta_{n}
-definable
)
in

\mathfrak{A}
iff

X=\{y\in A:\mathfrak{A}\models\phi(y)\}

for some
\Sigma_{n}
(resp.
\Pi_{n}
and
\Delta_{n}
) formula (in the sense of Definition
2.7
)
\phi
with one free variable over
\sigma
.

If in addition,

•

\mathfrak{A}=(A;\in)
, and

•

\sigma=\{\ulcorner\in\urcorner\}
,

we may identify
\mathfrak{A}
with
A
and say
X
is
\Sigma_{n}
-definable
(resp.
\Pi_{n}
-definable
and
\Delta_{n}
-definable
)
in

A
iff
X
is
\Sigma_{n}
-definable (resp.
\Pi_{n}
-definable and
\Delta_{n}
-definable) in
\mathfrak{A}
.

We say
X
is
\Sigma_{n}
-definable
(resp.
\Pi_{n}
-definable
and
\Delta_{n}
-definable
) iff
X
is
\Sigma_{n}
-definable (resp.
\Pi_{n}
-definable and
\Delta_{n}
-definable) in
\mathfrak{A}^{\prime}
for every structure
\mathfrak{A}^{\prime}
in a possibly expanded language of set theory such that

\circ

\mathfrak{A}^{\prime}
has a transitive base class, and

\circ

\mathfrak{A}^{\prime}\models\mathsf{ZFC}
.

Definition 2.11
.

Let
\mathfrak{A}
be a structure in a possibly expanded language of set theory. When we say

\text{``}\mathfrak{A}\textit{ is a model of a sufficiently strong set theory'',}

(2.1)

we mean to emphasise the low strength of the set theory
\mathfrak{A}
satisfies.

In more concrete terms, what we typically term a
set theory
, is a set of first-order formulas in the language of set theory. Examples of set theories include

•

the set of axioms of
\mathsf{ZFC}
, and

•

the set of axioms of
Kripke-Platek set theory
(
without infinity
), denoted
\mathsf{KP}
, which is a much weaker theory than
\mathsf{ZFC}
.

For convenience’s sake, one may always assume (
2.1
) to mean

\text{``}\mathfrak{A}\models T\text{ for some set theory }T\text{ such that}

•

\mathsf{KP}\subset T
, and

•

T\vdash\ulcorner\forall x\ (\text{``}x\text{ is Dedekind-finite}\implies x\text{ is finite''})\urcorner
”.

Definition 2.12
.

Given (externally) a class
\mathbf{M}
of classes, we say a definition
\varphi
in
n
variables — for some finite number
n
— is
absolute for

\mathbf{M}
iff for every two members
X,Y
of
\mathbf{M}
such that
X\subset Y
, and for every sequence
\vec{x}
of
n
members of
X
,

X\models\varphi(\vec{x})\iff Y\models\varphi(\vec{x})\text{.}

Definition 2.13
.

Given that
V^{\prime}
and
W
are models of
\mathsf{ZFC}
, we say
V^{\prime}
is a
weak inner model
of
W
(or equivalently,
W
is a
weak outer model
of
V^{\prime}
) iff

•

V^{\prime}
and
W
are both transitive, and

•

V^{\prime}\subset W
.

If in addition,
V^{\prime}
and
W
share the same ordinals i.e.
ORD^{V^{\prime}}=ORD^{W}
, then
V^{\prime}
is an
inner model
of
W
(or equivalently,
W
is an
outer model
of
V^{\prime}
).

Definition 2.14
.

Let
Y
be any set.

We say
X

codes

Y
(or equivalently,
X

is a code of

Y
) iff

•

X
is a set of ordinals, and

•

every transitive model of
\mathsf{ZFC-Powerset}
containing
X
also contains
Y
.

X

codes

Y

as a real
iff
X
codes
Y
and
X\subset\omega
.

Y

has a real code
(or equivalently,
Y

can be coded as a real
) iff
X
codes
Y
as a real for some
X
.

Lemma 2.15
.

Let
X
be a set with
|trcl(X)|=\kappa
. Then there is
A\subset\kappa
such that
A
codes
X
.

In particular, any set with a countable transitive closure has a real code.

Proof.

Let
Y:=trcl(X)\cup\{X\}
. Note that

X\text{ is the unique }\!\in\!\text{-maximal member of }Y

(2.2)

in any transitive model of
\mathsf{ZFC-Powerset}
containing
Y
. Choose any bijection
f
from
Y
into
\kappa
. Define

R:=\{(f(x),f(y))\in\kappa\times\kappa:(x,y)\in Y\times Y\text{ and }x\in y\}.

Now
R
can be thought of as a subset
A
of
\kappa
via the canonical Gödel numbering of pairs. If
V^{\prime}
is a transitive model of
\mathsf{ZFC-Powerset}
containing
A
, then we can recover
R
in
V^{\prime}
. The Mostowski collapse function works on
R
in
V^{\prime}
to give us
Y\in V^{\prime}
. This implies
X\in V^{\prime}
since
X
definable from
Y
via (
2.2
).
∎

2.4
Forcing and Generic Objects

Following the meta-theoretic convention highlighted in Subsection
2.1
, we start with a countable transitive ground model
V
. In the language of forcing, a forcing notion in
V
is just any partial order in
V
. If
\mathbb{P}
is a forcing notion in
V
, then a
\mathbb{P}
-generic filter over

V
is a filter on
\mathbb{P}
intersecting all dense subsets of
\mathbb{P}
in
V
.

Given a forcing notion
\mathbb{P}
in
V
, the class of
\mathbb{P}
-names in
V
— denoted
V^{\mathbb{P}}
— and the forcing relation
\Vdash_{\mathbb{P}}^{V}
(which relates elements of
\mathbb{P}
with formulas parametrised by
\mathbb{P}
-names in
V
), are both essential to a forcing argument involving
\mathbb{P}
carried out in
V
. These two classes are uniformly definable in
V
over the class of all forcing notions
\mathbb{P}
.
\mathbb{P}
-names in
V
are “evaluated at" a
\mathbb{P}
-generic filter
g
over
V
to obtain the
\mathbb{P}
-generic extension
V[g]
, which is necessarily countable and transitive. In other words, if
g
is a
\mathbb{P}
-generic filter over
V
, then

V\subset V[g]=\{\dot{x}[g]:\dot{x}\in V^{\mathbb{P}}\},

where
\dot{x}[g]
means “
x
evaluated at
g
". The evaluation procedure is done outside
V
because
g
typically (in order to be of use at all) does not exist in
V
.

Convention 2.16
.

When it is clear that the background universe is
V
, we suppress mention of
V
when writing forcing relations in
V
. This means that given a forcing notion
\mathbb{P}
in
V
,
\Vdash_{\mathbb{P}}
is used interchangeably with
\Vdash_{\mathbb{P}}^{V}
.

Definition 2.17
.

We call
W
a
generic extension
(or a
forcing extension
) of
V
iff there exists a forcing notion
\mathbb{P}
in
V
and a
\mathbb{P}
-generic filter
g
over
V
, such that
W=V[g]
.

Definition 2.18
.

We write “
\Vdash_{\mathbb{P}}\phi
" to mean

\text{``}\forall p\ (p\in\mathbb{P}\implies p\Vdash_{\mathbb{P}}\phi)\text{''.}

Remark 2.19
.

A theorem fundamental to the technique of forcing intricately connects the forcing relation
\Vdash_{\mathbb{P}}^{V}
with truth in
\mathbb{P}
-generic extensions. It goes as follows:

If
\mathbb{P}
is a forcing notion in
V
,
p\in\mathbb{P}
,
\phi
is a formula with
n
free variables, and
\dot{x}_{1}
, …,
\dot{x}_{n}
are
\mathbb{P}
-names in
V
, then

•

​
\begin{aligned} &p\Vdash_{\mathbb{P}}\phi(\dot{x}_{1},\dots,\dot{x}_{n})\iff\\
&\forall g\ ((g\text{ is }\mathbb{P}\text{-generic over }V\text{ and }p\in g)\\
&\implies V[g]\models\phi(\dot{x}_{1}[g],\dots,\dot{x}_{n}[g])),\text{ and}\end{aligned}

•

​
\begin{aligned} \forall g\ (&(g\text{ is }\mathbb{P}\text{-generic over }V\text{ and }V[g]\models\phi(\dot{x}_{1}[g],\dots,\dot{x}_{n}[g]))\\
&\implies\exists q\ (q\Vdash_{\mathbb{P}}\phi(\dot{x}_{1},\dots,\dot{x}_{n})\text{ and }q\in g))\text{.}\end{aligned}

This theorem, colloquially known as the
forcing theorem
, enables us to reason about truth in generic extensions from within the ground model, and often reduces the argument from one about semantic entailment to one pertaining to combinatorial properties of partial orders. For a technical lowdown of forcing terminology and the proof of the forcing theorem, the reader is encouraged to read Chapter IV of
[
14
]
.

Definition 2.20
.

Let
\mathbb{P}=(P,\leq_{\mathbb{P}})
be a forcing notion and
X
be any set. The
upward closure of

X

in

\mathbb{P}
, denoted
\mathrm{UC}(\mathbb{P},X)
, is the set

\{p\in P:\exists q\ (q\in X\text{ and }q\leq_{\mathbb{P}}p)\}.

Definition 2.21
.

Let
\mathbb{P}=(P,\leq_{\mathbb{P}})
be a forcing notion,
D\subset P
and
A
be any set. We say a subset
g
of
P

meets

D

in

A
iff

g\cap\{p\in P:p\in D\text{ or }\forall q\ (q\leq_{\mathbb{P}}p\implies q\not\in D)\}\cap A\neq\emptyset.

We say
g

meets

D
iff
g
meets
D
in
V
.

Definition 2.22
.

Let
\mathbb{P}=(P,\leq_{\mathbb{P}})
be a forcing notion and
\mathfrak{A}=(A;\in,\vec{X})
be a structure in a possibly expanded language of set theory. We say a subset
g
of
P
is
\mathbb{P}
-generic over

\mathfrak{A}
(or
g
is a
\mathbb{P}
-generic subset over

\mathfrak{A}
) iff
g
meets
D
in
A
for all
D
such that

•

D\subset P

•

D
is dense in
\mathbb{P}
, and

•

D
is definable in the language associated with
\mathfrak{A}
.

If in addition,
g
is a filter on
\mathbb{P}
, then we call
g
a
\mathbb{P}
-generic filter over

\mathfrak{A}
.

Definition 2.23
.

Let
\mathbb{P}=(P,\leq_{\mathbb{P}})
be a forcing notion and
\mathfrak{A}=(A;\in,\vec{X})
be a structure in a possibly expanded language of set theory. Further, let
n<\omega
. We say a subset
g
of
P
is
\mathbb{P}
-
\Sigma_{n}
-generic
(resp.
\mathbb{P}
-
\Pi_{n}
-generic
and
\mathbb{P}
-
\Delta_{n}
-generic
)
over

\mathfrak{A}
iff
g
meets
D
in
A
for all
D
such that

•

D\subset P

•

D
is dense in
\mathbb{P}
, and

•

D
is
\Sigma_{n}
-definable (resp.
\Pi_{n}
-definable and
\Delta_{n}
-definable) in the language associated with
\mathfrak{A}
.

If in addition,
g
is a filter on
\mathbb{P}
, then we call
g
a
\mathbb{P}
-
\Sigma_{n}
-generic
(resp.
\mathbb{P}
-
\Pi_{n}
-generic
and
\mathbb{P}
-
\Delta_{n}
-generic
)
filter over

\mathfrak{A}
.

Definition 2.24
.

Let
\mathbb{P}
be a forcing notion and
\mathfrak{A}=(A;\in,\vec{X})
be a
transitive
structure in a possibly expanded language of set theory. A set
x
is a
(\mathbb{P},\mathfrak{A})
-generic object
iff there exists
g
a
\mathbb{P}
-generic filter over
\mathfrak{A}
such that

•

x\in A[g]
, and

•

g
is definable in the language associated with
(A\cup\{x\};\in,\vec{X})
,

in which case we say
g

witnesses

x
is a
(\mathbb{P},\mathfrak{A})
-generic object.

Definition 2.25
.

Let
x
be any set. We call
x
a
generic object
iff there is a pair
(\mathbb{P},\mathfrak{A})
for which
x
is a
(\mathbb{P},\mathfrak{A})
-generic object. We call
x
a
V
-generic object
iff there is
\mathbb{P}
for which
x
is a
(\mathbb{P},V)
-generic object.

Observation 2.26
.

Let
\mathbb{P}=(P,\leq_{\mathbb{P}})
be a forcing notion and
X
be any set. Then there is a structure
\mathfrak{A}=(A;\in)\in V
such that in every weak outer model of
V
,

\displaystyle x\text{ is a }(\mathbb{P},\mathfrak{A})\text{-generic object}\iff x\text{ is a }(\mathbb{P},V)\text{-generic object}

for all
x\subset X
. In fact, we can choose
A
to be
H(\kappa)
for any
\kappa>|trcl(\{P,X\})|
.

Observation
2.26
allows us to refer to
(\mathbb{P},V)
-generic objects for any forcing notion
\mathbb{P}
, without needing to quantify over all formulas.

There are looser definitions of a generic filter or a generic object in the literature. For example, we can require the filter to only meet subsets with definitions belonging to a certain complexity class, as is commonly seen in computability theory. Informally then, the study of genericity boils down to observing the effects of a filter meeting a bunch of subsets.

Section
1
hinted at a key difference between forcing theory and the study of partial orders in order theory, and that is the nature of the properties studied apropos of their common subjects. In order theory, only first-order properties of partial orders are considered, whereas forcing theory concerns itself with their higher-order properties. Now, another such differentiating factor is the overwhelming focus on generic objects in forcing theory. In fact, so much attention is paid to generic objects in forcing theory that one might as well call it
genericity theory
.

Definition 2.27
.

If
\mathbb{B}
is a boolean algebra with base set
B
and least element
\mathbb{0}
, then
\mathbb{B}^{+}
denotes the partial order reduct of
\mathbb{B}
restricted to
B\setminus\{\mathbb{0}\}
.

Definition 2.28
.

Let
\mathbb{P}=(P,\leq_{\mathbb{P}})
and
\mathbb{Q}=(Q,\leq_{\mathbb{Q}})
be preorders. We say
\pi
is an
embedding from

\mathbb{P}

into

\mathbb{Q}
iff
\pi
is an injective function from
P
into
Q
satisfying

•

p_{1}\leq_{\mathbb{P}}p_{2}\iff\pi(p_{1})\leq_{\mathbb{Q}}\pi(p_{2})
, and

•

p_{1}\ \bot_{\mathbb{P}}\ p_{2}\implies\pi(p_{1})\ \bot_{\mathbb{Q}}\ \pi(p_{2})
.

Definition 2.29
.

Let
\mathbb{P}=(P,\leq_{\mathbb{P}})
and
\mathbb{Q}=(Q,\leq_{\mathbb{Q}})
be preorders. An embedding
\pi
from
\mathbb{P}
into
\mathbb{Q}
is
complete
iff for every maximal antichain
A
of
\mathbb{P}
,

\{\pi(p):p\in A\}

is a maximal antichain of
\mathbb{Q}
.

Definition 2.30
.

Let
\mathbb{P}=(P,\leq_{\mathbb{P}})
and
\mathbb{Q}=(Q,\leq_{\mathbb{Q}})
be preorders. An embedding
\pi
from
\mathbb{P}
into
\mathbb{Q}
is
dense
iff
ran(\pi)
is dense in
\mathbb{Q}
.

Fact 2.31
.

Let
\mathbb{P}
and
\mathbb{Q}
be preorders. Then every dense embedding from
\mathbb{P}
into
\mathbb{Q}
is a complete embedding from
\mathbb{P}
into
\mathbb{Q}
.

Definition 2.32
.

Let
\mathbb{P}=(P,\leq_{\mathbb{P}})
be a preorder. Define

\displaystyle w(\leq_{\mathbb{P}}):=\

\displaystyle\{(p,q)\in P\times P:\{q^{\prime}:q^{\prime}\leq_{\mathbb{P}}q\}\text{ is dense below }p\}\text{, and}

\displaystyle w(\mathbb{P}):=\

\displaystyle(P,w(\leq_{\mathbb{P}}))\text{.}

Fact 2.33
.

For any preorder
\mathbb{P}
,
w(w(\mathbb{P}))=w(\mathbb{P})
.

Fact 2.34
.

If
\mathbb{P}=(P,\leq_{\mathbb{P}})
is a preorder, then
w(\mathbb{P})
is also a preorder.

Definition 2.35
.

A preorder
\mathbb{P}
is
separative
iff
w(\mathbb{P})=\mathbb{P}
.

Fact 2.36
.

If
\mathbb{P}
is a separative forcing notion, then there is a unique (up to isomorphism) complete boolean algebra
B(\mathbb{P})
such that a dense embedding exists from
\mathbb{P}
into
B(\mathbb{P})^{+}
.

Fix a preorder
\mathbb{P}=(P,\leq_{\mathbb{P}})
. Note that by Fact
2.34
,
w(\leq_{\mathbb{P}})
induces an equivalence relation on
P
. To wit, for any
p,q\in P
, let

p\sim_{\mathbb{P}}q\text{ iff }(p,q)\in w(\leq_{\mathbb{P}})\text{ and }(q,p)\in w(\leq_{\mathbb{P}})\text{.}

Then
\sim_{\mathbb{P}}
is an equivalence relation on
P
.

Definition 2.37
.

Given a preorder
\mathbb{P}
, call

s(\mathbb{P}):=w(\mathbb{P})/\sim_{\mathbb{P}}

the
separative quotient
of
\mathbb{P}
.

Remark 2.38
.

By Fact
2.33
,
s(\mathbb{P})
is a separative forcing notion given any preorder
\mathbb{P}
.

Definition 2.39
.

Given preorders
\mathbb{P}
and
\mathbb{Q}
, we say
\mathbb{P}\lessdot\mathbb{Q}
iff there is a complete embedding from
s(\mathbb{P})
into
B(s(\mathbb{Q}))^{+}
.

Fact 2.40
.

The relation
\lessdot
is a preordering of the class of all preorders. Hence it also pre-orders the class of all forcing notions.

Definition 2.41
.

Preorders
\mathbb{P}
and
\mathbb{Q}
are
forcing equivalent
iff
\mathbb{P}\lessdot\mathbb{Q}
and
\mathbb{Q}\lessdot\mathbb{P}
.

Remark 2.42
.

By Facts
2.31
,
2.33
,
2.36
and Definition
2.41
, for any preorder
\mathbb{P}
,
\mathbb{P}
and
w(\mathbb{P})
are forcing equivalent.

Fact 2.43
.

Let
\mathbb{P}
and
\mathbb{Q}
be preorders. If there is a dense embedding from
\mathbb{P}
into
\mathbb{Q}
, then
\mathbb{P}
and
\mathbb{Q}
are forcing equivalent.

Definition 2.44
.

Let
\mathbb{P}=(P,\leq_{\mathbb{P}})
and
\mathbb{Q}=(Q,\leq_{\mathbb{Q}})
be preorders. We say
\pi
is a
weak embedding from

\mathbb{P}

into

\mathbb{Q}
iff
\pi
is an embedding from
w(\mathbb{P})
into
w(\mathbb{Q})
.

Definition 2.45
.

Let
\mathbb{P}=(P,\leq_{\mathbb{P}})
and
\mathbb{Q}=(Q,\leq_{\mathbb{Q}})
be preorders. A weak embedding
\pi
from
\mathbb{P}
into
\mathbb{Q}
is
dense
iff
\pi
is dense as an embedding from
w(\mathbb{P})
into
w(\mathbb{Q})
.

Remark 2.46
.

Let
\mathbb{P}
and
\mathbb{Q}
be preorders. By Remark
2.42
and Fact
2.43
, if there is a dense weak embedding from
\mathbb{P}
into
\mathbb{Q}
, then
\mathbb{P}
and
\mathbb{Q}
are forcing equivalent.

Definition 2.47
.

If
\mathbb{P}=(P,\leq_{\mathbb{P}})
is a forcing notion and
p\in P
, we let
g_{p}(\mathbb{P})
denote the set

\{q\in P:p\not\!\!\bot_{\mathbb{P}}\ q\}.

Definition 2.48
.

Let
\mathbb{P}=(P,\leq_{\mathbb{P}})
be a forcing notion. A member
p
of
P
is an
atom
of
\mathbb{P}
iff

\forall q_{1}\ \forall q_{2}\ ((q_{1}\leq_{\mathbb{P}}p\text{ and }q_{2}\leq_{\mathbb{P}}p)\implies q_{1}\not\!\!\bot_{\mathbb{P}}\ q_{2}).

Lemma 2.49
.

If
\mathbb{P}=(P,\leq_{\mathbb{P}})
is a forcing notion and
p
is an atom of
\mathbb{P}
, then
g_{p}(\mathbb{P})
is a
\mathbb{P}
-generic filter over
V
.

Proof.

If
D
is dense in
\mathbb{P}
, then there is
q\in D
with
q\leq_{\mathbb{P}}p
. Obviously,
q\in g_{p}(\mathbb{P})
. Therefore
g_{p}(\mathbb{P})
is a
\mathbb{P}
-generic subset over
V
. To see that
g_{p}(\mathbb{P})
is a filter, let
q_{1}
and
q_{2}
be members of
g_{p}(\mathbb{P})
. By the definition of
g_{p}(\mathbb{P})
, there are
r_{1}
and
r_{2}
such that

•

r_{1}\leq_{\mathbb{P}}q_{1}
,

•

r_{1}\leq_{\mathbb{P}}p
,

•

r_{2}\leq_{\mathbb{P}}q_{2}
,

•

r_{2}\leq_{\mathbb{P}}p
.

As
p
is an atom of
\mathbb{P}
, it must be the case that
r_{1}\not\!\!\bot_{\mathbb{P}}\ r_{2}
, which means
q_{1}\not\!\!\bot_{\mathbb{P}}\ q_{2}
.
∎

Definition 2.50
.

A forcing notion
\mathbb{P}
is
atomic
iff the set of atoms of
\mathbb{P}
is dense in
\mathbb{P}
.

Definition 2.51
.

A forcing notion
\mathbb{P}=(P,\leq_{\mathbb{P}})
is
atomless
iff no member of
P
is an atom of
\mathbb{P}
.

Definition 2.52
.

Define

\displaystyle C:=\

\displaystyle 2^{<\omega}\text{, and}

\displaystyle\leq_{\mathbb{C}}\ :=\

\displaystyle\{(p,q):q\subset p\}\text{.}

Call the forcing notion
\mathbb{C}:=(C,\leq_{\mathbb{C}})

Cohen forcing
.

Definition 2.53
.

Let
\mathbb{P}=(P,\leq_{\mathbb{P}})
and
\mathbb{Q}=(Q,\leq_{\mathbb{Q}})
be forcing notions. We say
\mathbb{P}
is a
regular suborder
of
\mathbb{Q}
, denoted
\mathbb{P}\lessdot\mathbb{Q}
, iff

•

\mathbb{P}
is a suborder of
\mathbb{Q}
, and

•

for all
q\in Q
there is
p\in P
such that every
p^{\prime}\leq_{\mathbb{P}}p
is compatible with
q
in
\mathbb{Q}
.

Fact 2.54
.

If
\mathbb{P}=(P,\leq_{\mathbb{P}})\lessdot\mathbb{Q}
, then for every
\mathbb{Q}
-generic filter
g
over
V
,
g\cap P
is a
\mathbb{P}
-generic filter over
V
.

2.5
Universally Baire Sets and Productive Classes

Definition 2.55

(Feng-Magidor-Woodin)
.

Let

•

1\leq k<\omega
,

•

D\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{k})
, and

•

T
and
U
be trees on
{{}^{k}{\omega}}\times\lambda
for some cardinal
\lambda
.

We say
T
and
U

witness

D

is universally Baire
iff

•

D=p[T]
, and

•

\Vdash^{V}_{\mathbb{P}}``p[U]=\mathbb{R}^{k}\setminus p[T]"
for all set forcing notions
\mathbb{P}
.

We say
D
is
universally Baire
iff there are trees
T
and
U
witnessing
D
is universally Baire.

The definition of universally Baire sets of reals first appeared in
[
7
, Section 2]
.

If
T
and
U
witness
D
is universally Baire, then we can read off
T
an unambiguous version of
D
, which we denote
D^{*}
, in any generic extension of
V
. Essentially, we let
(D^{*})^{V[g]}=(p[T])^{V[g]}
for any poset
\mathbb{P}\in V
and any
\mathbb{P}
-generic filter
g
over
V
.

Note also that if

•

T
and
U
witness
D
is universally Baire, and

•

T^{\prime}
and
U^{\prime}
witness
D
is universally Baire,

then for all set forcing notions
\mathbb{P}
,

\Vdash^{V}_{\mathbb{P}}``p[T]=p[T^{\prime}]",

so the evaluation of
D^{*}
is independent of the witnesses for
D
being universally Baire.

Definition 2.56
.

Let
\Gamma^{\infty}
denote the set of all universally Baire sets of reals, i.e.

\Gamma^{\infty}:=\{D\in\bigcup_{1\leq k<\omega}\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{k}):D\text{ is universally Baire}\}.

Definition 2.57
.

Let
\Gamma\subset\bigcup_{1\leq k<\omega}\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{k})
. We say
\Gamma
is
productive
iff

(1)

\Gamma\subset\Gamma^{\infty}
,

(2)

\Gamma
is closed under complements, i.e. for all
k<\omega
, if
D\in\Gamma\cap\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{k+1})
, then
\mathbb{R}^{k+1}\setminus D\in\Gamma
,

(3)

\Gamma
is closed under projections, i.e. for all
k<\omega
, if
D\in\Gamma\cap\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{k+2})
, then

\exists^{\mathbb{R}}D:=\{\vec{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k+1}:\exists y\ (\vec{x}^{\frown}(y)\in D)\}\in\Gamma,

and

(4)

the closure of
\Gamma
under projections is preserved by set forcing notions in a strong way: for all
k<\omega
, if
D\in\Gamma\cap\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{k+2})
, then

(\exists^{\mathbb{R}}D)^{*}=\{\vec{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k+1}:\exists y\ (\vec{x}^{\frown}(y)\in D^{*})\}

in all generic extensions of
V
.

Lemma 2.58
.

Let
\Gamma=\bigcup_{1\leq k<\omega}P(\mathbb{R}^{k})\cap L(\Gamma,\mathbb{R})
be productive,
D\in\Gamma
, and
\phi
be a projective formula. If
\vec{s}\in{{}^{<\omega}{\mathbb{R}}}
and
arity(\phi)=dom(\vec{s})+1
, then

V\models\phi(\vec{s},D)\iff\ \Vdash^{V}_{\mathbb{P}}\phi(\vec{s},D^{*})

for all set forcing notions
\mathbb{P}
.

Proof.

By induction on the length of
\phi
.
∎

2.6
Generic Iterations

We want to first define a fragment of
\mathsf{ZFC}
rich enough to

•

allow for basic analyses of generic ultrapowers, and

•

be preserved by the generic ultrapowers we will be using.

To this end, we follow
[
13
, Section 3.1]
.

Definition 2.59

(Woodin)
.

Let
\mathsf{ZFC}^{*}
be the conjunction of

\mathsf{ZFC}-\mathsf{Replacement}-\mathsf{Powerset}

and the following schema:

“Given any nonempty class of functions
\mathcal{R}
with

•

dom(f)<\omega_{1}
and

•

f\restriction\alpha\in\mathcal{R}

for all
f\in\mathcal{R}
and all
\alpha\in dom(f)
, there is
\beta\leq\omega_{1}
and a function
g
with domain
\beta
such that

•

g\not\in\mathcal{R}
,

•

for all
\gamma<\beta
,
g\restriction\gamma\in\mathcal{R}
, and

•

if
\beta=\gamma+1
, then
g\restriction\gamma
is
\subset
-maximal in
\mathcal{R}
.”

Informally, the schema found in the block quote in Definition
2.59
says that every tree of height at most
\omega_{1}
has a path.

Definition 2.60
.

For
\alpha\leq\omega_{1}
, we say a class

\mathfrak{C}=\langle\bar{N}_{i}=(N_{i};\tilde{\in}_{i},I_{i}),\sigma_{ij}:i\leq j<\alpha\rangle

is a
generic iteration
iff

•

\tilde{\in}_{0}
is a binary relation on
N_{0}
interpreting
\ulcorner\in\urcorner
in the language of set theory,

•

I_{0}
is a unary relation on
N_{0}
,

•

(N_{0};\tilde{\in}_{0})\models\mathsf{ZFC}^{*}
,

•

\bar{N}_{0}\models``I_{0}
is a normal uniform ideal on
\omega_{1}"
,

•

for all
i<\alpha
,
\sigma_{ii}=id_{N_{i}}
,

•

for all
i<\alpha
such that
i+1<\alpha
, there is
g_{i}
a
\mathcal{P}(\omega_{1})^{\bar{N}_{i}}/I
-generic filter over
N_{i}
such that

\circ

N_{i+1}=Ult(N_{i},g_{i})
, and

\circ

\sigma_{i(i+1)}:N_{i}\longrightarrow N_{i+1}
is the corresponding ultrapower embedding,

•

for all limit ordinals
k<\alpha
,
(\bar{N}_{k},\langle\sigma_{ik}:i<k\rangle)
is the direct limit of
\langle\bar{N}_{i},\sigma_{ij}:i\leq j<k\rangle
, and

•

for all
i\leq j\leq k<\alpha
,
\sigma_{ik}=\sigma_{jk}\circ\sigma_{ij}
.

In this case, we call
\alpha
the
length
of
\mathfrak{C}
.

Definition 2.61
.

For
\alpha\leq\omega_{1}
and any class
\bar{N}
, a class

\langle\bar{N}_{i},\sigma_{ij}:i\leq j<\alpha\rangle

is a
generic iteration of

\bar{N}
iff

•

\langle\bar{N}_{i},\sigma_{ij}:i\leq j<\alpha\rangle
is a generic iteration, and

•

\bar{N}=\bar{N}_{0}
.

Definition 2.62
.

A generic iteration

\langle\bar{N}_{i}=(N_{i};\tilde{\in}_{i},I_{i}),\sigma_{ij}:i\leq j<\alpha\rangle

is
well-founded
iff for all
i<\alpha
,
\tilde{\in}_{i}
is a well-founded relation on
N_{i}
.

Following convention, if

\langle\bar{N}_{i},\sigma_{ij}:i\leq j<\alpha\rangle

is a well-founded generic iteration, we shall identify

•

each
\bar{N}_{i}
with its transitive collapse, and

•

each
\sigma_{ij}
with the unique embedding that commutes with
\sigma_{ij}
and the transitive collapse isomorphisms of
\bar{N}_{i}
and
\bar{N}_{j}
.

Definition 2.63
.

For any class
\bar{N}
,
\bar{N}
is
generically iterable
iff

•

for some
\sigma_{00}
,
\langle\bar{N}_{0},\sigma_{00}\rangle
is a generic iteration of
\bar{N}
, and

•

every generic iteration of
\bar{N}
is well-founded.

Definition 2.64
.

Given a class
\bar{N}=(N;\in,I)
, we say

\bar{N}\models``I\text{ is a precipitous ideal on }\omega_{1}"

iff

•

for some
\sigma_{00}
,
\langle\bar{N}_{0},\sigma_{00}\rangle
is a generic iteration of
\bar{N}
, and

•

every generic iteration of
\bar{N}
of length
2
is well-founded.

Fact 2.65
.

If
\bar{N}=(N;\in,I)
is such that

•

for some
\sigma_{00}
,
\langle\bar{N_{0}},\sigma_{00}\rangle
is a generic iteration of
\bar{N}
, and

•

\bar{N}\models``I\text{ is a saturated ideal on }\omega_{1}"
,

then

\bar{N}\models``I\text{ is a precipitous ideal on }\omega_{1}".

Lemma 2.66
.

If
\bar{N}_{0}
is generically iterable,
J
is a normal uniform ideal on
\omega_{1}
, and

\langle\bar{N}_{i}=(N_{i};\in,I_{i}),\sigma_{ij}:i\leq j\leq\omega_{1}\rangle

is a generic iteration of
\bar{N}_{0}
, then
I_{\omega_{1}}\subset J
.

Proof.

As
\mathrm{NS}_{\omega_{1}}
is the smallest normal uniform ideal on
\omega_{1}
, it suffices to show
I_{\omega_{1}}\subset\mathrm{NS}_{\omega_{1}}
. Note that

C=\{\omega_{1}^{\bar{N}_{i}}:i<\omega_{1}\}

is a club in
\omega_{1}
. Now let
x\in I_{\omega_{1}}
, so that for some
i<\omega_{1}
, there is
x_{i}\in I_{i}
for which
\sigma_{i\omega_{1}}(x_{i})=x
. Since

\bar{N}_{j+1}\cong Ult(\bar{N}_{j},g_{j}:=\{y\in\mathcal{P}(\omega_{1})^{\bar{N}_{j}}:\omega_{1}^{\bar{N}_{j}}\in\sigma_{j(j+1)}(y)\})

and

g_{j}\cap I_{j}=\emptyset

for all
i\leq j<\omega_{1}
, we have

\omega_{1}^{\bar{N}_{j}}\not\in\sigma_{i(j+1)}(x_{i})=x\restriction(\omega_{1}^{\bar{N}_{j+1}})

for all
i\leq j<\omega_{1}
, whence

x\cap(C\setminus\omega_{1}^{\bar{N}_{i}})=\emptyset.

This means
x\in\mathrm{NS}_{\omega_{1}}
, and we are done.
∎

Lemma 2.67
.

Suppose

•

for some
\sigma_{00}
,
\langle\bar{N}_{0},\sigma_{00}\rangle
is a generic iteration of
\bar{N}=(N;\in,I)
,

•

for some
\pi_{00}
,
\langle\bar{M}_{0},\pi_{00}\rangle
is a generic iteration of
\bar{M}=(M;\in,I)
,

•

\bar{N}\in M
,

•

\bar{N}\models``\text{every }{\omega_{1}^{\bar{M}}}\text{-sequence is a set}"
,

•

N
contains all maximal antichains of
(\mathcal{P}(\omega_{1})/I)^{M}
.

Then for each generic iteration

\langle\bar{N}_{i}=(N_{i};\in,I_{i}),\sigma_{ij}:i\leq j\leq\alpha\rangle

of
\bar{N}
, there is a unique generic iteration

\langle\bar{M}_{i}=(M_{i};\in,I_{i}),\pi_{ij}:i\leq j\leq\alpha\rangle

of
\bar{M}
such that for all
i\leq j\leq\alpha
,

•

\pi_{0i}(N)=N_{i}
,

•

\bar{N}_{i}\models``\text{every }{\omega_{1}^{\bar{M}_{i}}}\text{-sequence is a set}"
,

•

N_{i}
contains all maximal antichains of
(\mathcal{P}(\omega_{1})/I_{i})^{M_{i}}
, and

•

\pi_{ij}\restriction N_{i}=\sigma_{ij}
.

Proof.

By induction on
\gamma
.
∎

Lemma
2.67
is a modified version of
[
12
, Lemma 1.5]
: instead of requiring
N
to contain
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{P}(\omega_{1})/I)^{M}
, we only require it to contain all the maximal antichains of
(\mathcal{P}(\omega_{1})/I)^{M}
. These two lemmas share the same proof.

Lemma 2.68
.

Suppose

•

M
is a transitive model of
\mathsf{ZFC}
,

•

\bar{M}:=(M;\in,J)\models``J
is a precipitous ideal on
\omega_{1}"
, and

•

for some
\alpha\in\omega_{1}^{V}\cap M
,

\mathfrak{C}=\langle\bar{M}_{i},\pi_{ij}:i\leq j\leq\alpha\rangle

is a generic iteration of
\bar{M}
.

Then
\mathfrak{C}
is well-founded.

Proof.

This follows from
[
12
, Lemma 1.6]
.
∎

Lemma 2.69
.

Let
V\subset W
be transitive models of
\mathsf{ZFC}
, such that

•

V
is definable in
W
,

•

V\models``\mathrm{NS}_{\omega_{1}}
is saturated”, and

•

\omega_{1}^{W}\in V
.

If

•

\lambda\leq\kappa\leq\omega_{1}^{W}
, and

•

in
W
,

\langle\bar{M}_{i}=(M_{i};\in,J_{i}),\pi_{ij}:\lambda\leq i\leq j\leq\kappa\rangle

is a generic iteration of
(H(\omega_{2})^{V};\in,\mathrm{NS}_{\omega_{1}}^{V})

then
\pi_{\lambda\kappa}
lifts to a generic ultrapower map
\pi^{+}_{\lambda\kappa}:V\longrightarrow M^{+}
, for some inner model
M^{+}
of
W
.

Proof.

With Fact
2.65
in mind, the lemma follows immediately from applications of Lemmas
2.67
and
2.68
in
W
, with

(V;\in,\mathrm{NS}_{\omega_{1}}^{V})

in place of
\bar{M}
.
∎

2.7
\mathbb{P}_{max}
Forcing

We start by overloading what it means to be a generic iteration.

Definition 2.70
.

A class

\mathfrak{C}=\langle\bar{N}_{i}=(N_{i};\tilde{\in}_{i},I_{i},a_{i}),\sigma_{ij}:i\leq j<\alpha\rangle

is a
generic iteration
iff

•

a_{i}\in N_{i}
for all
i<\alpha
,

•

\langle(N_{i};\tilde{\in}_{i},I_{i}),\sigma_{ij}:i\leq j<\alpha\rangle
is generic iteration in the sense of Definition
2.60
, and

•

\sigma_{ij}(a_{i})=a_{j}
.

Going forward, unless specified otherwise or under clear context, the term “generic iteration” will be used in the sense of Definition
2.70
.

Definition 2.71
.

The conditions of
\mathbb{P}_{max}
are exactly the structures
\bar{N}=(N;\in,I,a)
such that

•

N
is countable and transitive,

•

\bar{N}\models\mathsf{ZFC}^{*}+\mathsf{MA}(\omega_{1})
,

•

I\subset N
,

•

x\cap I\in N
for all
x\in N
,

•

\bar{N}\models``I
is a normal uniform ideal on
\omega_{1}"
,

•

a\in N

•

\bar{N}\models``a\subset\omega_{1}
and
\omega_{1}=\omega_{1}^{L[a,x]}
for some real
x"
, and

•

(N;\in,I)
is generically iterable.

Let
\leq_{\mathbb{P}_{max}}
be a binary relation on the conditions of
\mathbb{P}_{max}
, such that

\bar{M}=(M;\in,J,b)\leq_{\mathbb{P}_{max}}\bar{N}=(N;\in,I,a)

iff one of the following conditions hold:

•

\bar{M}=\bar{N}
, or

•

\bar{N}\in M
and

\displaystyle\bar{M}\models``

there is a generic iteration

\displaystyle\langle\bar{N}_{i}=(N_{i};\in,I_{i},a_{i}),\sigma_{ij}:i\leq j\leq\omega_{1}\rangle

\displaystyle\text{of }\bar{N}\text{ such that}

\displaystyle a_{\omega_{1}}=b\text{ and }J\cap N_{\omega_{1}}=I_{\omega_{1}}".

We can easily check that
\leq_{\mathbb{P}_{max}}
is a partial ordering.

Although the theory of
\mathbb{P}_{max}
forcing is replete with remarkable combinatorial arguments, one need not understand these arguments to appreciate our
\mathbb{P}_{max}
-related work in Subsection
4.3
. As such, we present only the following curious lemma.

Lemma 2.72
.

Assume
\mathrm{NS}_{\omega_{1}}
is saturated,
MA(\omega_{1})
holds,
2^{\omega_{1}}=\omega_{2}
, and
A\subset\omega_{1}
is such that
\omega_{1}^{L[A]}=\omega_{1}
. Then

\Vdash_{Col(\omega,\omega_{2})}(H(\omega_{2})^{\dot{V}};\in,\mathrm{NS}_{\omega_{1}}^{\dot{V}},A)\in\mathbb{P}_{max}.

Proof.

First, note that
|H(\omega_{2})|=\aleph_{2}
, so

\Vdash_{Col(\omega,\omega_{2})}``H(\omega_{2})^{\dot{V}}\text{ is countable}".

Next, we can invoke Lemma
2.69
with
V^{Col(\omega,\omega_{2})}
in place of
W
, to give us the generic iterability of

(H(\omega_{2})^{V};\in,\mathrm{NS}_{\omega_{1}}^{V})

in
V^{Col(\omega,\omega_{2})}
. It is easy to check that

(H(\omega_{2})^{V};\in,\mathrm{NS}_{\omega_{1}}^{V},A)

satisfies the other prerequisites (as per Definition
2.71
) of being a
\mathbb{P}_{max}
condition in
V^{Col(\omega,\omega_{2})}
.
∎

Lemma
2.72
is a first step towards deriving the
Col(\omega,\omega_{2})
-name
\dot{p}
in Fact
4.40
. It is also why the structure

(H(\omega_{2});\in,\mathrm{NS}_{\omega_{1}},A)

is instrumental in the proof of Theorem
4.41
.

3
Forcing with Language Fragments

In model theory, a Henkin construction involves building a model of a theory over a language, from terms of that language. When such a construction is unequivocally guided by a given theory, we can safely identify the resulting term model with said theory. As such, we have the following viable means of proving the existence of a object with property
P
:

1.

Translate
P
into a specification
S
for a theory, such that the unique term model of any theory satisfying
S
has property
P
.

2.

Prove that a theory satisfying
S
exists.

In a similar fashion, we can force the existence of an object with property
P
by forcing the existence of a theory satisfying
S
. Naturally, this leads to forcing notions with conditions being fragments of the language over which a theory satisfying
S
is defined.

The idea of forcing models of a theory has been studied by model theorists — notably, Robinson and Barwise — since the 1970s (see e.g.
[
5
]
), under the label
model-theoretic forcing
. More recently, set theorists have leveraged on model-theoretic forcing to generate conditions of the forcing notions used in various relative consistency proofs. Some examples include
[
11
]
and
[
15
]
. However, these forcing conditions involve are highly complicated and specialised structures, and it is not immediately clear how much of the analysis of one forcing notion can be recycled in the analysis of another.

Asperó and Schindler are perhaps the first to present a construction with language fragments as forcing conditions, in their seminal work
[
16
]
. It quickly became clear that this construction generalises well to extend model-theoretic forcing, allowing us to obtain models outside
V
which are generic over
V
. Streamlining and modularising the analysis and construction of forcing notions similar to the Asperó-Schindler ones thus seems like a useful proposition.

This section details a framework for constructing forcing notions with fragments of a language
\mathcal{L}
as conditions, based on specifications of a theory over
\mathcal{L}
. To state these specifications, a “meta-language” dependent on
\mathcal{L}
is required. Our goal is to ensure that the generic filters of each forcing notion produced indeed give rise to theories satisfying the given specifications. We will make precise the relevant technical terms and concepts as we build our framework over the subsequent three subsections.

The main result in this section is Lemma
3.42
, which is stated and proven in greater generality than is needed for our framework. From Lemma
3.42
we derive Lemma
3.56
, the primary workhorse of the entire paper.

3.1
General Languages and Meta-languages

The initial step in the development of our framework involves the ability to potentially interpret any set as a language.

Definition 3.1
.

The canonical negation function
\neg
on
V
is defined as follows.

\displaystyle\neg x:=\neg(x)=\begin{cases}y&\text{if }x=\ulcorner\neg y\urcorner\text{ for some }y\\
\ulcorner\neg x\urcorner&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Basically,
\neg
takes a member of
\mathcal{L}
as input, and check whether it is a string with first (leftmost) character
\ulcorner\neg\urcorner
. If so, it removes the leading
\ulcorner\neg\urcorner
; otherwise, it “casts” the input as a string (mapping the input to a string of length 1 containing the input as the only character, if the input is not already a string) and prepend
\ulcorner\neg\urcorner
to the result. For ease of argument, we identify the string containing a single character
x
with
x
itself.

Remark 3.2
.

Note that
\neg
is
\Delta_{0}
-definable with a single parameter
\ulcorner\neg\urcorner
, which we assume is in every
\mathfrak{A}
satisfying

•

\mathfrak{A}
is a structure in a possibly expanded language of set theory, and

•

\mathfrak{A}
is a “sufficiently transitive” (see
(c)
of Definition
3.4
for a formal treatment of “sufficiently transitive”) model of a sufficiently strong set theory.

So the definition of
\neg
is absolute for all such structures
\mathfrak{A}
.

We sometimes abuse notation and use
\neg
the function and
\ulcorner\neg\urcorner
the first-order logical symbol interchangeably. However, we take special care to distinguish them wherever is crucial in our definitions and arguments.

Definition 3.3
.

A set
\mathcal{L}
is
closed under negation
iff for each
\phi\in\mathcal{L}
,
\neg\phi\in\mathcal{L}
.

Before we proceed, fix a set
\mathcal{L}
that is closed under negation and does not contain any variable symbol. We will stick to this
\mathcal{L}
for the rest of this section.

Definition 3.4
.

A structure
\mathfrak{A}=(A;\in,\vec{R})
in a possibly expanded language of set theory is
\mathcal{L}
-suitable
iff

(a)

\vec{R}
is a set of relations on
A
,

(b)

\mathfrak{A}
is a model of a sufficiently strong set theory,

(c)

A
is
\mathfrak{A}
-finitely transitive
: that is,

\mathfrak{A}\models\text{``}x\text{ is finite''}\implies x\subset A

whenever
x\in A
,

(d)

\mathcal{L}\subset A
, and

(e)

\mathcal{L}
is
\Pi_{1}
-definable in the language associated with
\mathfrak{A}
.

We can think of
\mathfrak{A}
as a first-order structure expanding on
(A;\in)
, for constants and functions interpreted over the base set
A
can be represented by relations on
A
. In typical scenarios,
\mathfrak{A}
is

•

either
an expansion of a transitive model of
\mathsf{ZFC-Powerset-Infinity}
,

•

or
an elementary substructure of some expansion of a transitive model of

\mathsf{ZFC-Powerset-Infinity}
.

Any such
\mathfrak{A}
immediately satisfies
(b)
and
(c)
of Definition
3.4
.

The requirement for
\mathcal{L}
to be
\Pi_{1}
-definable in the language associated with
\mathfrak{A}
is only there so that the proof of Lemma
3.42
can go through given its hypothesis.

Remark 3.5
.

Let
\mathfrak{A}=(A;\in,\vec{R})
be a
\mathcal{L}
-suitable structure in a possibly expanded language of set theory. Then the following can be deduced from
(b)
and
(c)
of Definition
3.4
.

(1)

H(\omega)\subset A
.

(2)

For each
x\in A
, it must be the case that

\mathfrak{A}\models\text{``}x\text{ is non-empty''}\iff x\text{ is non-empty.}

(3)

For each
x\in A
, it must be the case that

\mathfrak{A}\models\text{``}x\text{ is Dedekind-finite''}\iff x\text{ is finite.}

(4)

A
is
finitely transitive
. That is, for each
x\in A
, it must be the case that

x\text{ is finite}\implies x\subset A\text{.}

Fix a
\mathcal{L}
-suitable
\mathfrak{A}=(A;\in,\vec{R})
for the rest of this section.

Definition 3.6
.

Define
\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}}
to be the language associated with
(A;\{\in,\vec{R},E\})
, where
E
is a unary predicate symbol not occurring in
\vec{R}
.

We want to use
\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}}
to reason about subsets of
\mathcal{L}
. Intuitively, a richer
\mathfrak{A}
should allow us to formulate more statements about these subsets. Certain subsets of
\mathcal{L}
are particularly interesting.

Definition 3.7
.

A set
\Sigma
is
\mathcal{L}
-
nice
iff

•

\Sigma\subset\mathcal{L}
,

•

for all
\phi\in\mathcal{L}
,

\circ

\{\phi,\neg\phi\}\not\subset\Sigma
, and

\circ

either
\phi\in\Sigma
or
\neg\phi\in\Sigma
.

Definition 3.8
.

Let
\phi\in\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}}
. Define
\mathsf{pos}(\phi)
to be the formula in
\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}}
resulting from the following operation:

•

for each subformula
\varphi
of
\phi
, if
\varphi=\ulcorner\neg E(x)\urcorner
for some
x\in\mathcal{L}
, then replace
\varphi
with
\ulcorner E(\neg x)\urcorner
.

Definition 3.9
.

Let

•

\phi\in\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}}
, and

•

\nu
be any subset of a
\mathfrak{A}
-valuation.

Then
\nu^{*}(\phi)
is defined to be the sentence in
\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}}
resulting from the following operation:

•

for each
c\in dom(\nu)
, replace every free occurrence of
c
in
\phi
with
\nu(c)
.

Definition 3.10
.

Let

•

\phi\in\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}}
, and

•

\nu
be a
\mathfrak{A}
-valuation.

We say
X\models^{*}_{\mathfrak{A},\nu}\phi
iff

(A;\in,\vec{R},X\cap A)\models\nu^{*}(\phi)(\vec{x};\in,\vec{R},E).

We say
X\models^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}}\phi
iff for every
\mathfrak{A}
-valuation
\nu
,
X\models^{*}_{\mathfrak{A},\nu}\phi
.

If
\Gamma\subset\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}}
then we say

\displaystyle X\models^{*}_{\mathfrak{A},\nu}\Gamma\text{ iff }

\displaystyle X\models^{*}_{\mathfrak{A},\nu}\phi\text{ for all }\phi\in\Gamma,\text{ and}

\displaystyle X\models^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}}\Gamma\text{ iff }

\displaystyle X\models^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}}\phi\text{ for all }\phi\in\Gamma.

Remark 3.11
.

Let

•

\phi\in\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}}
be a sentence, and

•

p\in A\cap\mathcal{P}(A)
.

Then by Definition
3.10
,
p\models^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}}\phi
iff

(A;\in,\vec{R},p)\models\phi(\vec{x};\in,\vec{R},E).

(3.1)

Derive
\phi^{\prime}
from
\phi
by replacing every subformula of
\phi
of the form
\ulcorner E(x)\urcorner
with
\ulcorner x\in p\urcorner
, so that the symbol
\ulcorner E\urcorner
does not occur in
\phi^{\prime}
. It is easy to see that

•

the quantification structure of
\phi^{\prime}
is identical to that of
\phi
, and

•

(
3.1
) is semantically equivalent to

\mathfrak{A}=(A;\in,\vec{R})\models\phi^{\prime}(\vec{x};\in,\vec{R}).

In particular, if
C
is such that
C=A
or
C
occurs in
\vec{R}
, then

\{p\in C:p\models^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}}\phi\}

is a subset of
A
that is definable in the language associated with
\mathfrak{A}
. This definition is absolute for transitive models of
\mathsf{ZFC-Powerset}
.

Note that for any
\mathcal{L}
-nice
\Sigma
and any
x\in\mathcal{L}
,

x\not\in\Sigma\iff\neg x\in\Sigma,

so applying
\mathsf{pos}
to a
\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}}
formula does not alter its meaning with respect to
\mathcal{L}
-nice sets. More formally, we have the next lemma.

Lemma 3.12
.

Let

•

\phi\in\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}}
, and

•

\nu
be a
\mathfrak{A}
-valuation.

Then for every
\mathcal{L}
-nice
\Sigma
in every weak outer model of
V
,

\Sigma\models^{*}_{\mathfrak{A},\nu}\phi\iff\Sigma\models^{*}_{\mathfrak{A},\nu}\mathsf{pos}(\phi).

Proof.

By induction on the length of
\phi
, while taking note of the following.

•

\Sigma
being
\mathcal{L}
-nice means that for all
x\in\mathcal{L}
,

\Sigma\models^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}}\ulcorner\neg E(x)\urcorner\iff\Sigma\models^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}}\ulcorner E(\neg x)\urcorner.

•

If
\phi=\ulcorner\neg\varphi\urcorner
and
\phi\neq\ulcorner\neg E(x)\urcorner
for any
x
, then
\mathsf{pos}(\phi)=\ulcorner\neg\mathsf{pos}(\varphi)\urcorner
.

•

If
\phi=\ulcorner\varphi_{1}\wedge\varphi_{2}\urcorner
, then
\mathsf{pos}(\phi)=\ulcorner\mathsf{pos}(\varphi_{1})\wedge\mathsf{pos}(\varphi_{2})\urcorner
.

•

If
\phi=\ulcorner\exists y\ \varphi\urcorner
, then
\mathsf{pos}(\phi)=\ulcorner\exists y\ \mathsf{pos}(\varphi)\urcorner
.

The rest of the details are standard.
∎

Definition 3.13
.

If
\phi\in\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}}
, we say
\phi
is
(\mathfrak{A},\mathcal{L})
-satisfiable
iff there are
\nu
,
W
and
X
such that

•

\nu
is a
\mathfrak{A}
-valuation,

•

W
is a weak outer model of
V
,

•

X\in W\cap\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{L)}
, and

•

X\models^{*}_{\mathfrak{A},\nu}\phi
,

in which case the triple
(\nu,W,X)
is said to
witness the

(\mathfrak{A},\mathcal{L})
-satisfiability of

\phi
.

Definition 3.14
.

For any
\phi\in\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}}
, define
\mathsf{set}(\phi)
to be the pair
(p,q)
such that

•

q=\{x\in\mathcal{L}:\ulcorner E(x)\urcorner\text{ is a subformula of }\phi\}
, and

•

p=\{x\in q:\ulcorner(\neg E(x))\urcorner\text{ is not a subformula of }\phi\}
.

Definition 3.15
.

Let

•

\phi\in\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}}
, and

•

\nu
be a
\mathfrak{A}
-valuation.

For any sets
p
and
q
, we say
\phi

is

\models^{*}_{\mathfrak{A},\nu}
-true for

(p,q)
iff

•

p\subset q\subset\mathcal{L}
, and

•

for all

\circ

weak outer models
W
of
V
, and

\circ

X\in W\cap\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{L)}
,

X\cap q=p\implies X\models^{*}_{\mathfrak{A},\nu}\phi.

We say
\phi

is

\models^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}}

-true for

(p,q)
iff for every
\mathfrak{A}
-valuation
\nu
,
\phi
is
\models^{*}_{\mathfrak{A},\nu}
-true for
(p,q)
.

We say
\phi

is

\models^{*}_{\mathfrak{A},\nu}
-true for

p
iff
\phi
is
\models^{*}_{\mathfrak{A},\nu}
-true for
(p,p)
.

For our purposes, being
\models^{*}_{\mathfrak{A},\nu}
-true can be too strong a requirement; it is often enough to narrow the scope of our “test models” to just
\mathcal{L}
-nice sets. This motivates the following definition.

Definition 3.16
.

Let

•

\phi\in\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}}
, and

•

\nu
be a
\mathfrak{A}
-valuation.

For any sets
p
and
q
, we say
\phi

is

\models^{*}_{\mathfrak{A},\nu}
-nice for

(p,q)
iff

•

p\subset q\subset\mathcal{L}
, and

•

for all

\circ

weak outer models
W
of
V
, and

\circ

X\in W\cap\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{L)}
,

X\text{ is }\mathcal{L}\text{-nice and }X\cap q=p\implies X\models^{*}_{\mathfrak{A},\nu}\phi.

We say
\phi

is

\models^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}}

-nice for

(p,q)
iff for every
\mathfrak{A}
-valuation
\nu
,
\phi
is
\models^{*}_{\mathfrak{A},\nu}
-nice for
(p,q)
.

We say
\phi

is

\models^{*}_{\mathfrak{A},\nu}
-nice for

p
iff
\phi
is
\models^{*}_{\mathfrak{A},\nu}
-nice for
(p,p)
.

Definition 3.17
.

Let
\mathcal{L}^{*}_{0,\mathfrak{A}}
consist of all
\Delta_{0}
formulas (in a accordance with Definition
2.7
) in the language associated with
\mathfrak{A}
. Obviously,
\mathcal{L}^{*}_{0,\mathfrak{A}}\subset\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}}
.

Let
\mathcal{L}^{*}_{1,\mathfrak{A}}
be the smallest
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
satisfying the following conditions:

•

\mathcal{L}^{*}_{0,\mathfrak{A}}\subset\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
,

•

\{\ulcorner E(x)\urcorner:x\in\mathrm{Ter}(\mathcal{L}^{*}_{0,\mathfrak{A}})\}\subset\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
,

•

if

\circ

\phi\in\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
,

\circ

\ulcorner z\urcorner
is a variable not bound in
\phi
, and

\circ

\ulcorner p\urcorner
is either a constant or a variable not bound in
\phi
,

then

\ulcorner\forall z\ ((z\in p\wedge\text{``}\emptyset\neq p\text{ and }p\text{ is Dedekind-finite''})\implies\phi)\urcorner\in\mathcal{L}^{\prime},

•

\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
is closed under all zeroth-order logical operations.

A first-order formula
\phi
is
\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}}
-
\Delta_{0}
iff
\phi\in\mathcal{L}^{*}_{1,\mathfrak{A}}
.

We should perhaps highlight the following trivial observations.

(I)

The statement

\text{``}\emptyset\neq p\text{ and }p\text{ is Dedekind-finite''}

can be expressed as a
\Pi_{1}
formula (
a la
Definition
2.7
) in the language associated with
\mathfrak{A}
. In fact, the clause

\text{``}p\text{ is Dedekind-finite''}

can be omitted from said statement without loss of generality if

\mathfrak{A}\models\text{``every set is Dedekind-finite'',}

leaving us with a
\Delta_{0}
formula.

(II)

\mathcal{L}^{*}_{1,\mathfrak{A}}\subset\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}}
.

Remark 3.18
.

As
\mathfrak{A}
is a first-order structure interpreting only relation symbols, the terms occurring in
\mathcal{L}^{*}_{0,\mathfrak{A}}
(i.e.
\mathrm{Ter}(\mathcal{L}^{*}_{0,\mathfrak{A}})
) are either variables or constant symbols representing members of
A
. In the usual fashion, we

•

identify each member of
A
with its corresponding constant symbol, and

•

have
\mathfrak{A}
interpret each constant symbol as its corresponding member of
A
.

Definition 3.19
.

We define the subset
\mathcal{D}
of
\mathcal{L}^{*}_{1,\mathfrak{A}}
to contain formulas of the form

\ulcorner\bigvee_{i<m}(\bigwedge_{j<n_{i}}L_{ij})\urcorner,

wherein for every
i<m
and every
j<n_{i}
, there is
P_{ij}
such that

•

P_{ij}\in\mathcal{L}^{*}_{0,\mathfrak{A}}
or
P_{ij}\in\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}}
is of the form
\ulcorner E(x)\urcorner
, and

•

L_{ij}=P_{ij}
or
L_{ij}=\ulcorner\neg P_{ij}\urcorner
.

Definition 3.20
.

Let
\phi
be a
\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}}
formula. Define
\mathrm{QA}(\phi)
to be the set of all subformulas
\varphi
of
\phi
such that

•

\varphi
starts with a quantifier, and

•

no prefix of
\varphi
is a subformula of
\phi
.

Definition 3.21
.

Given any
\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}}
formula
\phi
, a
\phi
-max peeling
is a maximal member of
\mathrm{QA}(\phi)
with respect to set inclusion.

Remark 3.22
.

Notice that if
\phi
is a
\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}}
formula, then any two distinct
\phi
-max peelings must not overlap in
\phi
.

Definition 3.23
.

Given a
\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}}
-
\Delta_{0}
formula
\phi
, we say
\phi
is
safe
iff every occurrence of
\ulcorner E\urcorner
in
\phi
lies outside the scope of any quantifier. In particular, every member of
\mathcal{D}
is a safe
\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}}
-
\Delta_{0}
formula.

There are algorithms to convert arbitrary propositional formulas into disjunctive normal forms. Fix one such algorithm, call it
\mathsf{P_{1}}
. If
\phi
is a
\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}}
-
\Delta_{0}
formula, then we can apply
\mathsf{P_{1}}
on
\phi
by viewing each
\phi
-max peeling as a(n atomic) proposition. Have
\mathsf{DNF}_{1}
denote the function that takes
\phi
to the result of this application of
\mathsf{P_{1}}
. It is always possible to choose
\mathsf{P_{1}}
in a way that guarantees

(III)

\mathsf{DNF}_{1}(\phi)=\mathsf{DNF}_{1}(\mathsf{DNF}_{1}(\phi))
for all
\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}}
-
\Delta_{0}
formulas
\phi
, and

(IV)

\mathsf{DNF}_{1}
commutes with substitution of literals modulo double-negation elimination.

For convenience of analysis, we shall do so.

Remark 3.24
.

Due to the nature of conversion algorithms such as
\mathsf{P_{1}}
, whenever
\phi
is a
\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}}
-
\Delta_{0}
formula,
\mathsf{DNF}_{1}(\phi)
must be logically equivalent to
\phi
. If in addition,
\phi
is safe, then
\mathsf{DNF}_{1}(\phi)
is a formula in
\mathcal{D}
.

Let
\mathsf{WNF}
be the function with domain

\{\phi:\phi\text{ is a }\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}}\text{-}\Delta_{0}\text{ formula}\}

defined by the following recursive procedure.

\mathsf{Procedure}

\mathsf{P_{W}}

On input
\phi
:

(1)

Set
\phi^{\prime}:=\mathsf{DNF}_{1}(\phi)
.

(2)

If there is no
\phi^{\prime}
-max peeling containing
\ulcorner E\urcorner
, return
\phi^{\prime}
.

(3)

For each
\phi^{\prime}
-max peeling
\varphi
containing
\ulcorner E\urcorner
(the order does not matter because of Remark
3.22
:

(F1)

Necessarily, for some
\ulcorner p\urcorner
and
\varphi^{\prime}
,

\displaystyle\varphi=\ulcorner\forall z\ (

\displaystyle(z\in p\wedge\text{``}\emptyset\neq p\text{ and }p\text{ is Dedekind-finite''})

\displaystyle\implies\varphi^{\prime})\urcorner\text{.}

(F2)

Replace
\varphi^{\prime}
with
\mathsf{P_{W}}(\varphi^{\prime})
in
\phi^{\prime}
.

(4)

Return
\phi^{\prime}
.

Remark 3.25
.

Obviously,
\mathsf{P_{W}}
always terminates and returns a
\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}}
-
\Delta_{0}
formula. Furthermore, routine code tracing with the aid of

•

Remark
3.24
, and the fact that

•

\mathsf{P_{W}}
returns the result of a function call of
\mathsf{DNF}_{1}
whenever its base case is fulfilled,

allows us to ascertain that the output of
\mathsf{P_{W}}
is always logically equivalent to its input. In other words,

\mathsf{WNF}(\phi)\text{ is logically equivalent to }\phi

whenever
\phi
is a
\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}}
-
\Delta_{0}
formula. We can therefore, without loss of generality, assume every
\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}}
-
\Delta_{0}
formula we encounter to be a member of
ran(\mathsf{WNF})
.

Now let
\mathsf{DNF}
be the function with domain

\mathrm{FV}:=\{\phi:\phi\text{ is a }\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}}\text{-}\Delta_{0}\text{ formula}\}\times\{\nu:\nu\text{ is a }\mathfrak{A}\text{-valuation}\}

defined by the following recursive procedure.

\mathsf{Procedure}

\mathsf{P_{0}}

On input
(\phi,\nu)
:

(1)

Set
\phi^{\prime}:=\mathsf{DNF}_{1}(\phi)
.

(2)

If there is no
\phi^{\prime}
-max peeling containing
\ulcorner E\urcorner
, return
\phi^{\prime}
.

(3)

For each
\phi^{\prime}
-max peeling
\varphi
containing
\ulcorner E\urcorner
(the order does not matter because of Remark
3.22
):

(F1)

Necessarily, for some
\ulcorner p\urcorner
and
\varphi^{\prime}
,

\displaystyle\varphi=\ulcorner\forall z\ (

\displaystyle(z\in p\wedge\text{``}\emptyset\neq p\text{ and }p\text{ is Dedekind-finite''})

\displaystyle\implies\varphi^{\prime})\urcorner\text{.}

There are only two possible cases.

Case 1:

\varphi
is a conjunct of a disjunct of
\phi
’.

Case 2:

\ulcorner\neg\varphi\urcorner
is a conjunct of a disjunct of
\phi
’.

For our next step, we consider these two cases separately.

(F2)

In the event of
(3)((F2))Case 1:

If
\ulcorner p\urcorner
is a not a free variable, then it must be a constant symbol. In this case, we check if

\mathfrak{A}\models\text{``}\emptyset\neq p\text{ and }p\text{ is Dedekind-finite''.}

If so, then it follows from
(2)
to
(4)
of Remark
3.5
that

•

\ulcorner p\urcorner^{\mathfrak{A}}
, the interpretation of
\ulcorner p\urcorner
by
\mathfrak{A}
, is indeed a non-empty finite set, and

•

\ulcorner p\urcorner^{\mathfrak{A}}\subset A
;

we can — and shall — thus replace
\varphi
with

\ulcorner\bigwedge\{\varphi^{\prime}[z\mapsto a]:a\in\ulcorner p\urcorner^{\mathfrak{A}}\}\urcorner

in
\phi^{\prime}
. If not, replace
\varphi
with
\ulcorner\text{``}\emptyset=\emptyset\text{''}\urcorner
in
\phi^{\prime}
.

Next, consider the case where
\ulcorner p\urcorner
is a free variable. In this case, we check if

\mathfrak{A}\models\text{``}\emptyset\neq\nu(p)\text{ and }\nu(p)\text{ is Dedekind-finite''.}

If so, then it follows from
(2)
to
(4)
of Remark
3.5
that

•

\nu(p)
is indeed a non-empty finite set, and

•

\nu(p)\subset A
;

we shall thus replace
\varphi
with

\ulcorner p=\nu(p)\wedge\bigwedge\{\varphi^{\prime}[z\mapsto a]:a\in\nu(p)\}\urcorner

in
\phi^{\prime}
. Otherwise, replace
\varphi
with

\ulcorner\neg\text{``}\emptyset\neq p\text{ and }p\text{ is Dedekind-finite''}\urcorner

in
\phi^{\prime}
.

In the event of
(3)((F2))Case 2:

If
\ulcorner p\urcorner
is a not a free variable, then it must be a constant symbol. In this case, we check if

\mathfrak{A}\models\text{``}\emptyset\neq p\text{ and }p\text{ is Dedekind-finite''.}

If so, then it follows from
(2)
to
(4)
of Remark
3.5
that

•

\ulcorner p\urcorner^{\mathfrak{A}}
, the interpretation of
\ulcorner p\urcorner
by
\mathfrak{A}
, is indeed a non-empty finite set, and

•

\ulcorner p\urcorner^{\mathfrak{A}}\subset A
;

we can — and shall — thus replace
\varphi
with

\ulcorner\bigvee\{\neg\varphi^{\prime}[z\mapsto a]:a\in\ulcorner p\urcorner^{\mathfrak{A}}\}\urcorner

in
\phi^{\prime}
. If not, replace
\varphi
with
\ulcorner\text{``}\emptyset\neq\emptyset\text{''}\urcorner
in
\phi^{\prime}
.

Next, consider the case where
\ulcorner p\urcorner
is a free variable. In this case, we check if

\mathfrak{A}\models\text{``}\emptyset\neq\nu(p)\text{ and }\nu(p)\text{ is Dedekind-finite''.}

If so, then it follows from
(2)
to
(4)
of Remark
3.5
that

•

\nu(p)
is indeed a non-empty finite set, and

•

\nu(p)\subset A
;

we shall thus replace
\varphi
with

\ulcorner p=\nu(p)\wedge\bigvee\{\neg\varphi^{\prime}[z\mapsto a]:a\in\nu(p)\}\urcorner

in
\phi^{\prime}
. Otherwise, replace
\varphi
with

\ulcorner\text{``}\emptyset\neq\emptyset\text{''}\urcorner

in
\phi^{\prime}
.

(4)

With the value of
\phi^{\prime}
updated as per Step
(3)(F2)
, call
\mathsf{P_{0}}(\phi^{\prime},\nu)
.

Remark 3.26
.

That
\mathsf{P_{0}}
always terminates and returns a safe
\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}}
-
\Delta_{0}
formula can be easily verified. Furthermore, since

•

Remark
3.24
holds,

•

\mathsf{P_{0}}
returns the result of a function call of
\mathsf{DNF}_{1}
right before termination, and

•

the atomic nature of each
\phi
-max peeling in the running of
\mathsf{P_{1}}
on input
\phi
means
\mathsf{DNF}_{1}(\phi)
cannot be safe if
\phi
is not,

\mathsf{P_{0}}
must return a member of
\mathcal{D}
. We can thus conclude that
\mathsf{DNF}
is a function from
\mathrm{FV}
into
\mathcal{D}
.

The next three propositions can be verified by routine — if tedious — applications of mathematical induction, with
(III)
and
(IV)
in mind.

Proposition 3.27
.

Let
\phi\in ran(\mathsf{WNF})
. Then for any
\mathfrak{A}
-valuation
\nu
,

\mathsf{pos}(\mathsf{DNF}(\phi,\nu))=\mathsf{DNF}(\mathsf{pos}(\phi),\nu)\text{.}

Proposition 3.28
.

Let

•

X
be a set in some weak outer model of
V
,

•

(\phi,\nu)\in\mathrm{FV}
, and

•

\nu^{\prime}
be a
\mathfrak{A}
-valuation.

Then

(1)

\mathsf{DNF}(\nu^{*}(\phi),\nu^{\prime})
is a sentence and

X\models^{*}_{\mathfrak{A},\nu}\phi\iff X\models^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}}\mathsf{DNF}(\nu^{*}(\phi),\nu^{\prime})\text{,}

(2)

X\models^{*}_{\mathfrak{A},\nu}\phi\implies X\models^{*}_{\mathfrak{A},\nu}\mathsf{DNF}(\phi,\nu)
,

(3)

X\models^{*}_{\mathfrak{A},\nu^{\prime}}\mathsf{DNF}(\phi,\nu)\implies X\models^{*}_{\mathfrak{A},\nu^{\prime}}\phi
,

Definition 3.29
.

A first-order formula
\phi
is
\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}}
-
\Sigma_{1}
iff it is of the form

\ulcorner\exists y_{1}\dots\exists y_{j}\ \phi\urcorner,

where

•

j<\omega
,

•

\phi
is
\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}}
-
\Delta_{0}
,

•

y_{1},\dots,y_{j}
are variables not bound in
\phi
.

Definition 3.30
.

A first-order formula
\phi
is
\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}}
-
\Pi_{2}
iff it is of the form

\ulcorner\forall x_{1}\dots\forall x_{i}\ \phi\urcorner,

where

•

i<\omega
,

•

\phi
is
\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}}
-
\Sigma_{1}
,

•

x_{1},\dots,x_{i}
are variables not bound in
\phi
.

Remark 3.31
.

Analogous to what the classification of general first-order formulas in prenex normal form, we can very naturally build on Definitions
3.17
,
3.29
and
3.30
, and inductively define
\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}}
-
\Sigma_{n}
and
\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}}
-
\Pi_{n}
sentences for all
n<\omega
. The only reason we did not is because our theorems and analyses neither mention nor require formulas outside of
\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}}
-
\Pi_{2}
.

Note also that in the definition of
\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}}
-
\Delta_{0}
formulas, members of
\mathcal{L}^{*}_{0,\mathfrak{A}}
are regarded, for all practical purposes, as atomic formulas. Further, bounded quantification is limited to finite sets — more in the spirit of arithmetical bounded quantification than the usual set-theoretic one. This is not merely a cosmetic choice, for extending bounded quantification to countable sets would render Lemma
3.42
false, as we shall show in Lemma
3.57
.

Lemma 3.32
.

Let

•

\phi
be a
\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}}
-
\Sigma_{1}
formula, and

•

(\nu,W,X)
witness the
(\mathfrak{A},\mathcal{L})
-satisfiability of
\phi
.

Then there are finite sets
p\in A\cap\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{L})
and
q\in A\cap\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{L})
such that

•

X\cap q=p
, and

•

\phi
is
\models^{*}_{\mathfrak{A},\nu}
-true for
(p,q)
.

Moreover, if
\phi
is a
\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}}
-
\Delta_{0}
sentence, then
\phi
is
\models^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}}
-true for
(p,q)
.

Proof.

By induction on the length of
\phi
. We work in
W
throughout.

Case 1:

\phi
is
\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}}
-
\Delta_{0}
. By
(1)
of Proposition
3.28
,

X\models^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}}\mathsf{DNF}(\nu^{*}(\phi),\nu^{\prime})

for some (in fact, any)
\mathfrak{A}
-valuation
\nu^{\prime}
. Next, since
\mathsf{DNF}(\nu^{*}(\phi),\nu^{\prime})\in\mathcal{D}
according to Remark
3.26
, there is a disjunct
\varphi
of
\mathsf{DNF}(\nu^{*}(\phi),\nu^{\prime})
for which
X\models^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}}\varphi
.

Let
(p,q)
be
\mathsf{set}(\varphi)
. Then
p
and
q
are finite sets with
p\subset q\subset\mathcal{L}
. Since
(A;\in)
models enough set theory and
\mathcal{L}\subset A
, we too have
\{p,q\}\subset A
. As
\varphi
has all occurrences of literals over
\{E\}
being conjuncts, due to

•

\varphi
being a disjunct of
\mathsf{DNF}(\nu^{*}(\phi),\nu^{\prime})
,

•

the definition of
\mathcal{D}
, and

•

the fact that
\mathsf{DNF}(\nu^{*}(\phi),\nu^{\prime})\in\mathcal{D}
,

we must have

X\cap q=p.

Fix
X^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{L}
in any weak outer model of
V
. It is now clear that

X^{\prime}\cap q=p\implies X^{\prime}\models^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}}\varphi\implies X^{\prime}\models^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}}\mathsf{DNF}(\nu^{*}(\phi),\nu^{\prime}).

By
(1)
of Proposition
3.28
again,

X^{\prime}\models^{*}_{\mathfrak{A},\nu}\phi\text{.}

We have thus shown that
\phi
is
\models^{*}_{\mathfrak{A},\nu}
-true for
(p,q)
.

If
\phi
is a sentence, then whenever
\nu^{\prime\prime}
is a
\mathfrak{A}
-valuation,

\nu^{*}(\phi)=(\nu^{\prime\prime})^{*}(\phi)\text{,}

and so

\mathsf{DNF}(\nu^{*}(\phi),\nu^{\prime})=\mathsf{DNF}((\nu^{\prime\prime})^{*}(\phi),\nu^{\prime})\text{.}

But this means
\phi
is
\models^{*}_{\mathfrak{A},\nu^{\prime\prime}}
-true for
(p,q)
for all
\mathfrak{A}
-valuations
\nu^{\prime\prime}
, or equivalently,
\phi
is
\models^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}}
-true for
(p,q)
.

Case 2:

\phi=\ulcorner\exists y\ \phi^{\prime}\urcorner
for some
y
and
\phi^{\prime}
. Then there must be a
\mathfrak{A}
-valuation
\nu^{\prime}
that agrees with
\nu
on the free variables of
\phi
, for which

X\models^{*}_{\mathfrak{A},\nu^{\prime}}\phi^{\prime}.

By the induction hypothesis, there are finite sets
p\in A\cap\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{L})
and
q\in A\cap\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{L})
satisfying

•

X\cap q=p
and

•

\phi^{\prime}
is
\models^{*}_{\mathfrak{A},\nu^{\prime}}
-true for
(p,q)
.

Since
(\nu^{\prime})^{*}(\phi^{\prime})
logically implies
\nu^{*}(\phi)
, it must also be that
\phi
is
\models^{*}_{\mathfrak{A},\nu}
-true for
(p,q)
. ∎

For any free variable
x
and any
\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}}
formula
\phi
, it is often a desideratum (if not an imperative) in practice to have
\phi
explicitly “guarantee
E(\neg x)
insofar as
x
is a member of
\mathcal{L}
”, whenever
\ulcorner\neg E(x)\urcorner
occurs in
\phi
. Towards this end, we are incentivised to augment
\phi
with a suitable gadget.

Definition 3.33
.

Let
\phi
be a
\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}}
-
\Delta_{0}
formula. Define
\mathsf{check}(\phi)
to be the unique result of replacing every atomic subformula
\varphi
of
\phi
satisfying

\varphi=\ulcorner\neg E(x)\urcorner\text{ for some variable }x

with

\ulcorner(\varphi\wedge(\text{``}x\in\mathcal{L}\text{''}\implies\text{``}E(\neg x)\text{''}))\urcorner

in
\phi
.

Remark 3.34
.

In the definition of
\mathsf{check}
,
\text{``}E(\neg x)\text{''}
is a shorthand for both

\ulcorner\exists z\ (\text{``}z=\neg(x)\text{''}\wedge E(z))\urcorner

and

\ulcorner\forall z\ (\text{``}z=\neg(x)\text{''}\implies E(z))\urcorner,

where
\neg(\cdot)
is the negation function on
V
(see Definition
3.1
), so that “
z=\neg(x)
” is expressible as a formula in
\mathcal{L}^{*}_{0,\mathfrak{A}}
by Remark
3.2
. Particularly, since

•

\text{``}E(\neg x)\text{''}
means exactly

\ulcorner\forall z\ (\text{``}z=\neg(x)\text{''}\implies E(z))\urcorner,

and

•

\mathfrak{A}
is a model of a sufficiently strong set theory,

we have that

\displaystyle X\models^{*}_{\mathfrak{A},\nu}\text{``}E(\neg x)\text{''}\iff

\displaystyle X\models^{*}_{\mathfrak{A},\nu}\ulcorner\exists p\ \forall z\ ((z\in p\wedge\text{``}\emptyset\neq p\text{ and }p\text{ is Dedekind-finite''})

\displaystyle\mspace{125mu}\implies(\text{``}z=\neg(x)\text{''}\implies E(z)))\urcorner

in case

•

X
is a set in some weak outer model of
V
,

•

\nu
is a
\mathfrak{A}
-valuation, and

•

\ulcorner p\urcorner
is a variable outside
\{\ulcorner x\urcorner,\ulcorner z\urcorner\}
bound in neither (the formal statement of)

\text{``}z=\neg(x)\text{''}

nor (the formal statement of)

\text{``}\emptyset\neq p\text{ and }p\text{ is Dedekind-finite''.}

As an implication, if
\ulcorner\exists x_{1}\dots\exists x_{n}\ \phi\urcorner
is a
\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}}
-
\Sigma_{1}
formula with
\phi\in ran(\mathsf{WNF})
, then we can show by induction on the complexity of
\phi
, noting

•

the recursive structure of
\mathsf{P_{W}}
,

•

how
\mathsf{DNF_{1}}
is used in
\mathsf{P_{W}}
, and

•

Remark
3.24
,

that there exists a
\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}}
-
\Sigma_{1}
formula
\varphi
satisfying

X\models^{*}_{\mathfrak{A},\nu}\ulcorner\exists x_{1}\dots\exists x_{n}\ \mathsf{check}(\mathsf{pos}(\phi))\urcorner\iff X\models^{*}_{\mathfrak{A},\nu}\varphi

for every

•

\mathfrak{A}
-valuation
\nu
, and

•

set
X
found in a weak outer model of
V
.

We may thus assume, without loss of generality, that formulas of the form

\ulcorner\exists x_{1}\dots\exists x_{n}\ \mathsf{check}(\mathsf{pos}(\phi))\urcorner

are
\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}}
-
\Sigma_{1}
, as long as

•

n<\omega
, and

•

\phi\in ran(\mathsf{WNF})
.

The fact below can be derived from definitions through straightforward variable tracing.
