, we are left with showing
\Sigma^{*}\models^{*}_{j(\mathfrak{A})}j(\Gamma_{\lambda_{f}})
. That

\Sigma^{*}\models^{*}_{j(\mathfrak{A})}j(\text{(S<}k\text{>)}_{\lambda_{f}})

for <

Lemma 4.28
.

\mathbb{P}
is stationary-preserving.

Proof.

Let

•

S\in S(\omega_{1})
,

•

p\in\mathbb{P}
,

•

\dot{C}
be a
\mathbb{P}
-name such that
p\Vdash_{\mathbb{P}}``\dot{C}
is a club in
\omega_{1}^{W}"
,

•

D:=\{(q,\eta)\in\mathbb{P}\times\omega_{1}:q\Vdash_{\mathbb{P}}\eta\in\dot{C}\}
,

•

g
be a
\mathbb{P}
-generic filter over
W
with
p\in g
.

Applying (
\diamond
) with

•

\mathbb{P}
in place of
P
, and

•

D
in place of
B
,

we get

\displaystyle U:=\{\lambda\in C:(Q_{\lambda};\in,\mathbb{P},A_{\lambda})\prec(H(\lambda_{f});\in,\mathbb{P},D)\}

is stationary in
\lambda_{f}
, so
U\in U(C,\lambda_{f})
.

In
W[g]
, there are
\mu\in S
and
\nu\in U
such that

\displaystyle\emptyset\neq(X_{\mu,\nu}(\bigcup g);\in,\mathbb{P},A_{\nu})\prec(Q_{\nu};\in,\mathbb{P},A_{\nu})\prec(H(\lambda_{f});\in,\mathbb{P},D),

since

\bigcup g\models^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}}\Gamma_{\lambda_{f}}\text{ (in particular }\bigcup g\models^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}}\text{(S7)}_{\lambda_{f}}\text{) and }\bigcup g\models^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}}\text{(}\ref{c9}\text{)}

by Lemmas
3.42
,
4.24
and
4.27
. Now, noting
(P1)
, we have

\displaystyle\emptyset\neq(X_{\mu,\nu}(\bigcup g);\in,\mathbb{P},D)\prec(Q_{\nu};\in,\mathbb{P}_{\nu},A_{\nu})\prec(H(\lambda_{f});\in,\mathbb{P},D).

(4.3)

It suffices to show that
\mu
is a limit point of
\dot{C}[g]
. We fix
\zeta<\mu
and seek some
\eta\in\dot{C}[g]
with
\zeta<\eta<\mu
.

The set

E_{\zeta}:=\{q\in\mathbb{P}:\exists\eta>\zeta\ ((q,\eta)\in D)\}

is dense in
\mathbb{P}
, so (
4.3
) tells us

E_{\zeta}\cap Q_{\nu}=\{q\in\mathbb{P}_{\nu}:\exists\eta>\zeta\ ((q,\eta)\in A_{\nu})\}

is dense in
\mathbb{P}_{\nu}
. As
\bigcup g\models^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}}\text{(S5)}_{\lambda_{f}}
, we know
\zeta\in X_{\mu,\nu}(\bigcup g)
. Having
\bigcup g\models^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}}\text{(S8)}_{\lambda_{f}}
then bestows us the existence of some

q\in[\bigcup g]^{<\omega}\cap X_{\mu,\nu}(\bigcup g)\cap E_{\zeta}\cap Q_{\nu}\neq\emptyset.

That
q\in X_{\mu,\nu}(\bigcup g)
and (
4.3
) holds means

(X_{\mu,\nu}(\bigcup g);\in,\mathbb{P},D)\models\exists\eta>\zeta\ ((q,\eta)\in D).

Invoking the fact that
\bigcup g\models^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}}\text{(S6)}_{\lambda_{f}}
gives us some
\eta
such that

•

\zeta<\eta<\mu
, and

•

(q,\eta)\in D
.

Recalling the definition of
D
, we conclude
\eta\in\dot{C}[g]
because
q\in[\bigcup g]^{<\omega}=g
,
∎

Lemma 4.29
.

\Vdash_{\mathbb{P}}``cof(\lambda_{f})=\omega_{1}"
. That is,
\mathbb{P}
fulfils
(2’)
of Lemma
4.12
.

Proof.

Let
g
be
\mathbb{P}
-generic over
W
. By Lemma
4.24
,
\bigcup g\models^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}}\Gamma_{\lambda_{f}}
, so that in
W[g]
,

K:=\{(\delta,\lambda):\exists x\ (\ulcorner x\in\dot{X}_{\delta,\lambda}\urcorner\in g)\}

is a strictly increasing function with domain contained in
\omega_{1}^{W}
and range contained in
\lambda_{f}
. Lemma
4.27
tells us that
dom(K)
is cofinal in
\omega_{1}^{W}
and
ran(K)
is cofinal in
\lambda_{f}
, hence

cof^{W[g]}(\lambda_{f})=cof^{W[g]}(\omega_{1}^{W}).

By Lemma
4.28
, we have
\omega_{1}^{W}=\omega_{1}^{W[g]}
, and consequently,

cof^{W[g]}(\lambda_{f})=\omega_{1}^{W[g]}.\qed

In view of Lemma
4.12
, the theorem follows from Lemmas
4.21
,
4.25
,
4.28
and
4.29
.
∎

According to Theorem
4.11
, if
\mathrm{NS}_{\omega_{1}}
is precipitous, then there is a uniform way of generating witnesses — in place of
\mathbb{P}_{N}
— to analogues of Fact
4.7
. To wit, we have the following corollary.

Corollary 4.30
.

Assume

•

\mathrm{NS}_{\omega_{1}}
is precipitous, and

•

2^{\omega_{1}}=\omega_{2}
.

Then
Nb^{\prime}_{1}(\lambda)
— thus also
Nb_{1}(\lambda)
— holds for each regular cardinal
\lambda>\omega_{2}
. Furthermore,
Nb_{0}(\alpha)
holds for each ordinal
\alpha>\omega_{2}
.

An advantage of the forcing framework of Subsection
3.3
is that it facilitates modular analyses of the generic object. Adding components to the generic object can be done by extending the language on which the forcing notion is based. Under the right circumstances, that said addition preserves a property of the original forcing notion is readily derived from examining the extended language.

In the next subsection, we will augment the forcing notion
\mathbb{P}
defined in the proof of Theorem
4.11
while assuming a stronger hypothesis, so that the
\mathbb{P}
-generic object has a generic iteration as one of its components. The reader should notice that there is ample carryover from the proof of Theorem
4.11
in the analysis of the new and augmented
\mathbb{P}
.

4.3
Incorporating the Asperó-Schindler Construction

Asperó’s and Schindler’s approach to proving “
\mathsf{MM}^{++}
implies
(*)
” in
[
16
]
goes along the following lines.

(1)

Assume
\mathsf{MM}^{++}
.

(2)

Define

\displaystyle g_{A}:=\{\bar{N}\in\mathbb{P}_{max}:\

there is a generic iteration

\displaystyle\langle\bar{N}_{i}=(N_{i};\in,I_{i},a_{i}),\sigma_{ij}:i\leq j\leq\omega_{1}\rangle

\displaystyle\text{of }\bar{N}\text{ such that}

\displaystyle a_{\omega_{1}}=A\text{ and }\mathrm{NS}_{\omega_{1}}\cap N_{\omega_{1}}=I_{\omega_{1}}\}\text{.}

(3)

Show that whenever
\omega_{1}^{L[A]}=\omega_{1}
,

•

g_{A}
is a filter, and

•

if
g_{A}
is
\mathbb{P}_{max}
-generic over
L(\mathbb{R})
, then
\mathcal{P}(\omega_{1})\subset L(\mathbb{R})[g_{A}]
.

(4)

For each dense subset
D\in L(\mathbb{R})
of
\mathbb{P}_{max}
, find a stationary-preserving forcing notion
\mathbb{P}(D)
that forces

“there are
p\in D^{*}
(
D^{*}
being the interpretation of
D
in
V^{\mathbb{P}(D)}
via some universally Baire encoding) and a generic iteration

\langle\bar{N}_{i}=(N_{i};\in,I_{i},a_{i}),\sigma_{ij}:i\leq j\leq\omega_{1}^{V}\rangle

for which

•

p=\bar{N}_{0}
,

•

I_{\omega_{1}^{V}}=\mathrm{NS}_{\omega_{1}}\cap N_{\omega_{1}^{V}}
, and

•

a_{\omega_{1}^{V}}=A
”,

so that
g_{A}
is
\mathbb{P}_{max}
-generic over
L(\mathbb{R})
.

Each of the
\mathbb{P}(D)
’s satisfying
(4)
, as defined in the proof of Lemma 2.14 of
[
16
]
, possesses curious properties tangential to its chief purpose:

(i)

its conditions are fragments of a language depending on
D
,

(ii)

it forces “
cof(\omega_{2}^{V})=\omega
”, and

(iii)

it forces “
cof(\omega_{3}^{V})=\omega_{1}
”.

Since
(ii)
and
(iii)
make each
\mathbb{P}(D)
“Namba-like”, the conjunction of
(i)
to
(iii)
points to the viability of incorporating the design of each
\mathbb{P}(D)
into the construction of
\mathbb{P}
as described in the proof of Theorem
4.11
, so as to strengthen Lemma 2.14 of
[
16
]
. This incorporation can be thought of both as

•

an augmentation of
\mathbb{P}
to serve an expanded agenda, and

•

a means to extend the “Namba consequences” of each
\mathbb{P}(D)
,

hence it is sufficiently motivated. We shall spend the rest of this subsection ironing out the details of our (natural) incorporation attempt, with Theorem
4.41
being its upshot.

Convention 4.37

Definition 4.38

Definition 4.39

If

, let
pr(s)
denote the member
t\in{{}^{A}{B}}
such that for all
a\in A
,
t(a)=b
iff there is some
c
for which
s(a)=(b,c)
.

We will use the fact below without proof.

Fact 4.40
.

Assume

(i)

\Gamma=\bigcup_{1\leq k<\omega}\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{k})\cap L(\Gamma,\mathbb{R})
,

(ii)

\Gamma
is productive,

(iii)

\mathrm{NS}_{\omega_{1}}
is saturated,

(iv)

2^{\omega_{1}}=\mathbf{\delta^{1}_{2}}=\omega_{2}
, and

(v)

MA(\omega_{1})
holds.

Let
D\in L(\Gamma,\mathbb{R})
be a dense subset of
\mathbb{P}_{max}
, and
A\subset\omega_{1}
such that
\omega_{1}^{L[A]}=\omega_{1}
. Then there are

•

a
\Delta_{1}
-definable partial map
F^{*}
from
{}^{\omega}{\omega}
onto the members of
\mathbb{P}_{max}
,

•

a tree
T
of size
\aleph_{2}
on
\omega\times\omega_{2}
, and

•

a
Col(\omega,\omega_{2})
-name
\dot{p}\subset H(\omega_{2})
for a member of
{{}^{\omega}{\omega}}

such that

(4.34.1)

\Vdash_{Col(\omega,\omega_{2})}``\dot{p}\in p[T]\wedge F^{*}(\dot{p})\leq_{\mathbb{P}_{max}}(H(\omega_{2})^{V};\in,\mathrm{NS}_{\omega_{1}}^{V},A)"
,

(4.34.2)

D^{\prime}:=(F^{*})^{-1}(D)
is universally Baire,

and in every forcing extension of
V
,

(4.34.3)

D^{\prime*}\subset dom(F^{*})
,

(4.34.4)

D^{*}:=(F^{*})"(D^{\prime*})
is a dense subset of
\mathbb{P}_{max}
,

(4.34.5)

F^{*}(pr(\bigcup S))\in D^{*}
for every
S
satisfying

•

S\subset T
, and

•

\bigcup S\in[T]
,

(4.34.6)

whenever
\bar{M}
,
\bar{N}
,
f
,
S
fulfil the following:

•

\bar{N}
is an expansion of some structure of the form
(N;\in,I,a)
, where

interprets
\dot{I}
and

interprets
\dot{a}
,

•

f:\omega\longrightarrow N
is a surjection,

•

S\subset T
,

•

\bigcup S\in[T]
, and

•

ran(pr(\bigcup S))=Gd"Th^{0}_{\mathcal{L}}(\bar{N},f)
,

it must be the case that
F^{*}(pr(\bigcup S))=\bar{N}\in D^{*}
, and

(4.34.7)

whenever
\bar{M}
,
\bar{N}
fulfil the following:

•

\bar{N}
is a member of
\mathbb{P}_{max}
, and

•

\bar{N}\models``\bar{M}\text{ is a member of }\mathbb{P}_{max}"
,

it must be the case that

•

\bar{M}\in\bar{N}
, and

•

\bar{M}
is a member of
\mathbb{P}_{max}
.

Fix
F^{*}
,
T
and
\dot{p}
as provided by Fact
4.40
. In light of said fact, we can make sense of — and subsequently prove — the next theorem.

Theorem 4.41
.

Assume

(i)

\Gamma=\bigcup_{1\leq k<\omega}\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{k})\cap L(\Gamma,\mathbb{R})
,

(ii)

\Gamma
is productive,

(iii)

\mathrm{NS}_{\omega_{1}}
is saturated,

(iv)

2^{\omega_{1}}=\mathbf{\delta^{1}_{2}}=\omega_{2}
, and

(v)

MA(\omega_{1})
holds.

Let

•

D\in L(\Gamma,\mathbb{R})
be a dense subset of
\mathbb{P}_{max}
,

•

A\subset\omega_{1}
be such that
\omega_{1}^{L[A]}=\omega_{1}
, and

•

\lambda_{f}>\omega_{2}
be a regular cardinal .

Then there is a stationary-preserving forcing notion
\mathbb{P}
such that in
V^{\mathbb{P}}
,

•

there is a generic iteration

\langle\bar{N}_{i}=(N_{i};\in,I_{i},a_{i}),\sigma_{ij}:i\leq j\leq\omega_{1}^{V}\rangle

satisfying

(1)

\bar{N}_{0}\in D^{*}:=(F^{*})"((F^{*})^{-1}(D)^{*})\subset\mathbb{P}_{max}
,

(2)

I_{\omega_{1}^{V}}=\mathrm{NS}_{\omega_{1}}^{V^{\mathbb{P}}}\cap N_{\omega_{1}^{V}}
, and

(3)

a_{\omega_{1}^{V}}=A
, and

•

Nb^{\prime}_{1}(\lambda_{f})
holds.

Proof.

We first import the notation the labelling of definitions from the proof of Theorem
4.11
; they will be reused until subsequent reassignments.

Move to
W
as in the proof of Theorem
4.11
via forcing with
Col(\lambda_{f},\lambda_{f})
, so that

(
\diamond
)

for all
P,B\subset H(\lambda_{f})
, the set

\displaystyle\{\lambda\in C:(Q_{\lambda};\in,P,A_{\lambda})\prec(H(\lambda_{f});\in,P,B)\}

is stationary in
\lambda_{f}
.

holds in
W
. We further require

\mathrm{Par}:=\{T,\dot{p},H(\omega_{2})^{W}=H(\omega_{2})^{V},\mathrm{NS}_{\omega_{1}}^{W}=\mathrm{NS}_{\omega_{1}}^{V},A\}\subset Q_{\lambda}

for all
\lambda\in C
. But this is easily done because
\mathrm{Par}
is small by Fact
4.40
and the hypothesis of the theorem.

Set

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{o}:=\

\displaystyle\text{the closure of }\mathcal{L}_{1}\cup\mathcal{L}_{2}\text{ under negation}

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{s}:=\

\displaystyle\text{the closure of }\mathcal{L}^{s_{0}}\text{ under negation},

and enlarge
\mathcal{L}
just enough to include
\mathcal{L}^{s}
. It is easy to see that

•

\mathcal{L}^{o}
is the original
\mathcal{L}
before enlargement,

•

both
\mathcal{L}^{s}
and the newly enlarged
\mathcal{L}
are closed under negation,

•

\mathcal{L}
now equals
\mathcal{L}^{o}\cup\mathcal{L}^{s}
, and

•

\mathcal{L}^{s}\subset Q_{\lambda}
for every
\lambda\in C
, so that

•

\mathcal{L}\cap Q_{\lambda}=\mathcal{L}^{s}\sqcup(\mathcal{L}^{o}\cap Q_{\lambda})
for every
\lambda\in C\cup\{\lambda_{f}\}
.

Remark 4.42
.

The naturally extended version of Remark
4.14
applies to the updated
\mathcal{L}
.

Note also that the following hold in
W
:

•

the hypothesis
(iii)
, and

•

the conclusion of Fact
4.40
(i.e. the conjunction of
(4.34.1)
to
(4.34.7)
) with our given
D
and
A
.

The aforementioned truths in
W
are all we need to proceed, aided by the next fact, which can be viewed as an extension of Lemma
4.12
.

Fact 4.43
.

The theorem holds if in
W
, we can define a forcing notion
\mathbb{P}
with the following properties:

(K1)

\mathbb{P}\subset H(\lambda_{f})
, so that
|\mathbb{P}|\leq\lambda_{f}
by the proof of Lemma
4.21
,

(K2)

\mathbb{P}
is stationary-preserving,

(K3)

in
V^{\mathbb{P}}
there is a generic iteration

\langle\bar{N}_{i}=(N_{i};\in,I_{i},a_{i}),\sigma_{ij}:i\leq j\leq\omega_{1}^{V}\rangle

satisfying

(1)

\bar{N}_{0}\in D^{*}\subset\mathbb{P}_{max}
,

(2)

I_{\omega_{1}^{V}}=\mathrm{NS}_{\omega_{1}}^{V^{\mathbb{P}}}\cap N_{\omega_{1}^{V}}
and

(3)

a_{\omega_{1}^{V}}=A
.

(K4)

\Vdash_{\mathbb{P}}``cof(\alpha)=\omega"
for all regular cardinals
\alpha
satisfying
\omega_{2}\leq\alpha<\lambda_{f}
,

(K5)

\Vdash_{\mathbb{P}}``cof(\lambda_{f})=\omega_{1}"
,

Going forward, unless otherwise specified,

•

we work in
W
towards a forcing notion
\mathbb{P}
as in Fact
4.43
, and

•

every new object (to be) defined in
W
always denotes its realisation in
W
.

Recall that

\displaystyle\kappa

\displaystyle:=(2^{\lambda_{f}})^{+}\text{,}

\displaystyle\mathfrak{A}

\displaystyle:=(H(\kappa);\in)\text{, and}

\displaystyle R

\displaystyle:=\{i<\lambda_{f}:\omega_{2}\leq i\text{ and }i\text{ is regular}\},

We shall formally describe the mathematical object that forcing with fragments of
\mathcal{L}
is supposed to help construct.

Definition 4.48
.

Let
\Gamma^{\prime}
be the following set

\displaystyle\{\phi:\

\displaystyle\phi\text{ is a }(\mathcal{L}^{s})^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}}\text{-}\Pi_{2}\text{ sentence and}

\displaystyle\Vdash_{Col(\omega,\lambda_{f})}\forall\mathfrak{D}\ \forall\lambda\in C\cup\{\lambda_{f}\}

\displaystyle\mspace{80mu}(``\mathfrak{D}\text{ is a }\lambda\text{-certificate}\implies\Sigma^{\prime}(\mathfrak{D},\mathcal{L}\cap Q_{\lambda})\models^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}}\phi")\}\text{.}

Notice whenever
\phi
is a
(\mathcal{L}^{s})^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}}
-
\Pi_{2}
sentence, it must be the case that in any

-generic extension of

,

\varphi(\phi):=\forall\mathfrak{D}\ \forall\lambda\in C\cup\{\lambda_{f}\}\ (``\mathfrak{D}\text{ is a }\lambda\text{-certificate}\implies\Sigma^{\prime}(\mathfrak{D},\mathcal{L}\cap Q_{\lambda})\models^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}}\phi")

is equivalent to a
\mathbf{\Pi^{1}_{2}}
sentence. We can thus employ an argument akin to that which proved Lemma
3.55
, bearing in mind to replace each invocation of Mostowski’s absoluteness theorem with an invocation of Shoenfield’s absoluteness theorem, to obtain the fact below.

Fact 4.49
.

Let
\phi
be a
(\mathcal{L}^{s})^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}}
-
\Pi_{2}
sentence. Then the following are equivalent.

(1)
ϕ

\Vdash_{Col(\omega,\lambda_{f})}\varphi(\phi)
.

(2)
ϕ

\mspace{-3mu}\not\mspace{3mu}\Vdash_{Col(\omega,\lambda_{f})}\neg\varphi(\phi)
.

(3)
ϕ

W\models\varphi(\phi)
for every outer model
W
of
V
.

Remark 4.50
.

That statements
(1)ϕ
and
(2)ϕ
of Fact
4.49
are equivalent can also be derived directly from the homogeneity of

.

Therefore,
\Gamma^{\prime}
is precisely the set

\displaystyle\{\phi:\phi\text{ is a }(\mathcal{L}^{s})^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}}\text{-}\Pi_{2}\text{ sentence and }W\models\varphi(\phi)\text{ for every outer model }W\text{ of }V\}\text{.}

As in the proof of Theorem
4.11
, we will (re)define

\{\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}:\lambda\in C\cup\{\lambda_{f}\}\}

by induction on
\lambda
. Indeed, we are inductively modifying the definitions of the

’s we knew from the proof of Theorem

. Assume that

has been modified for all
\lambda^{\prime}\in\lambda\cap C
.

Definition 4.51
.

Set

\Gamma^{\prime}_{\lambda}:=\Gamma^{\prime}\cup\Gamma_{\lambda}\text{,}

where

is as in Definition

.

Remark 4.52
.

(1)

If
\lambda\in C\cup\{\lambda_{f}\}
and

\mathfrak{D}=\langle\langle\bar{M}_{i},\pi_{ij},\bar{N}_{i},\sigma_{ij}:i\leq j\leq\omega_{1}^{W}\rangle,S,\langle e_{i}:i<\omega_{1}^{W}\rangle,\mathfrak{C}\rangle

, as long as

.

(3)

If

, then also

(\Sigma\cap\mathcal{L}^{o})\ \Gamma_{\lambda}(\mathcal{L}^{o}\cap Q_{\lambda},\mathfrak{A})\text{-certifies }\emptyset\text{.}

Redefine
\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}
as follows:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}:=\

\displaystyle(P_{\lambda},\leq_{\lambda})\text{, where }

\displaystyle P_{\lambda}:=\

\displaystyle\{p\in[\mathcal{L}\cap Q_{\lambda}]^{<\omega}:\ \Vdash_{Col(\omega,|H(\kappa)|)}\exists\Sigma\ (``\Sigma\ \Gamma^{\prime}_{\lambda}(\mathcal{L}\cap Q_{\lambda},\mathfrak{A})\text{-certifies }p")\},\text{ and}

\displaystyle\leq_{\lambda}\ :=\

\displaystyle\{(p,q)\in P_{\lambda}\times P_{\lambda}:q\subset p\}.

As before, let
\mathbb{P}
denote
\mathbb{P}_{\lambda_{f}}
.

Since
\Gamma_{\lambda}
contains references to the set

\{\mathbb{P}_{\lambda^{\prime}}:\lambda^{\prime}\in\lambda\cap C\}\text{,}

so we would expect the semantic value of
\Gamma_{\lambda}
to be altered when changes are made to the definitions of the
\mathbb{P}_{\lambda^{\prime}}
’s. In fact, it is through
\Gamma_{\lambda}
that the definition of
\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}
gets updated based on the updated definitions of the
\mathbb{P}_{\lambda^{\prime}}
’s.

Remark 4.53
.

(K1)
of Fact
4.43
is obvious from the definition of
\mathbb{P}
.

Convention 4.54
.

Given an elementary embedding
\pi
of
W
into some transitive model
M
of
\mathsf{ZFC}
such that
ORD^{W}=ORD^{M}
, we say a statement (or definition)
\varphi
holds when relativised to
(\pi,M)
iff
\varphi
holds with

•

every instance therein of each parameter
\zeta
replaced by
\pi(\zeta)
, and

•

every evaluation therein, after the replacement of parameters, being done in
M
instead of
W
.

Lemma 4.55
.

For all
\lambda\in C\cup\{\lambda_{f}\}
,
\emptyset\in P_{\lambda}
.

Proof.

Fix
\lambda\in C\cup\{\lambda_{f}\}
and let

•

h
be
Col(\omega,\omega_{2})
-generic over
W
,

•

S\in W[h]
be a path on
T
such that
pr(\bigcup S)=p:=\dot{p}[h]
, and

•

\bar{N}_{0}=F^{*}(p)\in W[h]
(possible by
(4.34.1)
).

Set
\theta:=\omega_{1}^{W[h]}
. Choose a generic iteration
\langle\bar{M}_{i},\pi_{ij}:i\leq j\leq\omega_{1}^{\bar{N}_{0}}\rangle
witnessing
\bar{N}_{0}\leq_{\mathbb{P}^{W[h]}_{max}}(H(\omega_{2})^{W};\in,\mathrm{NS}_{\omega_{1}}^{W},A)
, possible by
(4.34.2)
to
(4.34.5)
. Let

•

\langle\bar{N}_{i}=(N_{i};\in,I_{i},a_{i}),\sigma_{ij}:i\leq j\leq\theta\rangle\in W[h]
be a generic iteration of
\bar{N}_{0}
,

•

\langle\bar{M}_{i}=(M_{i};\in,J_{i},b_{i}),\pi_{ij}:i\leq j\leq\theta\rangle
denote
\sigma_{0\theta}(\langle\bar{M}_{i},\pi_{ij}:i\leq j\leq\omega_{1}^{\bar{N}_{0}}\rangle)
, and

•

\langle e_{i}:i<\theta\rangle
be such that for each
i<\theta
,
e_{i}
is a bijection from
\omega
onto
N_{i}
.

Then by Lemma
2.69
,
\pi_{0\theta}
lifts to a generic ultrapower map
\pi:W\longrightarrow M
, for some inner model
M
of
W[h]
.

Now let
h^{\prime}
be
Col(\omega,\pi(\lambda_{f}))
-generic over
W[h]
, so that in
W[h][h^{\prime}]
, there is

\langle F_{i}:i\in\pi(R)\rangle

for which
F_{i}
is a strictly increasing cofinal map from
\omega
into
i
whenever
i\in\pi(R)
. It is easy to verify that if

\mathfrak{C}:=\langle\langle F_{i}:i\in\pi(R)\rangle,\langle\rangle\rangle\text{,}

then without loss of generality,

\mathfrak{D}:=\langle\langle\bar{M}_{i},\pi_{ij},\bar{N}_{i},\sigma_{ij}:i\leq j\leq\theta\rangle,S,\langle e_{i}:i<\theta\rangle,\mathfrak{C}\rangle\in W[h][h^{\prime}]

is a
\pi(\lambda)
-certificate relative to
M
. In other words,
\mathfrak{D}
fulfils the requirements of Definition
4.44
relativised to
(\pi,M)
, bearing in mind
(4)
of Remark
4.45
.

Use
(\Sigma^{\prime})^{\pi,M}(\cdot,\cdot)
to denote the function
\Sigma^{\prime}(\cdot,\cdot)
relativised to
(\pi,M)
. Following
(2)
of Remark
4.52
, we have that

•

the hypothesis on
\mathfrak{D}
in
4.47
, relativised to
(\pi,M)
, is satisfied,

•

(\Sigma^{\prime})^{\pi,M}(\mathfrak{D},\pi(\mathcal{L}\cap Q_{\lambda}))
is a set found in some weak outer model of
M
, and

•

(\Sigma^{\prime})^{\pi,M}(\mathfrak{D},\pi(\mathcal{L}\cap Q_{\lambda}))

\pi(\Gamma^{\prime}_{\lambda})(\pi(\mathcal{L}\cap Q_{\lambda}),\pi(\mathfrak{A}))
-certifies
\emptyset
.

Applying Lemma
3.55
in
M
gives us the fact that

\emptyset\in\pi(P_{\lambda})\text{,}

so also
\emptyset\in\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}
by the elementarity of
\pi
.
∎

By Proposition
3.54
,
(\mathfrak{A},\mathbb{P})
is good for
\mathcal{L}
. Obviously,
\mathbb{P}
is definable in the language associated with
\mathfrak{A}
because
\mathbb{P}\in H(\kappa)
. Moreover, the following hold as they do in the proof of Theorem
4.11
.

(P1)

P_{\lambda_{0}}=P_{\lambda_{1}}\cap Q_{\lambda_{0}}
whenever
\lambda_{0},\lambda_{1}\in C\cup\{\lambda_{f}\}
and
\lambda_{0}\leq\lambda_{1}
, and

(P2)

P_{\lambda}=\bigcup\{P_{\lambda^{\prime}}:\lambda^{\prime}\in C\cap\lambda\}
whenever
\lambda\in C\cup\{\lambda_{f}\}
and
sup(\lambda\cap C)=\lambda
.

Lemma 4.56
.

Let

•

\lambda\in C\cup\{\lambda_{f}\}
, and

•

g
be a
\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}
-
\Sigma_{1}
-generic filter over
W
.

Then
\bigcup g\ \Gamma^{\prime}_{\lambda}(\mathcal{L}\cap Q_{\lambda},\mathfrak{A})\text{-certifies }\emptyset
.

Proof.

Straightforward, by Lemma
3.56
(cf. Lemma
4.24
).
∎

Lemma 4.57
.

There is a definition
\mathfrak{D}(\cdot)
of a function in one variable such that

(1)

\mathfrak{D}(\cdot)
is absolute for forcing extensions of
W
, and

(2)

whenever

•

\lambda\in C\cup\{\lambda_{f}\}
,

•

W^{\prime}
is a forcing extension of
W
, and

•

g\in W^{\prime}
is a
\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}
-
\Sigma_{1}
-generic filter over
W
,

\mathfrak{D}(g)
is a
\lambda
-certificate satisfying

\Sigma^{\prime}(\mathfrak{D}(g),\mathcal{L})=\bigcup g\text{.}

Proof.

Let
\lambda
,
W^{\prime}
and
g
fulfil the hypothesis of the lemma. Work in
W^{\prime}
for the rest of the proof. We shall unambiguously describe — constituent by constituent — the construction of a
\lambda
-certificate

\mathfrak{D}=\langle\langle\bar{M}_{i},\pi_{ij},\bar{N}_{i},\sigma_{ij}:i\leq j\leq\omega_{1}^{W}\rangle,S,\langle e_{i}:i<\omega_{1}^{W}\rangle,\mathfrak{C}\rangle

from
g
, checking that
\mathfrak{D}
fulfils the conditions of Definition
4.44
as we go along. The reader ought to check for themselves that

•

every step of the construction, as well as the argument for the purpose it serves, requires only facts which are absolute for forcing extensions (often, even for weak outer models) of
W
, and

•

at every step of the construction, whatever can be deduced about
\Sigma^{\prime}(\mathfrak{D}(g),\mathcal{L})
is consistent with

\Sigma^{\prime}(\mathfrak{D}(g),\mathcal{L})=\bigcup g\text{.}

First, by Lemma
4.56
,

\bigcup g\ \Gamma^{\prime}_{\lambda}(\mathcal{L}\cap Q_{\lambda},\mathfrak{A})\text{-certifies }\emptyset\text{,}

so
(C12)λ
of Definition
4.44
is satisfied with

\mathfrak{C}:=\langle\langle F_{i}(\bigcup g\cap\mathcal{L}^{o}):i\in R\cap\lambda\rangle,\langle X_{\delta,\lambda^{\prime}}(\bigcup g\cap\mathcal{L}^{o}):\delta<\omega_{1},\lambda^{\prime}\in C\cap\lambda\rangle\rangle\text{.}

By Remark
4.19
and
(3)
of Remark
4.52
, we too have
(C13)λ
of Definition
4.44
.

Set

S:=\{(\vec{u},\vec{\alpha}):\ulcorner(\vec{u},\vec{\alpha})\in\dot{T}\urcorner\in\bigcup g\}

as one would naturally do. For each
i<\omega_{1}^{W}
, define a binary relation
\sim_{i}
on

\hat{\sigma}^{\prime}:=(\hat{\sigma}\cup\{\dot{n}:n<\omega\})\setminus\{\dot{I}\}

as follows:

\tau\sim_{i}\rho\text{ iff }\ulcorner\dot{N}_{i}\models\tau=\rho\urcorner\in\bigcup g\text{.}

Whenever
i<\omega_{1}^{W}
, let
\sim^{\prime}_{i}
be the equivalence closure of
\sim_{i}
, and

\displaystyle N_{i}:=\

\displaystyle\{[\tau]_{\sim^{\prime}_{i}}:\tau\in\hat{\sigma}^{\prime}\}

\displaystyle\ulcorner\in\urcorner^{\bar{N}_{i}}=\tilde{\in}_{i}:=\

\displaystyle\{([\tau]_{\sim^{\prime}_{i}},[\rho]_{\sim^{\prime}_{i}}):\tau,\rho\in\hat{\sigma}^{\prime}\text{ and }\ulcorner\dot{N}_{i}\models\tau\in\rho\urcorner\in\bigcup g\}

\displaystyle I_{i}:=\

\displaystyle\{[\tau]_{\sim^{\prime}_{i}}\in N_{i}:\ulcorner\dot{N}_{i}\models\dot{I}(\tau)\urcorner\in\bigcup g\}

\displaystyle\tau^{\bar{N}_{i}}:=\

\displaystyle[\tau]_{\sim^{\prime}_{i}}\text{ for every }\tau\in\hat{\sigma}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\bar{N}_{i}:=\

\displaystyle(N_{i};\tilde{\in}_{i},I_{i},\langle\tau^{\bar{N}_{i}}:\tau\in\hat{\sigma}^{\prime}\rangle)

\displaystyle e_{i}:\

\displaystyle\omega\longrightarrow N_{i}\ (n\mapsto[\dot{n}]_{\sim^{\prime}_{i}})\text{.}

Then
Th^{1}_{\mathcal{L}}(\bar{N}_{i},e_{i},i)
is well-defined for all
i<\omega_{1}^{W}
.

Proposition 4.58
.

For all

\varphi=\ulcorner\dot{N}_{i}\models\phi(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{k},\dot{n_{1}},\ldots,\dot{n_{l}},\dot{I},\dot{a},\dot{M}_{j_{1}},\ldots,\dot{M}_{j_{m}},\dot{\pi}_{q_{1}r_{1}},\ldots,\dot{\pi}_{q_{s}r_{s}},\dot{\vec{M}})\urcorner\in\mathcal{L}_{3}\text{,}

we have

\displaystyle\varphi\in\bigcup g\iff\bar{N}_{i}\models\phi[\dot{n}_{1}\mapsto e_{i}(n_{1}),\ldots,\dot{n}_{l}\mapsto e_{i}(n_{l})]\text{.}

Proof.

Fix
i<\omega_{1}^{W}
. It suffices to show

\phi\in Th^{1}_{\mathcal{L}}(\bar{N}_{i},e_{i},i)\iff\phi\in\bigcup g

for all
\phi\in\mathcal{L}^{i}_{3}
. We do this by induction on the length of
\phi
.

Case 1:

\phi=\ulcorner\dot{N}_{i}\models\tau=\rho\urcorner
for some
\tau,\rho\in\hat{\sigma}^{\prime}
. Then

\phi\in Th^{1}_{\mathcal{L}}(\bar{N}_{i},e_{i},i)\iff\phi\in\bigcup g

is implied by
\sim_{i}
being an equivalence relation, the latter of which holds because

•

\ulcorner\forall\tau\in\hat{\sigma}^{\prime}\ (E(\ulcorner\dot{N}_{i}\models\tau=\tau\urcorner))\urcorner
,

•

\ulcorner\forall\tau,\rho\in\hat{\sigma}^{\prime}\ (E(\ulcorner\dot{N}_{i}\models\tau=\rho\urcorner)\implies E(\ulcorner\dot{N}_{i}\models\rho=\tau\urcorner))\urcorner
, and

•

​
\begin{aligned} \ulcorner\forall\tau,\rho,\zeta\in\hat{\sigma}^{\prime}\ (&(E(\ulcorner\dot{N}_{i}\models\tau=\rho\urcorner)\wedge(E(\ulcorner\dot{N}_{i}\models\rho=\zeta\urcorner))\\
&\implies E(\ulcorner\dot{N}_{i}\models\tau=\zeta\urcorner))\urcorner\end{aligned}

are members of
\Gamma^{\prime}
.

Case 2:

\phi=\ulcorner\dot{N}_{i}\models\tau\in\rho\urcorner
for some
\tau,\rho\in\hat{\sigma}^{\prime}
. Then

\phi\in Th^{1}_{\mathcal{L}}(\bar{N}_{i},e_{i},i)\iff\phi\in\bigcup g

is implied by

\displaystyle\ulcorner\forall\tau,\rho,\zeta,\gamma\in\hat{\sigma}^{\prime}\ ((

\displaystyle E(\ulcorner\dot{N}_{i}\models\tau=\rho\urcorner)\wedge E(\ulcorner\dot{N}_{i}\models\zeta=\gamma\urcorner)

\displaystyle\wedge E(\ulcorner\dot{N}_{i}\models\tau\in\zeta\urcorner))\implies E(\ulcorner\dot{N}_{i}\models\rho\in\gamma\urcorner))\urcorner,

being a member of
\Gamma^{\prime}
.

Case 3:

\phi=\ulcorner\dot{N}_{i}\models\dot{I}(\tau)\urcorner
for some
\tau\in\hat{\sigma}^{\prime}
. Then

\phi\in Th^{1}_{\mathcal{L}}(\bar{N}_{i},e_{i},i)\iff\phi\in\bigcup g

is implied by

\displaystyle\ulcorner\forall\tau,\rho\in\hat{\sigma}^{\prime}\ (

\displaystyle(E(\ulcorner\dot{N}_{i}\models\tau=\rho\urcorner)\wedge E(\ulcorner\dot{N}_{i}\models\dot{I}(\tau)\urcorner))

\displaystyle\implies E(\ulcorner\dot{N}_{i}\models\dot{I}(\rho)\urcorner))\urcorner,

being a member of
\Gamma^{\prime}
.

Case 4:

\phi=\ulcorner\dot{N}_{i}\models\neg\psi\urcorner
for some
\psi
. Then by the induction hypothesis, we have

\phi^{\prime}\in Th^{1}_{\mathcal{L}}(\bar{N}_{i},e_{i},i)\iff\phi^{\prime}\in\bigcup g\text{,}

where

\displaystyle\phi^{\prime}:=\ulcorner\dot{N}_{i}\models\psi\urcorner\text{.}

This means

\displaystyle\phi\in Th^{1}_{\mathcal{L}}(\bar{N}_{i},e_{i},i)\iff\phi^{\prime}\not\in Th^{1}_{\mathcal{L}}(\bar{N}_{i},e_{i},i)\iff\phi^{\prime}\not\in\bigcup g\text{,}

and we are done if

\phi^{\prime}\not\in\bigcup g\iff\phi\in\bigcup g\text{.}

But this must hold because
\phi^{\prime}=\neg(\phi)
and
\bigcup g
is
\mathcal{L}\cap Q_{\lambda}
-nice.

Case 5:

\phi=\ulcorner\dot{N}_{i}\models\psi_{1}\wedge\psi_{2}\urcorner
for some
\psi_{1}
and
\psi_{2}
. Then by the induction hypothesis, we have

\displaystyle\phi^{\prime}\in Th^{1}_{\mathcal{L}}(\bar{N}_{i},e_{i},i)

\displaystyle\iff\phi^{\prime}\in\bigcup g\text{ and }

\displaystyle\phi^{\prime\prime}\in Th^{1}_{\mathcal{L}}(\bar{N}_{i},e_{i},i)

\displaystyle\iff\phi^{\prime\prime}\in\bigcup g\text{,}

where

\displaystyle\phi^{\prime}:=\

\displaystyle\ulcorner\dot{N}_{i}\models\psi_{1}\urcorner\text{ and }

\displaystyle\phi^{\prime\prime}:=\

\displaystyle\ulcorner\dot{N}_{i}\models\psi_{2}\urcorner.

This means

\displaystyle\phi\in Th^{1}_{\mathcal{L}}(\bar{N}_{i},e_{i},i)\iff\phi^{\prime},\phi^{\prime\prime}\in Th^{1}_{\mathcal{L}}(\bar{N}_{i},e_{i},i)\iff\phi^{\prime},\phi^{\prime\prime}\in\bigcup g,

and we are done if

\phi^{\prime},\phi^{\prime\prime}\in\bigcup g\iff\phi\in\bigcup g\text{.}

But this is implied by

\displaystyle\ulcorner(E(\phi)\iff(E(\phi^{\prime})\wedge E(\phi^{\prime\prime}))\urcorner

being a member of
\Gamma^{\prime}
.

Case 6:

\phi=\ulcorner\dot{N}_{i}\models\exists x\ \psi\urcorner
for some
\psi
. Then by the induction hypothesis, we have

\phi_{\tau}\in Th^{1}_{\mathcal{L}}(\bar{N}_{i},e_{i},i)\iff\phi_{\tau}\in\bigcup g\text{,}

for all
\tau<\hat{\sigma}^{\prime}
, where

\displaystyle\phi_{\tau}:=\ulcorner\dot{N}_{i}\models\psi[x\mapsto\tau]\urcorner\text{.}

This means

\displaystyle\phi\in Th^{1}_{\mathcal{L}}(\bar{N}_{i},e_{i},i)

\displaystyle\iff\exists\tau\in\hat{\sigma}^{\prime}\ (\phi_{\tau}\in Th^{1}_{\mathcal{L}}(\bar{N}_{i},e_{i},i))

\displaystyle\iff\exists\tau\in\hat{\sigma}^{\prime}\ (\phi_{\tau}\in\bigcup g)\text{,}

and we are done if

\exists\tau\in\hat{\sigma}^{\prime}\ (\phi_{\tau}\in\bigcup g)\iff\phi\in\bigcup g\text{.}

But this is implied by

\displaystyle\ulcorner E(\phi)\iff\exists\tau\in\hat{\sigma}^{\prime}\ (E(\phi_{\tau}))\urcorner

being a member of
\Gamma^{\prime}
. ∎

In the rest of the proof, we will apply Proposition
4.58
repeatedly and with great fervour. To minimise annoyance, these applications will be done implicitly as much as possible.

For every
i<\omega_{1}^{W}
and
\phi\in\mathsf{ZFC}^{*}+\mathsf{MA}(\omega_{1})
,

\ulcorner E(\ulcorner\dot{N}_{i}\models\phi\urcorner)\urcorner\in\Gamma^{\prime}\text{,}

so also

\bar{N}_{i}\models\mathsf{ZFC}^{*}+\mathsf{MA}(\omega_{1})\text{.}

Particularly,

\bar{N}_{i}\models``\text{Axiom of Extensionality}"

for
i<\omega_{1}^{W}
. In a similar vein,

\bar{N}_{i}\models``I_{i}\text{ is a normal uniform ideal on }\omega_{1}\text{ and }a_{i}\subset\omega_{1}"

because

\ulcorner E(\ulcorner\dot{N}_{i}\models``\dot{I}\text{ is a normal uniform ideal on }\omega_{1}\text{ and }\dot{a}\subset\omega_{1}"\urcorner)\urcorner\in\Gamma^{\prime}\text{,}

as
i
ranges over
\omega_{1}^{W}
. Since

•

\ulcorner\forall\xi<\omega_{1}^{W}\ (E(\ulcorner\dot{N}_{i}\models``\xi\text{ is an ordinal}"\urcorner))\urcorner
,

•

\ulcorner\forall\tau\in\hat{\sigma}^{\prime}\ \exists\xi<\omega_{1}^{W}\ (E(\ulcorner\dot{N}_{i}\models``\tau\text{ is an ordinal}"\urcorner)\implies E(\ulcorner\dot{N}_{i}\models\tau=\xi\urcorner))\urcorner
,

•

\ulcorner\forall\xi_{1}<\xi_{2}<\omega_{1}^{W}\ (E(\ulcorner\dot{N}_{i}\models``\xi_{2}\neq\emptyset"\urcorner)\implies E(\ulcorner\dot{N}_{i}\models\xi_{1}\in\xi_{2}\urcorner))\urcorner
, and

•

\ulcorner\forall\tau\in\hat{\sigma}^{\prime}\ \forall\xi_{1}<\omega_{1}^{W}\ \exists\xi_{2}<\xi_{1}\ (E(\ulcorner\dot{N}_{i}\models\tau\in\xi_{1}\urcorner)\implies E(\ulcorner\dot{N}_{i}\models\tau=\xi_{2}\urcorner))\urcorner

are members of
\Gamma^{\prime}
for each
i<\omega_{1}^{W}
, we also have the
\tilde{\in}_{i}
’s being well-founded. This means the
\bar{N}_{i}
’s are isomorphic to their respective (well-defined) Mostowski collapse, the latter of which shall be henceforth identified with the former. For
n<\omega
, the
e_{i}(n)
’s shall also be identified with their respective images under the Mostowski collapse function. As a consequence, for all
i<\omega_{1}^{W}
,

•

(N_{i};\tilde{\in}_{i}=\ \in)
is a transitive model of
\mathsf{ZFC}^{*}+\mathsf{MA}(\omega_{1})

•

ORD\cap N_{i}\in\omega_{1}^{W}+1
,

•

\xi^{\bar{N}_{i}}=\xi
for all
\xi\in\omega_{1}^{W}\cap N_{i}
,

•

\xi^{\bar{N}_{i}}=0
for all
\xi\in\omega_{1}^{W}\setminus N_{i}
.

That

\ulcorner\forall i<\omega_{1}^{W}\ \exists\xi<\omega_{1}^{W}\ (\xi\neq 0\wedge E(\ulcorner\dot{N}_{i}\models``\xi=\emptyset"\urcorner))\urcorner\in\Gamma^{\prime}

allows us to conclude
(C7)λ
of Definition
4.44
.

Now for each
i<\omega_{1}^{W}
,

•

\ulcorner\forall\tau\in\hat{\sigma}^{\prime}\ \exists n<\omega\ (E(\ulcorner\dot{N}_{i}\models\tau=\dot{n}\urcorner))\urcorner
, and

•

\ulcorner\forall m,n<\omega\ (m\neq n\implies E(\ulcorner\dot{N}_{i}\models\neg\ \dot{m}=\dot{n}\urcorner))\urcorner

being members of
\Gamma^{\prime}
tells us that
e_{i}
is a bijection from
\omega
into
N_{i}
. This settles
(C3)λ
of Definition
4.44
. Then

•

​
\begin{aligned} \ulcorner&\forall(\vec{u}_{1},\vec{\alpha}_{1}),(\vec{u}_{2},\vec{\alpha}_{2})\in{{}^{<\omega}{\omega}}\times{{}^{<\omega}{\omega_{2}^{W}}}\\
&((E(\ulcorner(\vec{u}_{1},\vec{\alpha}_{1})\in\dot{T}\urcorner)\wedge E(\ulcorner(\vec{u}_{2},\vec{\alpha}_{2})\in\dot{T}\urcorner))\\
&\mspace{5mu}\implies((\vec{u}_{1}\subset\vec{u}_{2}\wedge\vec{\alpha}_{1}\subset\vec{\alpha}_{2})\vee(\vec{u}_{2}\subset\vec{u}_{1}\wedge\vec{\alpha}_{2}\subset\vec{\alpha}_{1})))\urcorner\text{,}\end{aligned}

•

\ulcorner\forall n<\omega\ \exists(\vec{u},\vec{\alpha})\in{{}^{<\omega}{\omega}}\times{{}^{<\omega}{\omega_{2}^{W}}}\ (E(\ulcorner(\vec{u},\vec{\alpha})\in\dot{T}\urcorner)\wedge n\in dom(\vec{u}))\urcorner
, and

•

\ulcorner\forall(\vec{u},\vec{\alpha})\in{{}^{<\omega}{\omega}}\times{{}^{<\omega}{\omega_{2}^{W}}}\ (E(\ulcorner(\vec{u},\vec{\alpha})\in\dot{T}\urcorner)\implies(\vec{u},\vec{\alpha})\in T)\urcorner

being members of
\Gamma^{\prime}
, and

•

​
\begin{aligned} \ulcorner&\forall x\in\mathcal{L}^{0}_{0}\ \exists(\vec{u},\vec{\alpha})\in{{}^{<\omega}{\omega}}\times{{}^{<\omega}{\omega_{2}^{W}}}\\
&(E(x)\implies(Gd(x)\in ran(\vec{u})\wedge E(\ulcorner(\vec{u},\vec{\alpha})\in\dot{T}\urcorner)))\urcorner\text{, and}\end{aligned}

•

​
\begin{aligned} \ulcorner&\forall(\vec{u},\vec{\alpha})\in{{}^{<\omega}{\omega}}\times{{}^{<\omega}{\omega_{2}^{W}}}\ \forall n<\omega\ \exists x\in\mathcal{L}^{0}_{0}\\
&((n\in ran(\vec{u})\wedge E(\ulcorner(\vec{u},\vec{\alpha})\in\dot{T}\urcorner))\implies(E(x)\wedge Gd(x)=n))\urcorner\end{aligned}

being members of
\Gamma^{\prime}
, respectively give us
(C4)λ
and
(C5)λ
of Definition
4.44
. According to
(4.34.4)
and
(4.34.6)
,
(N_{0};\in,I_{0},a_{0})
must be a member of
\mathbb{P}_{max}
. In particular,
(N_{0};\in,I_{0})
is generically iterable.

Whenever
i\leq j<\omega_{1}^{W}
, define

\sigma_{ij}:=\{(e_{i}(m),e_{j}(n)):\ulcorner E(\ulcorner\dot{\sigma}_{ij}(\dot{m})=\dot{n}\urcorner)\urcorner\in\bigcup g\}\subset N_{i}\times N_{j}\text{.}

We see that
\langle N_{i},\sigma_{ij}:i\leq j<\omega_{1}^{W}\rangle
is a directed system as

•

\ulcorner\forall i\leq j<\omega_{1}^{W}\ \forall m<\omega\ \exists n<\omega\ (E(\ulcorner\dot{\sigma}_{ij}(\dot{m})=\dot{n}\urcorner))\urcorner
,

•

​
\begin{aligned} \ulcorner\forall i\leq j<\omega_{1}^{W}\ \forall m,n_{1},n_{2}<\omega\ (&(E(\ulcorner\dot{\sigma}_{ij}(\dot{m})=\dot{n_{1}}\urcorner)\wedge E(\ulcorner\dot{\sigma}_{ij}(\dot{m})=\dot{n_{2}}\urcorner))\\
&\implies n_{1}=n_{2})\urcorner\text{,}\end{aligned}

•

​
\begin{aligned} \ulcorner\forall i\leq j<\omega_{1}^{W}\ \forall m_{1},m_{2},n<\omega\ (&(E(\ulcorner\dot{\sigma}_{ij}(\dot{m_{1}})=\dot{n}\urcorner)\wedge E(\ulcorner\dot{\sigma}_{ij}(\dot{m_{2}})=\dot{n}\urcorner))\\
&\implies m_{1}=m_{2})\urcorner\text{, and}\end{aligned}

•

​
\begin{aligned} \ulcorner\forall i\leq j\leq k<\omega_{1}^{W}\ \forall l,m,n<\omega\ (&(E(\ulcorner\dot{\sigma}_{ij}(\dot{l})=\dot{m}\urcorner)\wedge E(\ulcorner\dot{\sigma}_{jk}(\dot{m})=\dot{n}\urcorner))\\
&\implies E(\ulcorner\dot{\sigma}_{ik}(\dot{l})=\dot{n}\urcorner))\urcorner\end{aligned}

are members of
\Gamma^{\prime}
. Furthermore, because

•

​

Lemma 4.59
.

The
\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}}
-
\Pi_{2}
sentence

\displaystyle\begin{split}\ulcorner&\forall x\in H(\lambda_{f})\ \forall U\in U(C,\lambda_{f})\ \forall i<\omega_{1}^{W}\ \forall m<\omega\ \exists\delta<\omega_{1}^{W}\ \exists n<\omega\ \exists\nu\in U\\
&(E(\ulcorner\dot{N}_{i}\models``\dot{m}\in\mathcal{P}(\omega_{1})\setminus\dot{I}"\urcorner)\implies(i<\delta\wedge E(\ulcorner\dot{\sigma}_{i(\delta+1)}(\dot{m})=\dot{n}\urcorner)\\
&\mspace{279mu}\wedge E(\ulcorner\dot{N}_{\delta+1}\models\delta\in\dot{n}\urcorner)\\
&\mspace{279mu}\wedge E(\ulcorner x\in\dot{X}_{\delta,\nu}\urcorner)))\urcorner\text{,}\end{split}

(4.4)

where
U(C,\lambda_{f})
is as in Definition
4.26
, is
(\mathfrak{A},\mathbb{P},\mathcal{L})
-universal.

Proof.

Fix arbitrary

•

,

•

,

•

,

, it suffices to assume

\ulcorner\dot{N}_{i}\models``\dot{m}\in\mathcal{P}(\omega_{1})\setminus\dot{I}"\urcorner\in p

(4.5)

and show that there are

•

i<\delta<\omega_{1}^{W}
,

•

n<\omega
, and

•

\nu\in U

for which

p\cup\{\ulcorner\dot{\sigma}_{i(\delta+1)}(\dot{m})=\dot{n}\urcorner,\ulcorner\dot{N}_{\delta+1}\models\delta\in\dot{n}\urcorner,\ulcorner x\in\dot{X}_{\delta,\nu}\urcorner\}\in\mathbb{P}\text{.}

Choose

•

\nu\in U
such that

and

(possible by
(P2)
), and

•

g\times f
a
\mathbb{P}_{\nu}\times Col(\omega,\nu)
-generic filter over

with

,

is a
\mathbb{P}_{\nu}
-generic filter over

and
|\nu|^{W[g\times f]}=\omega
. By Lemma
4.57
,

\mathfrak{D}(g)=\langle\langle\bar{M}_{i},\pi_{ij},\bar{N}_{i}=(N_{i};\in,\vec{X}),\sigma_{ij}:i\leq j\leq\omega_{1}^{W}\rangle,S,\langle e_{i}:i<\omega_{1}^{W}\rangle,\mathfrak{C}\rangle

is a
\nu
-certificate satisfying

\Sigma^{\prime}(\mathfrak{D}(g),\mathcal{L})=\bigcup g\text{.}

Set
\theta:=\omega_{1}^{W[g\times f]}
, and find a one-step extension

\mathfrak{I}_{1}=\langle(N_{i};\in,I_{i},a_{i}),\sigma_{ij}:i\leq j\leq\omega_{1}^{W}+1\rangle

of the generic iteration

\langle(N_{i};\in,I_{i},a_{i}),\sigma_{ij}:i\leq j\leq\omega_{1}^{W}\rangle

in
W[g\times f]
, where
(N_{\omega_{1}^{W}+1};\in,I_{\omega_{1}^{W}+1})
is the generic ultrapower of
(N_{\omega_{1}^{W}};\in,I_{\omega_{1}^{W}})
via a
N_{\omega_{1}^{W}}
-generic ultrafilter on
I_{\omega_{1}^{W}}
containing
\sigma_{i\omega_{1}^{W}}(e_{i}(m))
. The latter is possible by (
4.5
). As a result,
\omega_{1}^{W}\in N_{\omega_{1}^{W}+1}
and

(N_{\omega_{1}^{W}+1};\in,I_{\omega_{1}^{W}+1})\models\omega_{1}^{W}\in\sigma_{i(\omega_{1}^{W}+1)}(e_{i}(m))\text{.}

(4.6)

Still in
W[g\times f]
, let

be a generic iteration extending
\mathfrak{I}_{1}
,

•

, and

.

of Definition

,

. Indeed, let us stipulate that

\displaystyle\{\ulcorner\dot{\sigma}_{i(\omega_{1}^{W}+1)}(\dot{m})=\dot{n}\urcorner,\ulcorner\dot{N}_{\omega_{1}^{W}+1}\models\omega_{1}^{W}\in\dot{n}\urcorner,\ulcorner j(x)\in\dot{X}_{\omega_{1}^{W},j(\nu)}\urcorner\}

and

Appealing once more to

and the elementarity of

, we obtain

\displaystyle(W;\in)\models\

\displaystyle\exists\delta<\omega_{1}^{W}\ \exists n<\omega

\displaystyle(``p\cup\{\ulcorner\dot{\sigma}_{i(\delta+1)}(\dot{m})=\dot{n}\urcorner,\ulcorner\dot{N}_{\delta+1}\models\delta\in\dot{n}\urcorner,\ulcorner x\in\dot{X}_{\delta,\nu}\urcorner\}\in\mathbb{P}

\displaystyle\mspace{15mu}\text{and }i<\delta")\text{,}

and we are done.
∎

Lemma 4.60
.

Let
g
be
\mathbb{P}
-generic over
W
, and

\mathfrak{D}(g)=\langle\langle\bar{M}_{i},\pi_{ij},\bar{N}_{i}=(N_{i};\in,\vec{X}),\sigma_{ij}:i\leq j\leq\omega_{1}^{W}\rangle,S,\langle e_{i}:i<\omega_{1}^{W}\rangle,\mathfrak{C}\rangle\in W[g]\text{.}

Then every member of
\mathcal{P}(\omega_{1}^{W})\cap N_{\omega_{1}^{W}}\setminus I_{\omega_{1}^{W}}
is stationary in
W[g]
.

Proof.

Let

•

p\in\mathbb{P}
,

•

\dot{C}
be a
\mathbb{P}
-name such that
p\Vdash^{W}_{\mathbb{P}}``\dot{C}
is a club in
\omega_{1}^{W}"
,

•

D:=\{(q,\eta)\in\mathbb{P}\times\omega_{1}:q\Vdash^{W}_{\mathbb{P}}\eta\in\dot{C}\}
,

•

g
be a
\mathbb{P}
-generic filter over
W
with
p\in g
.

Applying (
\diamond
) with

•

\mathbb{P}
in place of
P
, and

•

D
in place of
B
,

we get

\displaystyle U:=\{\lambda\in C:(Q_{\lambda};\in,\mathbb{P},A_{\lambda})\prec(H(\lambda_{f});\in,\mathbb{P},D)\}

is stationary in
\lambda_{f}
, so
U\in U(C,\lambda_{f})
.

Move to
W[g]
. There, due to Lemma
4.57
,
\mathfrak{D}(g)
is a
\lambda_{f}
-certificate and

\Sigma^{\prime}(\mathfrak{D}(g),\mathcal{L})=\bigcup g\text{.}

Choose
Y\in\mathcal{P}(\omega_{1}^{W})\cap N_{\omega_{1}^{W}}\setminus I_{\omega_{1}^{W}}
. It suffices to show

has non-trivial intersection with
\dot{C}[g]
. To that end, note that there are

and

for which
\sigma_{i\omega_{1}^{W}}(e_{i}(m))=Y
. But this means

\ulcorner\dot{N}_{i}\models``\dot{m}\in\mathcal{P}(\omega_{1})\setminus\dot{I}"\urcorner\in\bigcup g\text{,}

so by Lemma
4.59
,

\{\ulcorner\dot{\sigma}_{i(\delta+1)}(\dot{m})=\dot{n}\urcorner,\ulcorner\dot{N}_{\delta+1}\models\delta\in\dot{n}\urcorner,\ulcorner x\in\dot{X}_{\delta,\nu}\urcorner\}\subset\bigcup g

for some
\delta<\omega_{1}^{W}
and

. Consequently,

\delta\in\sigma_{i(\delta+1)}(e_{i}(m))\subset Y\text{.}

Furnished with
(3)
of Remark
4.52
, the argument for
\mu\in\dot{C}[g]
in the proof of Lemma

can be reused with

•

\delta
in place of

,

•

(

) in place of (

),

•

in place of Lemma

, and

•

in place of Lemma

,

to net us
\delta\in\dot{C}[g]
. This completes the proof.
∎

One may view Theorem
4.41
as a souped-up version of Theorem
4.11
. By doing so, the next corollary naturally becomes a souped-up analogue of Corollary
4.30
.

Corollary 4.64
.

Assume

(i)

\Gamma=\bigcup_{1\leq k<\omega}\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{k})\cap L(\Gamma,\mathbb{R})
,

(ii)

\Gamma
is productive,

(iii)

\mathrm{NS}_{\omega_{1}}
is saturated,

(iv)

2^{\omega_{1}}=\mathbf{\delta^{1}_{2}}=\omega_{2}
, and

(v)

MA(\omega_{1})
holds.

Let

•

D\in L(\Gamma,\mathbb{R})
be a dense subset of
\mathbb{P}_{max}
,

•

A\subset\omega_{1}
be such that
\omega_{1}^{L[A]}=\omega_{1}
, and

•

\alpha>\omega_{2}
be an ordinal.

Then there is a stationary-preserving forcing notion
\mathbb{P}
such that in
V^{\mathbb{P}}
,

•

there is a generic iteration

\langle\bar{N}_{i}=(N_{i};\in,I_{i},a_{i}),\sigma_{ij}:i\leq j\leq\omega_{1}^{V}\rangle

satisfying

(1)

\bar{N}_{0}\in D^{*}:=(F^{*})"((F^{*})^{-1}(D)^{*})\subset\mathbb{P}_{max}
,

(2)

I_{\omega_{1}^{V}}=\mathrm{NS}_{\omega_{1}}^{V^{\mathbb{P}}}\cap N_{\omega_{1}^{V}}
, and

(3)

a_{\omega_{1}^{V}}=A
,

•

Nb_{0}(\alpha)
holds, and

•

Nb^{\prime}_{1}(\alpha)
— thus also
Nb_{1}(\alpha)
— holds if
\alpha
is a regular cardinal.

4.4
Open Questions

Working in the universe
W
as defined in the proof of Theorem
4.11
and thinning
C
if necessary, fix any
\theta
such that
[Q_{\lambda}]^{<\theta}\subset Q_{\lambda}
for all
\lambda\in C
. Should we then alter the definition of
\Gamma_{\lambda}
and
P_{\lambda}
for each
\lambda\in C\cup\{\lambda_{f}\}
as follows:

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\lambda}(\theta):=\

\displaystyle\text{the set }\Gamma_{\lambda}\text{ defined according to Definition \ref{defc},}

\displaystyle\text{but with }\mathbb{P}_{\lambda^{\prime}}(\theta)\text{ in place of the parameter }\mathbb{P}_{\lambda^{\prime}}\text{ for each }\lambda^{\prime}\in\lambda\cap C\text{,}

\displaystyle P_{\lambda}(\theta):=\

\displaystyle\{p\in[\mathcal{L}\cap Q_{\lambda}]^{<\theta}:

\displaystyle\ \Vdash_{Col(\omega,|H(\kappa)|)}\exists\Sigma\ (``\Sigma\ \Gamma_{\lambda}(\theta)(\mathcal{L}\cap Q_{\lambda},\mathfrak{A})\text{-certifies }p")\}\text{, and}

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}(\theta):=\

\displaystyle(P_{\lambda}(\theta),\supset)\text{,}

would all subsequent lemmas in the proof still go through? What notable forcing-theoretic properties can we use to differentiate among the
\mathbb{P}_{\lambda_{f}}(\theta)
’s that result from varying
\theta
?

Let
\theta
be a cardinal greater than
\omega
. Then the most obvious gap in the proof shows up in the semantic interpretation of members of
\Gamma_{\lambda}(\theta)
. More specifically, since there are many dense subsets
D
of many of the
\mathbb{P}_{\lambda^{\prime}}
’s for which

•

D
is definable without parameters, and

•

D
contains only infinite sets,

(S8)λ
no longer means

“if
X_{\delta,\lambda^{\prime}}
is non-empty, then for every dense subset
D
of
\mathbb{P}_{\lambda^{\prime}}
definable over
(Q_{\lambda^{\prime}};\in,\mathbb{P}_{\lambda^{\prime}},A_{\lambda^{\prime}})
with parameters from
X_{\delta,\lambda^{\prime}}
,

[\Sigma]^{<\omega}\cap X_{\delta,\lambda^{\prime}}\cap D\neq\emptyset\text{'',}

(4.7)

where

•

\Sigma
interprets the unary relation symbol
\ulcorner E\urcorner
occurring in
\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}}
(formulas), and

•

X_{\delta,\lambda^{\prime}}:=X_{\delta,\lambda^{\prime}}(\Sigma)
.

In fact, it now seems impossible to translate (
4.7
) into a set of
\mathcal{L}^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}}
-
\Pi_{2}
sentences, and there is thus an inability to guarantee that the
\mathbb{P}_{\lambda_{f}}(\theta)
-generic filter over
V
is sufficiently generic over each non-empty
X_{\delta,\lambda^{\prime}}
. This throws a wrench into the side condition method so crucial to the main results of this section.

But is there a way to salvage things to certain reasonable extent, without a complete overhaul of the forcing construction? To be more concrete, we can ask the following question.

Question 4.65
.

Let
W
and
\lambda_{f}
be as in the proof of Theorem
4.11
. In
W
, can
\theta
,
C
and

(C\longrightarrow\mathcal{P}(H(\lambda_{f})))[\lambda\mapsto Q_{\lambda}]

be chosen such that

(1)

\theta>\omega
is a cardinal,

(2)

[Q_{\lambda}]^{<\theta}\subset Q_{\lambda}
for all
\lambda\in C
,

(3)

(1’)
and
(2’)
of Lemma
4.12
are fulfilled with
\mathbb{P}_{\lambda_{f}}(\theta)
in place of
\mathbb{P}
, and

(4)

\mathbb{P}_{\lambda_{f}}(\theta)
does not collapse
\omega_{1}^{W}
?

In the spirit of Jensen’s results on the extended Namba problem, we are interested in the possibility of not having the forcing notion
\mathbb{P}=\mathbb{P}_{\lambda_{f}}
constructed in the proof of Theorem
4.11
add reals, under the right assumptions. More generally, we can ask the same about the parametrised versions of said forcing notion.

,

and

holds in

(and so also in

is an infinite cardinal,

(2)

for all

,

and

of Lemma
4.12
are fulfilled with
\mathbb{P}_{\lambda_{f}}(\theta)
in place of
\mathbb{P}
, and

(4)

\mathbb{P}_{\lambda_{f}}(\theta)
does not add reals?

Comparing Theorem
4.11
with Theorem

makes clear the existence of close relatives of Questions

and

, given the following definitions in the universe

, where

is as defined in the proof of Theorem

:

\displaystyle\Gamma^{\prime}_{\lambda}(\theta):=\

\displaystyle\text{the set }\Gamma^{\prime}_{\lambda}\text{ defined according to Definition \ref{def447},}

\displaystyle\text{but with }\mathbb{P}_{\lambda^{\prime}}(\theta)\text{ in place of the parameter }\mathbb{P}_{\lambda^{\prime}}\text{ for each }\lambda^{\prime}\in\lambda\cap C\text{,}

\displaystyle P^{\prime}_{\lambda}(\theta):=\

\displaystyle\{p\in[\mathcal{L}\cap Q_{\lambda}]^{<\theta}:

\displaystyle\ \Vdash_{Col(\omega,|H(\kappa)|)}\exists\Sigma\ (``\Sigma\ \Gamma^{\prime}_{\lambda}(\theta)(\mathcal{L}\cap Q_{\lambda},\mathfrak{A})\text{-certifies }p")\}\text{, and}

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{\prime}_{\lambda}(\theta):=\

\displaystyle(P_{\lambda}(\theta),\supset)\text{.}

In the likely event that the answer to Question
4.66
is in the negative, it makes sense to consider a more general question.

(Q1)

Must
Nb_{2}(\lambda)
hold for all
\lambda
above
\omega_{2}
, where

\displaystyle Nb_{2}(\lambda):=\text{`}

\displaystyle\text{there is a stationary-preserving forcing notion }\mathbb{P}\text{ such that}

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\text{ does not add reals,}

\displaystyle\Vdash_{\mathbb{P}}``cof(\alpha)=\omega"\text{ for all regular cardinals }\alpha\text{ satisfying }\omega_{2}\leq\alpha<\lambda\text{,}

\displaystyle\Vdash_{\mathbb{P}}``cof(\lambda)=\omega_{1}"\text{, and}

\displaystyle\Vdash_{\mathbb{P}}``cof(\beta)>\omega"\text{ for all regular cardinals }\beta\text{ satisfying }\lambda<\beta\text{'?}

As a consequence of Jensen’s work, we need only consider the case of
\lambda
being a weakly inaccessible cardinal without further qualification.

Question 4.69
.

Must
Nb_{2}(\lambda)
hold for a weakly inaccessible
\lambda
, if
\lambda
is not strongly inaccessible?

In another direction, we can ask about the possibility of eschewing the assumptions in Corollary
4.30
.

(Q2)

Is it true that
\mathsf{ZFC}\vdash``Nb_{1}(\lambda)
holds for all
\lambda
above
\omega_{2}
”?

Very recent results by De Bondt and Veličković (as a part of De Bondt’s PhD dissertation,
[
17
]
) resolved
(Q2)
in the affirmative. In fact, the class of forcing notions constructed by De Bondt and Veličković assuming only
\mathsf{ZFC}
, bears witness to a theorem significantly stronger than

“
Nb_{1}(\lambda)
holds for all
\lambda
above
\omega_{2}
”.

However, these forcing notions always add reals, so they cannot be used to answer Question
4.69
. Additionally, because the De Bondt-Veličković forcing constructions appear vastly different from the Asperó-Schindler one, there is no obvious way to integrate one kind with the other towards proving Theorem
4.41
. In fact, it is clear that iterating forcing notions born from these two kinds of constructions would not work, since an Asperó-Schindler forcing notion must force

•

\omega_{2}^{V}
to have cofinality
\omega
, and

•

\omega_{3}^{V}
to have cofinality
\omega_{1}
.

5
Theories with Constraints in Interpretation (TCIs) and their Models

In the previous section, we discussed a method of forcing the cofinality of regular cardinals within an interval to be
\omega
. The idea of changing cofinalities via forcing involves extracting a cofinal function from an existing relation
R
on a structured set
A
. We can couple the structure on
A
with
R
to form a new structure
\mathfrak{A}
that sets the context of the problem. Then a subset of
R
being a cofinal function becomes a definable property over
\mathfrak{A}
. Compare and contrast this with the notion of a first-order theory, which defines a property over nothing more than a vocabulary; it makes sense that the addition of a structure interpreting said vocabulary would allow us to define more intricate properties.

Essentially, a structure can be used to provide additional constraints to a first-order theory, and forcing-related questions can often be framed as consistency questions that ask about the existence of models of first-order theories satisfying such constraints. This section is dedicated to studying the aforementioned models, with a focus on their relationship with genericity.

5.1
Definitions and Basic Properties

We first make precise the notion of first-order theories with added constraints, so that we can compare these mathematical objects with the first-order theories we are familiar with.

Definition 5.1
.

A
first-order theory with constraints in interpretation
(
first-order TCI
) — henceforth, just
theory with constraints in interpretation
(
TCI
) — is a tuple
(T,\sigma,\dot{\mathcal{U}},\vartheta)
, where

•

T
is a first order theory with signature
\sigma
,

•

\dot{\mathcal{U}}
is a unary relation symbol not in
\sigma
,

•

\vartheta
is a function (the
constraint function
) with domain
\sigma\cup\{\dot{\mathcal{U}}\}
,

•

if
x\in ran(\vartheta)
, then there is
y
such that

\circ

either
x=(y,0)
or
x=(y,1)
, and

\circ

if
\vartheta(\dot{\mathcal{U}})=(z,a)
, then
y\subset z^{n}
for some
n<\omega
, and

•

if
\vartheta(\dot{\mathcal{U}})=(z,a)
, then

\circ

z\cap z^{n}=\emptyset
whenever
1<n<\omega
, and

\circ

z^{m}\cap z^{n}=\emptyset
whenever
1<m<n<\omega
.

Definition 5.2
.

Let
(T,\sigma,\dot{\mathcal{U}},\vartheta)
be a TCI. We say
\mathcal{M}:=(U;\mathcal{I})\models^{*}(T,\sigma,\dot{\mathcal{U}},\vartheta)
— or
\mathcal{M}

models

(T,\sigma,\dot{\mathcal{U}},\vartheta)
— iff all of the following holds:

•

\mathcal{M}
is a structure,

•

\sigma
is the signature of
\mathcal{M}
,

•

\mathcal{M}\models T
,

•

if
\vartheta(\dot{\mathcal{U}})=(y,0)
, then
U\subset y
,

•

if
\vartheta(\dot{\mathcal{U}})=(y,1)
, then
U=y
, and

•

for
\dot{X}\in\sigma
,

\circ

if
\dot{X}
is a constant symbol and
\vartheta(\dot{X})=(y,z)
, then
\mathcal{I}(\dot{X})\in y\cap U
,

\circ

if
\dot{X}
is a
n
-ary relation symbol and
\vartheta(\dot{X})=(y,0)
, then
\mathcal{I}(\dot{X})\subset y\cap U^{n}
,

\circ

if
\dot{X}
is a
n
-ary relation symbol and
\vartheta(\dot{X})=(y,1)
, then
\mathcal{I}(\dot{X})=y\cap U^{n}
,

\circ

if
\dot{X}
is a
n
-ary function symbol and
\vartheta(\dot{X})=(y,0)
, then

\{z\in U^{n+1}:\mathcal{I}(\dot{X})(z\!\restriction_{n})=z(n)\}\subset y\cap U^{n+1},\text{ and}

\circ

if
\dot{X}
is a
n
-ary function symbol and
\vartheta(\dot{X})=(y,1)
, then

\{z\in U^{n+1}:\mathcal{I}(\dot{X})(z\!\restriction_{n})=z(n)\}=y\cap U^{n+1}.

We say
(T,\sigma,\dot{\mathcal{U}},\vartheta)
has a model if there exists
\mathcal{M}
for which
\mathcal{M}\models^{*}(T,\sigma,\dot{\mathcal{U}},\vartheta)
.

Example 5.3
.

Let
T
be any first-order theory over the signature
\sigma
,
\dot{\mathcal{U}}
be a unary relation symbol not in
\sigma
, and
A
be any set. Define
\vartheta
on
\sigma\cup\{\dot{\mathcal{U}}\}
such that

•

\vartheta(\dot{\mathcal{U}})=(A,1)
,

•

\vartheta(\dot{X})=(A,0)
whenever
\dot{X}\in\sigma
is a constant symbol,

•

\vartheta(\dot{X})=(A^{n},0)
whenever
\dot{X}\in\sigma
is a
n
-ary relation symbol, and

•

\vartheta(\dot{X})=(A^{n+1},0)
whenever
\dot{X}\in\sigma
is a
n
-ary function symbol.

If we set
\mathfrak{T}:=(T,\sigma,\dot{\mathcal{U}},\vartheta)
, then the models of
\mathfrak{T}
are precisely the models of
T
with base set
A
.

Definition 5.4
.

Let
\mathfrak{A}
and
T
be a structure and a first-order theory respectively, over the same signature
\sigma
. Define

\mathrm{Sub}(\mathfrak{A},T):=\{\mathfrak{B}:\mathfrak{B}\text{ is a substructure of }\mathfrak{A}\text{ and }\mathfrak{B}\models T\}.

Members of
\mathrm{Sub}(\mathfrak{A},T)
are called
T
-substructures of
\mathfrak{A}
.

Example 5.5
.

Let
T
be any first-order theory over the signature
\sigma
, and
\mathfrak{A}=(A;\mathcal{I})
be a structure interpreting
\sigma
. Define
\vartheta
on
\sigma\cup\{\dot{\mathcal{U}}\}
such that

•

\vartheta(\dot{\mathcal{U}})=(A,0)
,

•

\vartheta(\dot{X})=(\{\mathcal{I}(\dot{X})\},1)
whenever
\dot{X}\in\sigma
is a constant symbol,

•

\vartheta(\dot{X})=(\mathcal{I}(\dot{X}),1)
whenever
\dot{X}\in\sigma
is a
n
-ary relation symbol, and

•

\vartheta(\dot{X})=(R_{\mathcal{I}}(\dot{X}),1)
whenever
\dot{X}\in\sigma
is a
n
-ary function symbol, where

R_{\mathcal{I}}(\dot{X}):=\{z\in A^{n+1}:\mathcal{I}(\dot{X})(z\!\restriction_{n})=z(n)\}.

If we set
\mathfrak{T}:=(T,\sigma,\dot{\mathcal{U}},\vartheta)
, then

\{\text{models of }\mathfrak{T}\}=\mathrm{Sub}(\mathfrak{A},T).

In practice, we can view forcing as a technique to refine structures that provably exist in
V
. Often, such refinements cannot be carried out in
V
, for any successful attempt would result in objects that cannot exist in
V
. In each of these cases, forcing can be used to extend
V
to include an instance of the refined structure. The way we define TCIs allows them to specify — and act as blueprints for — refinements of this ilk. If
\mathfrak{T}
is a TCI specifying a particular refinement, then models of
\mathfrak{T}
correspond to the results of said refinement. We hope the next example can help illustrate our aforementioned idea of specification.

Example 5.6
.

Let
\dot{\mathcal{U}}
be a unary relation and
\dot{R}
be a binary relation. Define
\vartheta
on
\{\dot{\mathcal{U}},\dot{R}\}
such that

•

\vartheta(\dot{\mathcal{U}})=(\omega_{1},1)
, and

•

\vartheta(\dot{R})=(\omega_{1}\times\omega,0)
.

Set
T
to contain exactly the sentences

\displaystyle\ulcorner\forall x\ \exists y\ (\dot{R}(x,y))\urcorner\text{,}

\displaystyle\ulcorner\forall x\ \forall y\ \forall z\ ((\dot{R}(x,y)\wedge\dot{R}(x,z))\implies y=z)\urcorner\text{ and}

\displaystyle\ulcorner\forall x\ \forall y\ \forall z\ ((\dot{R}(x,z)\wedge\dot{R}(y,z))\implies x=y)\urcorner\text{.}

Now
\mathfrak{T}:=(T,\{\dot{R}\},\dot{\mathcal{U}},\vartheta)
is a TCI that specifies a refinement of the structure

\mathfrak{A}:=(\omega_{1};\{(\dot{R},\omega_{1}\times\omega)\})

to some

\mathfrak{A}^{\prime}:=(\omega_{1};\{(\dot{R},F)\}),

where
F\subset\omega_{1}\times\omega
is an injection from
\omega_{1}
into
\omega
. As an implication,
\mathfrak{T}
must not have any model in
V
. However, a weak outer model of
V
in which
\omega_{1}^{V}
is collapsed to
\omega
necessarily contains models of
\mathfrak{T}
.

Example
5.6
reminds us that the existence of models for a TCI is not absolute between
V
and its (weak) outer models. There is thus a fundamental difference between the model existence of a TCI and that of a first-order theory. This should reflect in our definition of what it means for a TCI to be consistent.

Definition 5.7
.

A TCI
(T,\sigma,\dot{\mathcal{U}},\vartheta)
is
consistent
iff
(T,\sigma,\dot{\mathcal{U}},\vartheta)
has a model in some outer model of
V
.

Remark 5.8
.

It might seem at first glance, that the the consistency of a TCI is not a first-order property in the language of set theory, since it involves quantifying over outer models of
V
. This is not a real problem, as we shall see in the next subsection, because said definition is equivalent to a first-order property at the metalevel.

Definition 5.9
.

A TCI
(T,\sigma,\dot{\mathcal{U}},\vartheta)
is
finitely consistent
iff for all finite
T^{\prime}\subset T
,
(T^{\prime},\sigma,\dot{\mathcal{U}},\vartheta)
is consistent.

Definition 5.10
.

A TCI
(T,\sigma,\dot{\mathcal{U}},\vartheta)
is
\Pi_{n}
iff
T
contains only
\Pi_{n}
sentences.

A TCI
(T,\sigma,\dot{\mathcal{U}},\vartheta)
is
\Sigma_{n}
iff
T
contains only
\Sigma_{n}
sentences.

A TCI
(T,\sigma,\dot{\mathcal{U}},\vartheta)
is
\Sigma_{n}\cup\Pi_{n}
iff every sentence in
T
is either
\Sigma_{n}
or
\Pi_{n}
.

TCIs allow natural constraints that are not first-order definable to be imposed on the models of a theory. However, they are not a “true” generalisation of first-order theories because their models have uppers bounds in size. In fact, we can show that the size limitation of models of TCIs is in some sense, the only shortcoming of TCIs
vis-a-vis
first-order theories.

Lemma 5.11
.

Let
T
be a first-order theory over the signature
\sigma
, and
\dot{\mathcal{U}}
be a unary relation symbol not in
\sigma
. For every cardinal
\kappa
, there is a TCI
\mathfrak{T}
such that

•

\mathfrak{T}=(T,\sigma,\dot{\mathcal{U}},\vartheta)
for some
\vartheta
, and

•

every model
\mathfrak{A}=(A;\mathcal{I})
of
T
with
|A|\leq\kappa
is isomorphic to some model of
\mathfrak{T}
.

Proof.

Define

•

\vartheta(\dot{\mathcal{U}}):=(\kappa,0)
,

•

\vartheta(\dot{X}):=(\kappa,0)
if
\dot{X}
is a constant symbol,

•

\vartheta(\dot{X}):=(\kappa^{n},0)
if
\dot{X}
is a
n
-ary relation symbol,

•

\vartheta(\dot{X}):=(\kappa^{n+1},0)
if
\dot{X}
is a
n
-ary function symbol.

Then

\mathfrak{T}:=(T,\sigma,\dot{\mathcal{U}},\vartheta)

is as required.
∎

It turns out that there is an analogue of the downward Lowenheim-Skolem theorem for TCIs.

Lemma 5.12
.

Let

•

\mathfrak{T}=(T,\sigma,\dot{\mathcal{U}},\vartheta)
be a TCI,

•

y
be a set, and

•

\alpha
be an infinite ordinal.

Assume that
\vartheta(\dot{\mathcal{U}})=(y,0)
and in some weak outer model
W
of
V
, there is a pair
(\mathcal{M},f)
such that

•

\mathcal{M}=(U;\mathcal{I})\models^{*}\mathfrak{T}
, and

•

f:\alpha\longrightarrow U
is a bijection.

Then for every
\beta
with
\omega\leq\beta\leq\alpha
, there is a pair
(\mathcal{M}^{\prime},f^{\prime})
in
W
such that

•

\mathcal{M}^{\prime}=(U^{\prime};\mathcal{I}^{\prime})\models^{*}\mathfrak{T}
, and

•

f^{\prime}:\beta\longrightarrow U^{\prime}
is a bijection.

Proof.

Let
\beta
be such that
\omega\leq\beta\leq\alpha
. By the downward Lowenheim-Skolem theorem applied to
\mathcal{M}
in
W
, there exists a structure
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}:=(U^{\prime};\mathcal{I}^{\prime})
for which
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\prec\mathcal{M}
and
|U^{\prime}|=|\beta|
. This means
\mathcal{M^{\prime}}\models T
. Further,
U^{\prime}\subset U\subset y
and whenever
\dot{X}\in\sigma
is a constant symbol,
\mathcal{I}(\dot{X})=\mathcal{I}^{\prime}(\dot{X})\in U^{\prime}
. The other criteria for
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\models^{*}\mathfrak{T}
are easy to check. Fix
f^{\prime}
to be any bijection from
\beta
into
U^{\prime}
, and we are done.
∎

If we allow movement among outer models of
V
, we get the following (somewhat trivial) version of the general Lowenheim-Skolem theorem for TCIs.

Lemma 5.13
.

Let
\mathfrak{T}=(T,\sigma,\dot{\mathcal{U}},\vartheta)
be a TCI with an infinite model in some outer model of
V
. Then for every infinite ordinal
\beta
, there is a pair
(\mathcal{M},f)
in some outer model of
V
such that

•

\mathcal{M}=(U;\mathcal{I})\models^{*}\mathfrak{T}
, and

•

f:\beta\longrightarrow U
is a bijection.

Proof.

By our assumptions on
\mathfrak{T}
, it has a model
\mathcal{M}=(U,\mathcal{I})
in some outer model
W
of
V
, such that
U
is infinite. Let
g
be
Col(\omega,|U\cup\beta|^{W})
-generic over
W
. In
W[g]
,
\mathcal{M}
is still a model of
\mathfrak{T}
; moreover, both
U
and
\beta
are countably infinite, so there is a bijection
f
from
\beta
into
U
. Obviously,
W[g]
is an outer model of
V
, so
(\mathcal{M},f)
is as required.
∎

On the other hand, we have no good analogue of the compactness theorem for TCIs. Indeed, there are simple examples in which compactness fails. We give one such example below.

Lemma 5.14
.

There is a
\Sigma_{1}\cup\Pi_{1}
TCI
\mathfrak{T}:=(T,\{\dot{R}\},\dot{\mathcal{U}},\vartheta)
with a countable transitive closure, such that

•

\dot{R}
is binary relation symbol,

•

if
x\in ran(\vartheta)
, then
x=(y,0)
for some set
y
, and

•

\mathfrak{T}
is finitely consistent but not consistent.

Proof.

Choose
\dot{R}
and
\dot{\mathcal{U}}
to be relation symbols of their appropriate arity in
H(\omega)
. We define
\vartheta
on
\{\dot{\mathcal{U}},\dot{R}\}
as follows:

\displaystyle\vartheta(\dot{\mathcal{U}}):=\

\displaystyle(\omega,0)

\displaystyle S_{n}:=\

\displaystyle\{(k,l):2^{n}\leq k,l<2^{n}+n\text{ and }k<l\}

\displaystyle S:=\

\displaystyle\bigcup\{S_{n}:n<\omega\}

\displaystyle\vartheta(\dot{R}):=\

\displaystyle(S,0).

Here,
\vartheta
encodes a set of disjoint finite linear orders of unbounded lengths. Quite clearly,
\mathfrak{T}
has a countable transitive closure, as all first-order sentences over the signature
\{\dot{R}\}
are members of
H(\omega)
. Next, we want
T
to contain the first-order definition of a strict linear ordering, namely the conjunction of the three sentences (properties):

\displaystyle\varphi_{1}\text{ (irreflexivity)}:\

\displaystyle\ulcorner\forall x\ (\neg\dot{R}(x,x))\urcorner

\displaystyle\varphi_{2}\text{ (transitivity)}:\

\displaystyle\ulcorner\forall x\ \forall y\ \forall z\ ((\dot{R}(x,y)\wedge\dot{R}(y,z))\implies\dot{R}(x,z))\urcorner

\displaystyle\varphi_{3}\text{ (trichotomy)}:\

\displaystyle\ulcorner\forall x\ \forall y\ (\dot{R}(x,y)\vee\dot{R}(y,x)\vee y=x)\urcorner.

Finally, we define

T^{\prime}:=\{\ulcorner\exists x_{1}\ \exists x_{2}\ \dots\ \exists x_{n}\ (\bigwedge_{1\leq k<n}\dot{R}(x_{k},x_{k+1}))\urcorner:1<n<\omega\}

and let

T:=T^{\prime}\cup\{\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2},\varphi_{3}\}.

Note that any finite subset of
T
can be satisfied by a sufficiently long finite linear order, examples of which
\vartheta
provides in abundance. However, a model of
T
is necessarily an infinite linear order, and our definition of
\vartheta
precisely prohibits all infinite linear orders. We thus have
\mathfrak{T}
being finitely consistent but not consistent.
∎

Fix any infinite set
X
. By the Lowenheim-Skolem theorem for first-order logic, the compactness theorem for first-order logic holds even if we require the base set of the models in question to be subsets of
X
. As a result, the failure of compactness in a TCI of the form specified by Lemma
5.14
must come from restrictions imposed by
\vartheta
. In a sense, then, Lemma
5.14
gives one of the simplest examples of such a
\vartheta
, considering it has a singleton as its domain.

Definition 5.15
.

Given a TCI
\mathfrak{T}
and any
\mathcal{M}
, we say
\mathcal{M}
is a
finitely determined model of

\mathfrak{T}
iff
\mathcal{M}\models^{*}\mathfrak{T}
and for some quantifier-free sentence
\varphi
in the language associated with
\mathcal{M}
,

\displaystyle\forall W\ \forall\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\ (

\displaystyle(W\text{ is an outer model of }V\text{ and }\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\in W\text{ and }\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\models^{*}\mathfrak{T}\text{ and }\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\models\varphi)

\displaystyle\implies\mathcal{M}^{\prime}=\mathcal{M}).

In this case,
\mathcal{M}
is
finitely determined by

\varphi
.

Naturally, all finite models of any TCI are finitely determined. As it turns out, if a TCI is consistent, then all its finitely determined models can be read off a forcing notion associated with it. We will prove this in the next subsection.

We end this subsection with a technical fact.

Fact 5.16
.

Let
\mathfrak{T}
be a TCI in
V
. If
\mathcal{M}\models^{*}\mathfrak{T}
in an outer model of
V
, then there is a smallest transitive model
W
of
\mathsf{ZFC}
such that
V\subset W
and
\mathcal{M}\in W
. We use
V[\mathcal{M}]
to denote this
W
.

5.2
Forcing Extensions and Models of TCIs

In this subsection and the next one, we investigate how one could “force” the existence of models of TCIs, under different restrictions and in a variety of settings. As a starting point, we would like to frame the problem of constructing models of TCIs in the context of Section
3
, just so we can utilise Lemma
3.56
, among other things.

Lemma 5.17
.

There is a formula
\psi_{cert}
in two free variables, such that in any model of
\mathsf{ZFC}
,

•

\psi_{cert}(\mathfrak{T},(\mathfrak{A}_{\mathfrak{T}},\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}},\Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}}))
defines a function

\mathfrak{T}\mapsto(\mathfrak{A}_{\mathfrak{T}},\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}},\Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}})

on the class of all TCIs, wherewith

\circ

\mathfrak{A}_{\mathfrak{T}}=(H(|trcl(\mathfrak{T})|^{+});\in)
,

\circ

\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}}
is a set closed under negation,

\circ

\mathfrak{A}_{\mathfrak{T}}
is
\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}}
-suitable, and

\circ

\Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}}
a set of
({\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}}})^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}_{\mathfrak{T}}}
sentences, and

•

whenever

\circ

\mathfrak{T}=(T,\sigma,\dot{\mathcal{U}},\vartheta)
,

\circ

\psi_{cert}(\mathfrak{T},(\mathfrak{A}_{\mathfrak{T}},\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}},\Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}}))
, and

\circ

T
contains only
\Pi_{2}
sentences,

\Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}}
must contain only
({\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}}})^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}_{\mathfrak{T}}}
-
\Pi_{2}
sentences.

Proof.

Fix any TCI
\mathfrak{T}=(T,\sigma,\dot{\mathcal{U}},\vartheta)
. We will constructively define the tuple
(\mathfrak{A}_{\mathfrak{T}},\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}},\Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}})
based on
\mathfrak{T}
alone, and in the process, check that the requirements of the lemma are satisfied.

Of course, we have to set

\mathfrak{A}_{\mathfrak{T}}:=(H(|trcl(\mathfrak{T})|^{+});\in).

Note that
\mathfrak{A}_{\mathfrak{T}}\models\mathsf{ZFC-Powerset}
, so
\mathfrak{A}_{\mathfrak{T}}
is a transitive model of a sufficiently strong set theory. Next, let

\displaystyle\sigma^{\prime}:=\

\displaystyle\sigma\cup\{\dot{\mathcal{U}}\}\text{, and}

\displaystyle U:=\

\displaystyle\text{the unique }y\text{ for which there exists }z\text{ such that }\vartheta(\dot{\mathcal{U}})=(y,z).

For
\dot{X}\in\sigma^{\prime}
, define
\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}}(\dot{X})
as follows:

•

if
\dot{X}
is a constant symbol and
\vartheta(\dot{X})=(y,z)
, then

\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}}(\dot{X}):=\{\ulcorner\dot{X}=x\urcorner:x\in y\cap U\},

•

if
\dot{X}
is a
n
-ary relation symbol and
\vartheta(\dot{X})=(y,z)
, then

\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}}(\dot{X}):=\{\ulcorner\dot{X}(x)\urcorner:x\in y\cap U^{n}\},\text{ and}

•

if
\dot{X}
is a
n
-ary function symbol and
\vartheta(\dot{X})=(y,z)
, then

\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}}(\dot{X}):=\{\ulcorner\dot{X}(x\!\restriction_{n})=x(n)\urcorner:x\in y\cap U^{n+1}\}.

Then

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{T}}:=\

\displaystyle\bigcup\{\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}}(\dot{X}):\dot{X}\in\sigma^{\prime}\}\text{, and}

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}}:=\

\displaystyle\text{the closure of }\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{T}}\text{ under negation.}

Obviously,
\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}}
is both a member and a subset of
H(|trcl(\mathfrak{T})|^{+})
, so it is definable in the language associated with
H(|trcl(\mathfrak{T})|^{+})
. We thus have that
\mathfrak{A}_{\mathfrak{T}}
is
\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}}
-suitable.

Before we get to
\Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}}
, a remark (or rather, a reminder) is imperative.

Remark 5.18
.

In the same vein as what was elaborated after Definition
4.13
, we will use functions to pass parameters of an expression in
\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}}
via variables, whenever necessary in the construction of
({\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}}})^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}_{\mathfrak{T}}}
sentences involving the symbol
\ulcorner E\urcorner
. In fact, this can be done uniformly by the universal function
\chi_{\mathfrak{T}}
:

\displaystyle(S,x_{1},\dots,x_{n},x_{n+1})\mapsto\begin{cases}\ulcorner S(x_{1},\dots,x_{n},x_{n+1})\urcorner&\text{if }S\text{ is a }n+1\text{-ary relation symbol}\\
\ulcorner S(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})=x_{n+1}\urcorner&\text{if }S\text{ is a }n\text{-ary function symbol }\\
\ulcorner S=x_{n+1}\urcorner&\text{if }S\text{ is a }\text{constant symbol and }n=0\\
\emptyset&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}

which is defined in
V
by a
\Delta_{0}
formula in the language of set theory (as per Definition
2.7
).

As in the case of the proof of Lemma
4.11
, we will abuse notation and abbreviate the use of
\chi_{\mathfrak{T}}
with straightforward substitutions of variables for parameters in the writing of
({\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}}})^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}_{\mathfrak{T}}}
sentences. There are no intrinsic “hidden costs” in terms of complexity to such a presentation of
({\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}}})^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}_{\mathfrak{T}}}
sentences.

Now, we define
\Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}}
as follows:

(1)

For each constant symbol
\dot{X}\in\sigma^{\prime}
,

\displaystyle\ulcorner\exists x\ (E(\ulcorner\dot{\mathcal{U}}(x)\urcorner)\wedge E(\ulcorner\dot{X}=x\urcorner))\urcorner

\displaystyle\in\Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}},

\displaystyle\ulcorner\forall x\ \forall y\ ((E(\ulcorner\dot{X}=x\urcorner)\wedge E(\ulcorner\dot{X}=y\urcorner))\implies x=y)\urcorner

\displaystyle\in\Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}}.

(2)

For each
n
-ary relation symbol
\dot{X}\in\sigma^{\prime}
,

\ulcorner\forall x_{1}\dots\forall x_{n}\ (E(\ulcorner\dot{X}((x_{1},\dots,x_{n}))\urcorner)\implies(\bigwedge_{1\leq k\leq n}E(\ulcorner\dot{\mathcal{U}}(x_{k})\urcorner)))\urcorner\in\Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}}.

(3)

If
\vartheta(\dot{\mathcal{U}})=(y,1)
, then

\ulcorner\forall x\ (x\in y\implies E(\ulcorner\dot{\mathcal{U}}(x)\urcorner))\urcorner\in\Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}}.

(4)

For each
n
-ary relation symbol
\dot{X}\in\sigma
such that
\vartheta(\dot{X})=(y,1)
,

\displaystyle\ulcorner\forall x_{1}\dots\forall x_{n}\ (((\bigwedge_{1\leq k\leq n}E(\ulcorner\dot{\mathcal{U}}(x_{k})\urcorner))\wedge\ulcorner\dot{X}((x_{1},\dots,x_{n}))\urcorner\in\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}})

\displaystyle\implies E(\ulcorner\dot{X}((x_{1},\dots,x_{n}))\urcorner))\urcorner

\displaystyle\in\Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}}.

(5)

For each
n
-ary function symbol
\dot{X}\in\sigma^{\prime}
,

\displaystyle\ulcorner\forall x_{1}\dots\forall x_{n+1}\ (

\displaystyle E(\ulcorner\dot{X}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})=x_{n+1}\urcorner)

\displaystyle\mspace{90mu}\implies(\bigwedge_{1\leq k\leq n+1}E(\ulcorner\dot{\mathcal{U}}(x_{k})\urcorner)))\urcorner\in\Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}},

\displaystyle\ulcorner\forall x_{1}\dots\forall x_{n}\ \exists y\ (

\displaystyle(\bigwedge_{1\leq k\leq n}E(\ulcorner\dot{\mathcal{U}}(x_{k})\urcorner))

\displaystyle\mspace{70mu}\implies E(\ulcorner\dot{X}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})=y\urcorner))\urcorner\in\Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}},

\displaystyle\ulcorner\forall x_{1}\dots\forall x_{n}\ \forall y\ \forall z\ (

\displaystyle(E(\ulcorner\dot{X}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})=y\urcorner)

\displaystyle\wedge E(\ulcorner\dot{X}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})=z\urcorner))\implies y=z)\urcorner\in\Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}}.

(6)

For each
n
-ary function symbol
\dot{X}\in\sigma^{\prime}
such that
\vartheta(\dot{X})=(y,1)
,

\displaystyle\ulcorner\forall x_{1}\dots\forall x_{n+1}\ (((\bigwedge_{1\leq k\leq n+1}E(\ulcorner\dot{\mathcal{U}}(x_{k})\urcorner))\wedge\ulcorner\dot{X}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})=x_{n+1}\urcorner\in\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}})

\displaystyle\implies E(\ulcorner\dot{X}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})=x_{n+1}\urcorner))\urcorner

\displaystyle\in\Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}}.

(7)

We finally deal with members of
T
. So let
\phi\in T
. We first assume that for every atomic subformula
\varphi
of
\phi
,

\displaystyle\varphi\text{ contains no more than one symbol from }\sigma\text{ (counting recurrences).}

(5.1)

To see why this assumption can be made without loss of generality, notice that there are canonical algorithms
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
such that, when given any atomic formula
\varphi^{\prime}
over
\sigma
as input,

•

M_{1}
returns a
\Sigma_{1}
formula
\varphi
which is logically equivalent to
\varphi^{\prime}
and satisfies (
5.1
), and

•

M_{2}
returns a
\Pi_{1}
formula
\varphi
which is logically equivalent to
\varphi^{\prime}
and satisfies (
5.1
).

Consequently, by

(i)

replacing atomic subformulas of
\phi
via
M_{1}
or
M_{2}
according to their parities, and then

(ii)

canonically converting the result of
(7)(iii)
to prenex normal form,

we can obtain a sentence that

•

is logically equivalent to
\phi
,

•

has each of its atomic subformulas
\varphi
satisfy (
5.1
), and

•

is
\Pi_{2}
whenever
\phi
is
\Pi_{2}
.

Next, we transform
\phi
into
\phi^{\prime}
by first inductively relativising
\phi
to “members of
\dot{\mathcal{U}}
”, then simultaneously translating all its atomic subformulas to correspond to membership in
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{T}}
. In more detail, we carry out the procedure below.

(a)

Cast
\phi
as a string.

Given any string
A
, we can view
A
as a sequence of (possibly non-distinct) characters. The ordering of this sequence gives rise to the notion of (relative)
position
. Intuitively, the leftmost character of
A
marks its first position (position
=1
), and for any
k
, the character at the (
k+1
)-th position of
A
necessarily lies to the immediate right of the character at position
k
. Therefore, the positions of
A
must range from
1
to the length of
A
.

(b)

Initialise a pointer
p
at the first position of the
\phi
.

The rationale of having
p
is to help us traverse the characters of
\phi
as we modify it. Like any pointer,
p
occupies exactly one position at any point in time. Specifically, we want
p
to keep moving rightwards, even though
\phi
as we now know it might change in length over the run of this procedure.

We will modify
\phi
in steps, each step being a pass of a numbered stage in the enumeration of this procedure. For clarity of exposition, it is useful to distinguish
\phi
pre- and post-modification. As we describe the procedure going forward, we shall let
\phi
refer to the unaltered string: its state right after
(7)(b)
. At any particular step,
the current frame
denotes the modified form of
\phi
at the beginning of said step.

(c)

Let
x
be the current position occupied by
p
. If there is
\varphi
such that

•

\varphi
is a subformula of
\phi
,

•

the leading character of
\varphi
is a first-order quantifier, and

•

\varphi
is a substring of the current frame starting at position
x
,

then we let
\varphi^{\prime}
be the shortest such string, and proceed according to the cases below. Otherwise, skip to
(7)(b)
.

Case 1:

\varphi^{\prime}=\ulcorner\forall x\ \psi\urcorner
for some
x
and
\psi
. Then we replace
\varphi^{\prime}
starting at
x
of the current frame with the string

\ulcorner\forall x\ (E(\ulcorner\dot{\mathcal{U}}(x)\urcorner)\implies\psi)\urcorner.

Case 2:

\varphi^{\prime}=\ulcorner\exists x\ \psi\urcorner
for some
x
and
\psi
. Then we replace
\varphi^{\prime}
starting at
x
of the current frame with the string

\ulcorner\exists x\ (E(\ulcorner\dot{\mathcal{U}}(x)\urcorner)\wedge\psi)\urcorner.

When we speak of replacing a substring
Y
starting at
x
of
F
with another string
Z
, we mean to produce the concatenated string
A^{\frown}Z^{\frown}B
, where
A
and
B
are the two unique strings for which

•

either
A
is empty or the last character of
A
occupies position
x-1
of
F
, and

•

F=A^{\frown}Y^{\frown}B
.

After the replacement, the position occupied by
p
remains unchanged — it should still be at
x
relative to
A^{\frown}Z^{\frown}B
. Note that replacements of this kind make no changes to
F
at any position less than (to the left of)
x
.

(d)

If
p
is not at the rightmost position of the current frame, increment the position it occupies by
1
. Otherwise skip to
(7)(b)
.

(e)

Go to
(7)(b)
.

(f)

Substitute each atomic subformula
\psi
of
\phi
occurring in the current frame with
\ulcorner E(\psi)\urcorner
, bearing in mind the abbreviations adopted in Remark
5.18
. These substitutions can be done simultaneously because it is impossible to have two distinct substitutable instances occupy overlapping positions of the current frame.

The aforementioned procedure produces a sentence
\phi^{\prime}\in({\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}}})^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}_{\mathfrak{T}}}
sharing the
quantification structure
of
\phi
. More precisely, this means the existence of a string
A
such that

•

A
contains only quantifiers,

•

A
is a subsequence of both
\phi
and
\phi^{\prime}
,

•

if
B
is a subsequence of
\phi
containing only quantifiers, then
B
is a subsequence of
A
, and

•

if
B^{\prime}
is a subsequence of
\phi^{\prime}
containing only quantifiers, then
B^{\prime}
is a subsequence of
A
.

Now, convert
\phi^{\prime}
to a logically equivalent formula
\phi^{*}
in prenex normal form, through an application of the standard conversion algorithm. This algorithm preserves the quantification structure of
\phi^{\prime}
— so that
\phi^{*}
and
\phi
have the same quantification structure — whenever
\phi
is in prenex normal form.

Enforce that
\phi^{*}\in\Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}}
.

(8)

Nothing else is in
\Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}}
.

Assume
T
contains only
\Pi_{2}
sentences. Then necessarily every member of
T
is in prenex normal form. As a consequence, the transformation

\varpi:\phi\mapsto\phi^{*}

described in
(7)
takes every member of
T
to a
({\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}}})^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}_{\mathfrak{T}}}
sentence in prenex normal form with the same quantification structure, making
\varpi"T
a set of
({\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}}})^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}_{\mathfrak{T}}}
-
\Pi_{2}
sentences. Clearly, all additions to
\Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}}
as per
(1)
to
(6)
are
({\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}}})^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}_{\mathfrak{T}}}
-
\Pi_{2}
sentences. By
(8)
,
\Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}}
contains only
({\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}}})^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}_{\mathfrak{T}}}
-
\Pi_{2}
sentences.
∎

Fix
\psi_{cert}
to be as in Lemma
5.17
. We are then justified in our next definition.

Definition 5.19
.

Let
\mathfrak{T}=(T,\sigma,\dot{\mathcal{U}},\vartheta)
be a TCI. Define

\displaystyle\mathfrak{A}_{\mathfrak{T}}:=\

\displaystyle\text{the unique }\mathfrak{A}\text{ for which there are }\mathcal{L}\text{ and }\Gamma\text{ satisfying }\psi_{cert}(\mathfrak{T},(\mathfrak{A},\mathcal{L},\Gamma))\text{,}

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}}:=\

\displaystyle\text{the unique }\mathcal{L}\text{ for which there are }\mathfrak{A}\text{ and }\Gamma\text{ satisfying }\psi_{cert}(\mathfrak{T},(\mathfrak{A},\mathcal{L},\Gamma))\text{, and}

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}}:=\
