\displaystyle\text{the unique }\Gamma\text{ for which there are }\mathfrak{A}\text{ and }\mathcal{L}\text{ satisfying }\psi_{cert}(\mathfrak{T},(\mathfrak{A},\mathcal{L},\Gamma))\text{.}

Definition 5.20
.

Let
\mathfrak{T}=(T,\sigma,\dot{\mathcal{U}},\vartheta)
be a TCI. Define

\displaystyle P(\mathfrak{T}):=\

\displaystyle\{p\in[\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}}]^{<\omega}:\ \Vdash_{Col(\omega,|trcl(\mathfrak{A}_{\mathfrak{T}})|)}\exists\Sigma\ (``\Sigma\ \Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}},\mathfrak{A}_{\mathfrak{T}})\text{-certifies }p")\}\text{,}

\displaystyle\leq_{\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})}:=\

\displaystyle\{(p,q)\in P(\mathfrak{T})\times P(\mathfrak{T}):q\subset p\}\text{, and}

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T}):=\

\displaystyle(P(\mathfrak{T}),\leq_{\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})})\text{.}

By Lemma
5.17
, if
\mathfrak{T}
is a TCI and
A
the base set of
\mathfrak{A}_{\mathfrak{T}}
, then

•

\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})\in A\cap\mathcal{P}(A)
, so
\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})
is definable in the language associated with
\mathfrak{A}_{\mathfrak{T}}
, and

•

(\mathfrak{A}_{\mathfrak{T}},\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T}))
is good for
\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}}
.

By Lemmas
3.59
and
5.17
, the definition of
\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})
from
\mathfrak{T}
is absolute for transitive models of
\mathsf{ZFC}
.

At this juncture, it is customary for us to revisit the main forcing construction of the previous section.

Remark 5.21
.

Consider the sequence of forcing notions

\{\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}:\lambda\in C\cup\{\lambda_{f}\}\}

constructed within
W
in the proof of Theorem
4.11
. The adaptation of this inductive construction to the language of TCIs is straightforward: given
\lambda\in C\cup\{\lambda_{f}\}
and
\vec{\mathbb{P}}_{\lambda}:=\{\mathbb{P}_{\theta}:\theta\in\lambda\cap C\}
, define
\sigma
to contain

(A)

the variable names of all subsets of
H(\lambda_{f})
germane to the definition of
\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}
in the original proof (this set includes
\vec{\mathbb{P}}_{\lambda}
), along with

(B)

two other ternary relation symbols,
\dot{F}
and
\dot{X}
,

and nothing else. If
\dot{Z}\in\sigma
is of type
(A)
, then we interpret
\dot{Z}
invariably as whichever set it is defined to be in the original proof; for example, when
\dot{Z}=\ulcorner C\urcorner
, we set
\vartheta(\dot{Z})=(C,1)
. On the other hand, if
\dot{Z}\in\sigma
is of type
(B)
, then we interpret
\dot{Z}
according to either
(L1)λ
or
(L2)λ
based on the identity of
\dot{Z}
; for example, when
\dot{Z}=\dot{F}
, we set

\vartheta(\dot{Z})=(\{(i,n,\alpha):i\in R\cap\lambda,n<\omega\text{ and }\alpha<i\},0).

Also set
\vartheta(\dot{\mathcal{U}})=(H(\lambda_{f}),1)
.

Next, modify
(S1)λ
to
(S8)λ
such that each subformula of the form
\ulcorner E(\ulcorner\phi\urcorner)\urcorner
is replaced by
\ulcorner\phi\urcorner
, and let
T
contain only these formulas (noting and adjusting for the abuse of notation in the original presentation). Finally, letting
\mathfrak{T}_{\lambda}=(T,\sigma,\dot{\mathcal{U}},\vartheta)
, it takes no more than a routine unfurling and checking of definitions to ascertain that
\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T}_{\lambda})
is isomorphic to the forcing notion
\mathbb{P}_{\lambda}
defined in the original proof.

We see in Remark
5.21
that our procedure of associating a partial order with each TCI can be used to generate forcing notions as complex as the ones constructed to solve a difficult problem in set theory. More formal declarations of the power of this procedure will appear — and be proven — in the later parts of this section. But before that, let us return to the setting of the ground.

Lemma 5.22
.

There is a formula
\psi_{trans}
in three free variables, absolute for transitive models of
\mathsf{ZFC-Powerset}
, such that
\psi_{trans}(\mathfrak{T},\mathcal{M},\Sigma)
defines a bijection from

\{\mathcal{M}:\mathcal{M}\models^{*}\mathfrak{T}\}

into

\{\Sigma:\Sigma\ \Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}},\mathfrak{A}_{\mathfrak{T}})\text{-certifies }\emptyset\}

for every fixed TCI
\mathfrak{T}=(T,\sigma,\dot{\mathcal{U}},\vartheta)
.

Proof.

Let
U
be the unique
y
for which there exists
z
such that
\vartheta(\dot{\mathcal{U}})=(y,z)
. Given a model
\mathcal{M}=(M;\mathcal{I})
of
\mathfrak{T}
, define

U(\mathcal{M}):=\{\ulcorner\dot{\mathcal{U}}(x)\urcorner:x\in M\}\cup\{\ulcorner\neg\ \dot{\mathcal{U}}(x)\urcorner:x\in U\setminus M\}.

Now define
\psi_{trans}
as follows:

\psi_{trans}(\mathfrak{T},\mathcal{M},\Sigma)\iff(\mathcal{M}\models^{*}\mathfrak{T}\wedge\Sigma=(U(\mathcal{M})\cup Diag(\mathcal{M}))\cap\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}}),

where
Diag(\mathcal{M})
is the atomic diagram of
\mathcal{M}
. Verily,
\psi_{trans}
is a
\Delta_{1}
formula in the language of set theory (according to Definition
2.7
), because the binary relation
\models^{*}
is
\Delta_{1}
-definable and the set
\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}}
is
\Delta_{1}
-definable in
\mathfrak{T}
. As such,
\psi_{trans}
must be absolute for transitive models of
\mathsf{ZFC-Powerset}
. We can then straightforwardly check that
\psi_{trans}
defines a bijection as required by the lemma for any fixed
\mathfrak{T}
, based on how the triple
(\mathfrak{A}_{\mathfrak{T}},\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}},\Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}})
is constructed from
\mathfrak{T}
.
∎

Remark 5.23
.

For any TCI
\mathfrak{T}
and any structure
\mathfrak{A}
, if
\mathfrak{A}
is
\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}}
-suitable and
\Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}}
is a set of
(\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}})^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}}
sentences, then for all
\Sigma
and
p
,

\Sigma\ \Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}},\mathfrak{A}_{\mathfrak{T}})\text{-certifies }p\iff\Sigma\ \Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}},\mathfrak{A})\text{-certifies }p.

We can therefore replace
\mathfrak{A}_{\mathfrak{T}}
in Lemma
5.22
with any appropriate
\mathfrak{A}
and still have the lemma hold true for the same
\psi_{trans}
.

Fix
\psi_{trans}
to be as in Lemma
5.22
for the next definition.

Definition 5.24
.

Let
\mathfrak{T}=(T,\sigma,\dot{\mathcal{U}},\vartheta)
be a TCI in
V
and
\mathcal{M}
be a model of
\mathfrak{T}
in some outer model of
V
. Define

\displaystyle\Sigma(\mathfrak{T},\mathcal{M}):=\text{the unique }\Sigma\text{ for which }\psi_{trans}(\mathfrak{T},\mathcal{M},\Sigma).

It is time to cash the cheque issued in Remark
5.8
.

Lemma 5.25
.

Let
\mathfrak{T}=(T,\sigma,\dot{\mathcal{U}},\vartheta)
be a TCI. Then
\mathfrak{T}
is consistent iff

\Vdash_{Col(\omega,\lambda)}\exists\Sigma\ (``\Sigma\ \Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}},\mathfrak{A}_{\mathfrak{T}})\text{-certifies }\emptyset")

iff

\Vdash_{Col(\omega,\lambda)}\exists\mathcal{M}\ (``\mathcal{M}\models^{*}\mathfrak{T}"),

where
\lambda\geq|H(|trcl(\mathfrak{T})|^{+})|
.

Proof.

By Lemmas
5.17
and
5.22
, we can find a triple
(\mathfrak{A}_{\mathfrak{T}},\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}},\Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}})
such that

•

\mathfrak{A}_{\mathfrak{T}}=(H(|trcl(\mathfrak{T})|^{+});\in)
,

•

\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}}
is a set closed under negation,

•

\mathfrak{A}_{\mathfrak{T}}
is
\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}}
-suitable,

•

\Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}}
a set of
({\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}}})^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}_{\mathfrak{T}}}
sentences, and

•

\mathfrak{T}
is consistent iff for some outer model
W
of
V
,

\exists\Sigma\in W\ (``\Sigma\ \Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}},\mathfrak{A}_{\mathfrak{T}})\text{-certifies }\emptyset").

Then the conjunction of

•

Lemma
3.55
,

•

the fact that every forcing extension of
V
is an outer model of
V
, and

•

the fact that every outer model of
V
is a weak outer model of
V

tells us that
\mathfrak{T}
is consistent iff

\Vdash_{Col(\omega,\lambda)}\exists\Sigma\ (``\Sigma\ \Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}},\mathfrak{A}_{\mathfrak{T}})\text{-certifies }\emptyset"),

where
\lambda\geq|H(|trcl(\mathfrak{T})|^{+})|
.

As every
Col(\omega,\lambda)
-generic extension of
V
is a transitive model of
\mathsf{ZFC-Powerset}
, we can apply Lemma
5.22
again to complete the proof.
∎

Corollary 5.26
.

There is a procedure to uniformly decide in
V
, whether any given
\Pi_{2}
TCI is consistent.

Proof.

By Lemma
5.25
, a
\Pi_{2}
TCI
\mathfrak{T}
is consistent iff
P(\mathfrak{T})
is non-empty.
∎

Intuitively, the consistency of a theory — however it is defined — should be absolute in a sufficiently strong sense. This is the case for first-order theories, any of which consistency is absolute for transitive models of set theory. The following Lemma establishes a similar absoluteness property with regards to the consistency of a TCI.

Lemma 5.27
.

Let
\mathfrak{T}=(T,\sigma,\dot{\mathcal{U}},\vartheta)
be a TCI. Then
\mathfrak{T}
being consistent is absolute for transitive models of
\mathsf{ZFC}
sharing the same ordinals.

Proof.

This is very much similar to the proof of Lemma
3.59
. Nevertheless, we shall provide details.

Let
V^{\prime}
and
W
be transitive models of
\mathsf{ZFC}
with
ORD^{V^{\prime}}=ORD^{W}
and
\mathfrak{T}\in V^{\prime}\subset W
. If
\mathfrak{T}
is consistent in
W
, then
\mathfrak{T}
has a model in some outer model of
W
. Said outer model is also an outer model of
V^{\prime}
, so
\mathfrak{T}
is consistent in
V^{\prime}
as well.

Now assume
\mathfrak{T}
is consistent in
V^{\prime}
. Letting

\lambda:=|H(((|trcl(\mathfrak{T})|^{V^{\prime}})^{+})^{V^{\prime}})^{V^{\prime}}|^{V^{\prime}},

Lemma
5.25
gives us

\Vdash_{Col(\omega,\lambda)}\exists\mathcal{M}\ (``\mathcal{M}\models^{*}\mathfrak{T}")

in
V^{\prime}
. Note that

\mathbb{P}:=Col(\omega,\lambda)^{V^{\prime}}

remains a forcing notion in
W
, so consider
g
a
\mathbb{P}
-generic filter over
W
. Necessarily,
g
is also
\mathbb{P}
-generic over
V^{\prime}
, and further,
V^{\prime}[g]\subset W[g]
. In
V^{\prime}[g]
,
\mathfrak{T}
is forced to have a model — call it
\mathcal{M}
. Being a model of
\mathfrak{T}
is absolute for transitive models of
\mathsf{ZFC}
, so
\mathcal{M}\models^{*}\mathfrak{T}
holds in
W[g]
too. Since
W[g]
is an outer model of
W
,
\mathfrak{T}
must be consistent in
W
.
∎

We now define a class of generic objects that manifest as models of TCIs.

Definition 5.28
.

Let
\mathfrak{T}
be a consistent
\Pi_{2}
TCI.

If
\mathfrak{A}
and
\mathbb{P}
are such that
\mathbb{P}
is definable in the language associated with
\mathfrak{A}
and
(\mathfrak{A},\mathbb{P})
is good for
\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}}
, then a
(\mathbb{P},\mathfrak{A})
-generic model
of
\mathfrak{T}
is a model
\mathcal{M}
of
\mathfrak{T}
satisfying

\Sigma(\mathfrak{T},\mathcal{M})=(\bigcup g)\cap\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}}

for some
\mathbb{P}
-generic filter
g
over
\mathfrak{A}
. In this case, we say
g

witnesses

\mathcal{M}

is a

(\mathbb{P},\mathfrak{A})
-
generic model of

\mathfrak{T}
. We say
g

witnesses

a

(\mathbb{P},\mathfrak{A})
-
generic model of

\mathfrak{T}
iff for some
\mathcal{M}
,
g
witnesses
\mathcal{M}
is a
(\mathbb{P},\mathfrak{A})
-generic model of
\mathfrak{T}
.

We call
\mathcal{M}
a
\mathfrak{A}
-generic model
of
\mathfrak{T}
iff for some
\mathbb{P}
definable in the language associated with
\mathfrak{A}
such that
(\mathfrak{A},\mathbb{P})
is good for
\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}}
,
\mathcal{M}
is a
(\mathbb{P},\mathfrak{A})
-generic model of
\mathfrak{T}
.

We call
\mathcal{M}
a
generic model
of
\mathfrak{T}
iff for some
\mathfrak{A}
and
\mathbb{P}
such that
\mathbb{P}
is definable in the language associated with
\mathfrak{A}
and
(\mathfrak{A},\mathbb{P})
is good for
\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}}
,
\mathcal{M}
is a
(\mathbb{P},\mathfrak{A})
-generic model of
\mathfrak{T}
.

Observation 5.29
.

(1)

If
\mathfrak{T}
is a consistent
\Pi_{2}
TCI, and
\mathfrak{A}
and
\mathbb{P}
are such that
\mathbb{P}
is definable in the language associated with
\mathfrak{A}
and
(\mathfrak{A},\mathbb{P})
is good for
\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}}
, then every
(\mathbb{P},\mathfrak{A})
-generic model of
\mathfrak{T}
is a
(\mathbb{P},\mathfrak{A})
-generic object.

(2)

If
g
witnesses
\mathcal{M}
is a
(\mathbb{P},V)
-generic model of
\mathfrak{T}
, and
\bigcup g\subset\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}}
, then
V[g]=V[\mathcal{M}]
.

(3)

In the same vein as Observation
2.26
, we see that given any consistent
\Pi_{2}
TCI
\mathfrak{T}
,

\displaystyle\forall x\ (

\displaystyle x\text{ is a }(\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T}),\mathfrak{A}_{\mathfrak{T}})\text{-generic model of }\mathfrak{T}

\displaystyle\iff x\text{ is a }(\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T}),V)\text{-generic model of }\mathfrak{T})

in every outer model of
V
. As a result, we can thus safely talk about
(\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T}),V)
-generic models of
\mathfrak{T}
without the need to quantify over all sets.

Our definition of generic models might seem overly restrictive at first glance. The next lemma provides justification that it is not so.

Lemma 5.30
.

Let
\mathfrak{T}
be a TCI. If
\mathcal{M}
is a model of
\mathfrak{T}
in some forcing extension of
V
, then
\mathcal{M}
is a
V
-generic model of
\mathfrak{T}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a model of
\mathfrak{T}
in a forcing extension
W
of
V
. Without loss of generality, we can assume the existence of
\mathbb{P}=(P,\leq_{\mathbb{P}})
and
\dot{\Sigma}
such that

•

\mathbb{P}
is a forcing notion,

•

P\cap\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}}=\emptyset
,

•

\dot{\Sigma}
is a
\mathbb{P}
-name,

•

\Vdash_{\mathbb{P}}``\exists\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\ (\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\models^{*}\mathfrak{T}\text{ and }\Sigma(\mathfrak{T},\mathcal{M}^{\prime})=\dot{\Sigma})"
, and

•

for some
\mathbb{P}
-generic filter
g_{0}
over
V
,
\Sigma(\mathfrak{T},\mathcal{M})=\dot{\Sigma}[g_{0}]
.

Define

\displaystyle P^{*}:=\{x\in[P\cup\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}}]^{<\omega}:\

\displaystyle x\cap P\text{ has a common extension in }\mathbb{P}\text{ and}

\displaystyle\forall y\ \exists p\ (y\in x\cap\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}}\implies(p\in x\cap P\text{ and }p\Vdash_{\mathbb{P}}y\in\dot{\Sigma}))\}

and have
\mathbb{P}^{*}:=(P^{*},\supset)
.

Fix
x\in P^{*}
, and let
p
be a common extension in
\mathbb{P}
of the members of
x\cap P
. Then any extension of
\{p\}
in
\mathbb{P}^{*}
is compatible with
x
in
\mathbb{P}^{*}
. This means that

(\pi:P\longrightarrow P^{*})\ [p\mapsto\{p\}]

is a dense weak embedding from
\mathbb{P}
into
\mathbb{P}^{*}
. As a result,

\sigma:g\mapsto\mathrm{UC}(w(\mathbb{P}^{*}),\pi"g)

is a bijection from

\{g:g\text{ is a }\mathbb{P}\text{-generic filter over }V\}\cap W

into

\{h:h\text{ is a }\mathbb{P}^{*}\text{-generic filter over }V\}\cap W

with inverse

\tau:h\mapsto\pi^{-1}h\text{,}

in every weak outer model
W
of
V
. The following fact is easy to see.

Fact 5.31
.

If
h
is a
\mathbb{P}^{*}
-generic filter over
V
, then
[(\bigcup h)\cap P]^{<\omega}\subset h
.

Proposition 5.32
.

Let
g
be a
\mathbb{P}
-generic filter over
V
. Then
(\bigcup\sigma(g))\cap P=g
.

Proof.

Denote
(\bigcup\sigma(g))\cap P
as
g^{\prime}
. By the definitions of
\pi
and
\sigma
,
g\subset g^{\prime}
clearly. Choose
p\in g^{\prime}
. Since
\sigma(g)
is a
\mathbb{P}^{*}
-generic filter over
V
, Fact
5.31
tells us that
\{p\}\in\sigma(g)
. As
\tau=\sigma^{-1}
,
p\in g
, and we are done.
∎

Proposition 5.33
.

Let
g
be a
\mathbb{P}
-generic filter over
V
. Then

(\bigcup\sigma(g))\cap\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}}=\{y\in\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}}:\exists p\ (p\in(\bigcup\sigma(g))\cap P\text{ and }p\Vdash_{\mathbb{P}}^{V}y\in\dot{\Sigma})\}.

Proof.

Denote
(\bigcup\sigma(g))\cap\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}}
as
\Sigma
and

\{y\in\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}}:\exists p\ (p\in(\bigcup\sigma(g))\cap P\text{ and }p\Vdash_{\mathbb{P}}^{V}y\in\dot{\Sigma})\}

as
\Sigma^{\prime}
. By the definition of
P^{*}
and the fact that
\sigma(g)\subset P^{*}
,
\Sigma\subset\Sigma^{\prime}
. Choose
y\in\Sigma^{\prime}
, so that there is
p\in(\bigcup\sigma(g))\cap P
with
p\Vdash_{\mathbb{P}}^{V}y\in\dot{\Sigma}
. But that entails the density of

D_{p}:=\{x\in P^{*}:y\in x\}

below
\{p\}
in
\mathbb{P}^{*}
. Since
\{p\}\in\sigma(g)
by Fact
5.31
,
D_{p}\cap\sigma(g)\neq\emptyset
, whence
y\in\Sigma
.
∎

Combining Propositions
5.32
and
5.33
, we know that whenever
g
is a
\mathbb{P}
-generic filter over
V
,
(\bigcup\sigma(g))\cap\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}}=\dot{\Sigma}[g]
. In particular,
(\bigcup\sigma(g_{0}))\cap\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}}=\Sigma(\mathfrak{T},\mathcal{M})
. As
\sigma(g_{0})
is a
\mathbb{P}^{*}
-generic filter over
V
,
\mathcal{M}
is a
(\mathbb{P}^{*},V)
-generic model of
\mathfrak{T}
.
∎

Henceforth, we will look more closely examine the relationship between genericity and TCIs, while looking into the extent to which generic models of
\Pi_{2}
TCIs are abundant.

First, we link the concept of TCIs and their models back to forcing notions and their generic extensions. The relation
\lessdot
on forcing notions can be used to define a partial order on the class of all TCIs via the map

\hat{\mathbb{P}}:\mathfrak{T}\mapsto\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})\text{.}

(5.2)

Given two TCIs
\mathfrak{T}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{T}_{2}
, let
\mathfrak{T}_{1}\trianglelefteq\mathfrak{T}_{2}
whenever
\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T}_{1})\lessdot\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T}_{2})
. The relation
\trianglelefteq
is a preordering of TCIs because of Fact
2.40
. Have
\mathfrak{T}_{1}\sim_{T}\mathfrak{T}_{2}
iff
\mathfrak{T}_{1}\trianglelefteq\mathfrak{T}_{2}
and
\mathfrak{T}_{2}\trianglelefteq\mathfrak{T}_{1}
. Then
\sim_{T}
is an equivalence relation on TCIs. Denoting
\sim_{P}
to be the forcing equivalence relation on forcing notions, we can easily verify that
\trianglelefteq/\sim_{T}
is a partial order isomorphic to a suborder of
\lessdot/\sim_{P}
, as witnessed by the map

\tilde{\mathbb{P}}:[\mathfrak{T}]\mapsto[\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})]\text{.}

(5.3)

The next two theorems — the main ones of this subsection — hint at a strong connection between
\Pi_{2}
TCIs and forcing extensions.

Theorem 5.34
.

Let
\mathbb{P}=(P,\leq_{\mathbb{P}})
be a partial order. Then there is a consistent
\Pi_{2}
TCI
\mathfrak{T}=(T,\sigma,\dot{\mathcal{U}},\vartheta)
such that

•

a dense weak embedding exists from
\mathbb{P}
into
\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})
, and

•

for a fixed unary relation symbol
\dot{X}\in\sigma
, every model
\mathcal{M}
of
\mathfrak{T}
in any outer model of
V
satisfies

``\{p:\mathcal{M}\models\dot{X}(p)\}\text{ is a }\mathbb{P}\text{-generic filter over }V"\text{.}

Proof.

Choose
\dot{\mathcal{U}}
,
\dot{\leq}
and
\dot{G}
and to be distinct relation symbols of arities
1
,
2
and
1
respectively. For each dense subset
D
of
\mathbb{P}
, choose a fresh unary relation symbol
\dot{D}
. Set
\sigma
to be

\{\dot{\leq},\dot{G}\}\cup\{\dot{D}:D\text{ is a dense subset of }\mathbb{P}\}\text{.}

We define
\vartheta
on
\{\dot{\mathcal{U}}\}\cup\sigma
as follows:

\displaystyle\vartheta(\dot{\mathcal{U}}):=\

\displaystyle(P,1)

\displaystyle\vartheta(\dot{\leq}):=\

\displaystyle(\leq_{\mathbb{P}},1)

\displaystyle\vartheta(\dot{G}):=\

\displaystyle(P,0)

\displaystyle\vartheta(\dot{D}):=\

\displaystyle(D,1)\text{ for each dense subset }D\text{ of }\mathbb{P}\text{.}

Now, have
T
contain only the sentences

\displaystyle\ulcorner\forall p\ \forall q\ \exists r\ ((\dot{G}(p)\wedge\dot{G}(q))\implies(\dot{G}(r)\wedge\dot{\leq}(r,p)\wedge\dot{\leq}(r,q)))\urcorner\text{,}

\displaystyle\ulcorner\forall p\ \forall q\ ((\dot{\leq}(p,q)\wedge\dot{G}(p))\implies\dot{G}(q))\urcorner\text{, as well as all members of}

\displaystyle\{\ulcorner\exists p\ (\dot{G}(p)\wedge\dot{D}(p))\urcorner:D\text{ is a dense subset of }\mathbb{P}\}\text{.}

Let
\mathfrak{T}:=(T,\sigma,\dot{\mathcal{U}},\vartheta)
. Then
\mathfrak{T}
is clearly a consistent
\Pi_{2}
TCI, for any
\mathbb{P}
-generic filter over
V
is an interpretation of
\dot{G}
satisfying
\mathfrak{T}
. Moreover, it is obvious from our definition of
\mathfrak{T}
that whenever
\mathcal{M}\models^{*}\mathfrak{T}
, the set
G(\mathcal{M}):=\{p:\mathcal{M}\models\dot{X}(p)\}
is a
\mathbb{P}
-generic filter over
V
. We are left to show the existence of a dense weak embedding from
\mathbb{P}
into
\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})
. Toward that end we note:

(1)

for each
p\in P
,
\{\ulcorner\dot{G}(p)\urcorner\}\in P(\mathfrak{T})
, and

(2)

for each
x\in P(\mathfrak{T})
,

\displaystyle\exists p\ \forall q\ (x\cup\{\ulcorner\dot{G}(p)\urcorner\}\in P(\mathfrak{T})

\displaystyle\wedge(\ulcorner\dot{G}(q)\urcorner\in x\implies p\leq_{\mathbb{P}}q))\text{.}

Define
\pi:P\longrightarrow P(\mathfrak{T})
to be

p\mapsto\{\ulcorner\dot{G}(p)\urcorner\},

which is possible by
(1)
. We argue that
\pi
is a dense weak embedding from
\mathbb{P}
into
\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})
.

Denote
w(\leq_{\mathbb{P}})
as
\leq^{\dagger}
,
w(\mathbb{P})
as
\mathbb{P}^{\dagger}
,
w(\leq_{\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})})
as
\leq^{*}
, and
w(\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T}))
as
\mathbb{P}^{*}
.

We first show that
\pi
is a weak embedding. Assume
p\leq^{\dagger}q
and let
x\leq_{\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})}\pi(p)
. Choose any model
\mathcal{M}
of
\mathfrak{T}
in some outer model of
V
such that
x\subset\Sigma(\mathfrak{T},\mathcal{M})
. Then
G(\mathcal{M})
is a
\mathbb{P}
-generic filter over
V
containing
p
, implying
q\in G(\mathcal{M})
.
\mathcal{M}
thus witnesses
x\cup\pi(q)\in P(\mathfrak{T})
, so
x\not\!\!\bot_{\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})}\ \pi(q)
. We thus have
\pi(p)\leq^{*}\pi(q)
. Next, assume
p\not\leq^{\dagger}q
. Then there is
r\leq_{\mathbb{P}}p
such that
r\ \bot_{\mathbb{P}}\ q
. This means
(\pi(p)\cup\pi(r))\ \bot_{\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})}\ \pi(q)
, and
\pi(p)\not\leq^{*}\pi(q)
. Lastly, the observation that

\displaystyle\pi(p)\ \bot_{\mathbb{P}^{*}}\ \pi(q)\iff\pi(p)\ \bot_{\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})}\ \pi(q)\text{ and}

\displaystyle p\ \bot_{\mathbb{P}^{\dagger}}\ q\iff p\ \bot_{\mathbb{P}}\ q

guarantees

p\ \bot_{\mathbb{P}^{\dagger}}\ q\implies\pi(p)\ \bot_{\mathbb{P}^{*}}\ \pi(q)\text{.}

To see that
ran(\pi)
is dense in
\mathbb{P}^{*}
, fix any
x\in P(\mathfrak{T})
. By
(2)
, there is
p\in P
for which

•

x\cup\{\ulcorner\dot{G}(p)\urcorner\}\in P(\mathfrak{T})
, and

•

\forall q\ (\ulcorner\dot{G}(q)\urcorner\in x\implies p\leq_{\mathbb{P}}q)
.

What this entails by our definition of
\mathfrak{T}
is, whenever
\mathcal{M}\models^{*}\mathfrak{T}
and
\pi(p)\subset\Sigma(\mathfrak{T},\mathcal{M})
, we must have
x\subset\Sigma(\mathfrak{T},\mathcal{M})
. Let
y\leq_{\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})}\pi(p)
. Choose a model
\mathcal{M}
of
\mathfrak{T}
in some outer model of
V
such that
y\subset\Sigma(\mathfrak{T},\mathcal{M})
. Then
\pi(p)\subset\Sigma(\mathfrak{T},\mathcal{M})
, so also
x\subset\Sigma(\mathfrak{T},\mathcal{M})
. As a result,
y\not\!\!\bot_{\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})}\ x
, and we can conclude
\pi(p)\leq^{*}x
.
∎

Remark 5.35
.

Theorem
5.34
tells us two things, in view of Remark
2.46
.

(1)

The map
\tilde{\mathbb{P}}
defined in (
5.3
) is an isomorphism between
\trianglelefteq/\sim_{T}
and
\lessdot/\sim_{P}
.

(2)

Every member of
\trianglelefteq/\sim_{T}
contains a
\Pi_{2}
TCI
\mathfrak{T}
for which

\displaystyle\{V[\mathcal{M}]:\mathcal{M}\models^{*}\mathfrak{T}\text{ in an outer model of }V\}

\displaystyle=\{V[g]:g\text{ is }\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})\text{-generic over }V\}\text{.}

It can be argued that the heart of forcing theory is in comparing the forcing extensions of different forcing notions. In this respect, and especially in the study of iterated forcing, a niceness result very often involves statements of the form

“every
\mathbb{Q}
-generic extension over
V
contains a
\mathbb{P}
-generic extension over
V
, and every
\mathbb{P}
-generic extension over
V
can be extended to a
\mathbb{Q}
-generic extension over
V
,”

which is virtually only provable by showing
\mathbb{P}\lessdot\mathbb{Q}
. Therefore, the relation
\lessdot
, and indeed
\lessdot/\sim_{P}
, encapsulates much of the core content of forcing theory. Points
(1)
and
(2)
can then be viewed as indicators that
\hat{\mathbb{P}}
(defined in (
5.2
)) gives rise to a morally correct correspondence between TCIs and forcing notions. Further,
(2)
suggests that
\Pi_{2}
is a natural upper bound to the complexity of objects accessible by the technique of forcing.

Theorem 5.36
.

Let
\mathfrak{T}=(T,\sigma,\dot{\mathcal{U}},\vartheta)
be a
\Pi_{2}
TCI. If
\mathfrak{T}
is consistent, then every
\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})
-generic filter over
V
witnesses a
(\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T}),V)
-generic model of
\mathfrak{T}
.

Proof.

The theorem follows directly from Lemmas
3.56
,
5.17
and
5.22
, noting that the hypothesis of Lemma
3.56
are satisfied with

•

\mathfrak{A}_{\mathfrak{T}}
in place of
\mathfrak{A}
,

•

|\mathfrak{A}_{\mathfrak{T}}|=|trcl(\mathfrak{A}_{\mathfrak{T}})|
in place of
\lambda
,

•

\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}}
in place of
\mathcal{L}
,

•

[\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}}]^{<\omega}
in place of
B
,

•

P(\mathfrak{T})
in place of
P
,

•

\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})
in place of
\mathbb{P}
,

•

\Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}}
in place of
\Gamma
,

•

g
a
\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})
-generic filter over
V
, and

•

V[g]
in place of
W
. ∎

Remark 5.37
.

By the proof of Corollary
5.26
, Theorem
5.36
is equivalent to, and can be restated as:

Theorem 5.36
′
.

Let
\mathfrak{T}=(T,\sigma,\dot{\mathcal{U}},\vartheta)
be a
\Pi_{2}
TCI. If
P(\mathfrak{T})
is not empty, then every
\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})
-generic filter over
V
witnesses a
(\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T}),V)
-generic model of
\mathfrak{T}
.

Remark 5.38
.

By Theorem
5.36
and
(2)
of Observation
5.29
, we see that for every
\Pi_{2}
TCI
\mathfrak{T}
,

\displaystyle\{V[\mathcal{M}]:\mathcal{M}\models^{*}\mathfrak{T}\text{ in an outer model of }V\}

\displaystyle\supset\{V[g]:g\text{ is }\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})\text{-generic over }V\}\text{.}

In other words, forcing allows one to construct abundant models of every
\Pi_{2}
TCI. This suggests that
\Pi_{2}
is a natural lower bound to the complexity of objects accessible by the technique of forcing.

Remarks
5.35
and
5.38
give two different ways of lensing forcing through the study of TCIs and their models. If we measure the power of forcing by the complexity of objects it has access to, then the two perspectives in question posit that
\Pi_{2}
is a good classification of said power.

This interpretation lends credence and weight to the informal thesis (a slogan, rather),

“Forcing is
\Pi_{2}
.”

More importantly, it pitches tantalising prospects for using a complexity class defined on TCIs as a measure of — or a proxy for — accessibility within the context of the set-theoretic multiverse. We hope more work can be done in the future to formally establish and justify an approach along these lines of thinking.

5.3
More Generic Models of
\Pi_{2}
TCIs

The remainder of this section concerns itself with finer details regarding the existence of generic models of
\Pi_{2}
TCIs. First up is a generic version of Lemma
5.13
.

Lemma 5.39
.

Let
\mathfrak{T}=(T,\sigma,\dot{\mathcal{U}},\vartheta)
be a
\Pi_{2}
TCI with an infinite model in some outer model of
V
. Then for every infinite ordinal
\beta
, there is a forcing notion
\mathbb{P}
such that whenever
g
is a
\mathbb{P}
-generic filter over
V
, there are sets
\mathcal{M}=(U;\mathcal{I})
and
f
in some outer model of
V
for which

(a)

g
witnesses
(\mathcal{M},f)
is a
(\mathbb{P},V)
-generic object,

(b)

g
witnesses
\mathcal{M}
is a
(\mathbb{P},V)
-generic model of
\mathfrak{T}
, and

(c)

f:\beta\longrightarrow U
is a bijection.

Proof.

Fix an infinite ordinal
\beta
. We want to modify
\mathfrak{T}
to get another consistent
\Pi_{2}
TCI
\mathfrak{T}^{*}
such that from every model
\mathcal{M}^{*}
of
\mathfrak{T}^{*}
we can read off a structure
\mathcal{M}=(U;\mathcal{I})
and a function
f
satisfying both

(b)’

\mathcal{M}\models^{*}\mathfrak{T}
and
\Sigma(\mathfrak{T}^{*},\mathcal{M}^{*})\cap\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}}=\Sigma(\mathfrak{T},\mathcal{M})

as well as
(c)
of the lemma.

Note that we can, without loss of generality, assume
\sigma
contains only relation symbols and constant symbols. This is because for any function symbol
\dot{X}
and any
n<\omega
,
\dot{X}
being a
n
-ary function is definable in a
(n+1)
-ary relation symbol
\dot{Y}
via the conjunction of the
\Pi_{2}
sentences

\displaystyle\ulcorner\forall x_{1}\dots\forall x_{n}\ \exists y\ (\dot{Y}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n},y))\urcorner\text{ and}

\displaystyle\ulcorner\forall x_{1}\dots\forall x_{n}\ \forall y\ \forall z\ ((\dot{Y}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n},y)\wedge\dot{Y}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n},z))\implies y=z)\urcorner\text{,}

if we interpret formulas of the form
\dot{Y}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n},x_{n+1})
as
\dot{X}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})=x_{n+1}
.

Have
(y,z)
be
\vartheta(\dot{\mathcal{U}})
and
\sigma^{\prime}
be
\sigma\cup\{\dot{\mathcal{U}}\}
. Choose

•

\dot{F}
to be a unary function symbol not in
\sigma^{\prime}
,

•

\dot{\mathcal{U}}^{*}
and
\dot{V}
to be distinct unary relation symbols not in
\sigma^{\prime}
, and

•

\dot{c}
to be a constant symbol not in
\sigma^{\prime}
, for each
c\in y
, such that
\dot{c}\neq\dot{d}
if
\{c,d\}\subset y
and
c\neq d
.

Let

\sigma^{*}:=\sigma\cup\{\dot{F},\dot{\mathcal{U}},\dot{V}\}\cup\{\dot{c}:c\in y\}.

We specify
\vartheta^{*}
by how it acts on members of its domain. Pick a set
b
of cardinality
|\beta|
that is disjoint from
y
, and set
\vartheta^{*}(\dot{\mathcal{U}}^{*}):=(y\cup b,1)
. Make the assignments

\displaystyle\vartheta^{*}(\dot{F}):=\

\displaystyle(b\times y,0)

\displaystyle\vartheta^{*}(\dot{\mathcal{U}}):=\

\displaystyle(y,z)

\displaystyle\vartheta^{*}(\dot{V}):=\

\displaystyle(b,1)

\displaystyle\vartheta^{*}(\dot{c}):=\

\displaystyle(\{c\},0)\text{ for each }c\in y\text{.}

Whenever
\dot{X}\in\sigma
and
\vartheta(\dot{X})=(y^{\prime},z^{\prime})
, we define
\vartheta^{*}(\dot{X}):=(y^{\prime},min\{z,z^{\prime}\})
.

Now, we modify
\Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}}
by first removing members of the type described in
(3)
and
(4)
of Lemma
5.17
, and then for each remaining member
\varphi
of
\Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}}
, replacing every subformula of
\varphi
of the form
\ulcorner E(\ulcorner x\urcorner)\urcorner
with
\ulcorner x\urcorner
.

Call the result of said modification
T^{\prime}
. Whenever
\dot{X}\in\sigma
is a
n
-ary relation symbol with
\vartheta(\dot{X})=(y^{\prime},1)
, define

T(\dot{X}):=\{\ulcorner\bigwedge_{1\leq k\leq n}\dot{\mathcal{U}}(\dot{c}_{k})\implies\dot{X}(\dot{c}_{1},\dots,\dot{c}_{n})\urcorner:(c_{1},\dots,c_{n})\in y^{\prime}\cap y^{n}\}.

Finally, define
T^{*}
to be the union of
T^{\prime}
,

\bigcup\{T(\dot{X}):\dot{X}\in\sigma\text{ and }\exists y^{\prime}\ (\vartheta(\dot{X})=(y^{\prime},1))\},

and the finite set of sentences

\displaystyle T^{*}_{0}:=\{

\displaystyle\ulcorner\forall x\ \exists y\ (\dot{V}(x)\implies(\dot{\mathcal{U}}(y)\wedge\dot{F}(x)=y))\urcorner,

\displaystyle\ulcorner\forall x\ \exists y\ (\dot{\mathcal{U}}(x)\implies(\dot{V}(y)\wedge\dot{F}(y)=x))\urcorner,

\displaystyle\ulcorner\forall x\ \forall y\ (\dot{F}(x)=\dot{F}(y)\implies x=y)\urcorner\}\text{.}

Clearly
T^{*}
is a set of
\Pi_{2}
sentences over the vocabulary
\sigma^{*}
.

A routine verification should enable the reader to see that

T^{*}_{1}:=T^{\prime}\cup(\bigcup\{T(\dot{X}):\dot{X}\in\sigma\text{ and }\exists y^{\prime}\ (\vartheta(\dot{X})=(y^{\prime},1))\})

is basically a translation of
\Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}}
in our expanded vocabulary
\sigma^{*}
, with the set of constants
\{\dot{c}:c\in y\}
fulfilling a role similar to that of the parameter
\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}}
(say, in the context of
(4)
in the proof of Lemma
5.17
). On the other hand,
T^{*}_{0}
expresses precisely the requirement that a bijection from
b
(and thus from
\beta
, in any outer model of
V
) into
\mathcal{I}(\dot{\mathcal{U}})
exists for every
\mathcal{I}
satisfying
(y\cup b;\mathcal{I})\models^{*}(T^{*}_{0},\sigma^{*},\dot{\mathcal{U}}^{*},\vartheta^{*})
— said bijection is just
\mathcal{I}(\dot{F})
. In fact, it does so in a manner independent of truths over the vocabulary
\sigma^{\prime}
, so that whenever
\mathcal{M}^{*}
is a model of

\mathfrak{T}^{*}:=(T^{*},\sigma^{*},\dot{\mathcal{U}}^{*},\vartheta^{*})=(T^{*}_{0}\cup T^{*}_{1},\sigma^{*},\dot{\mathcal{U}}^{*},\vartheta^{*}),

we have

\Sigma(\mathfrak{T}^{*},\mathcal{M}^{*})\cap\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}}=\Sigma(\mathfrak{T},\mathcal{M})

for some model
\mathcal{M}
of
\mathfrak{T}
. By Lemma
5.13
and our assumptions on
\mathfrak{T}
,
\mathfrak{T}^{*}
is consistent.

We have thus checked that
\mathfrak{T}^{*}
possesses the properties we want: it is a consistent
\Pi_{2}
TCI, and from every model
\mathcal{M}^{*}
of
\mathfrak{T}^{*}
we can read off a structure
\mathcal{M}=(U;\mathcal{I})
and a function
f
satisfying both
(b)’
defined at the beginning of the proof as well as
(c)
of the lemma. An invocation of Theorem
5.36
with
\mathfrak{T^{*}}
in place of
\mathfrak{T}
then completes the proof.
∎

By strengthening the hypothesis on
\mathfrak{T}
in Lemma
5.39
, we can derive more from our witnesses.

Lemma 5.40
.

Let
\mathfrak{T}=(T,\sigma,\dot{\mathcal{U}},\vartheta)
be a
\Pi_{2}
TCI with only infinite model(s) across all outer models of
V
. Then for every infinite ordinal
\beta
, there is a forcing notion
\mathbb{P}
such that whenever
g
is a
\mathbb{P}
-generic filter over
V
, there are sets
\mathcal{M}=(U;\mathcal{I})
and
f
in some outer model of
V
for which

(a)

g
witnesses
(\mathcal{M},f)
is a
(\mathbb{P},V)
-generic object,

(b)

g
witnesses
\mathcal{M}
is a
(\mathbb{P},V)
-generic model of
\mathfrak{T}
,

(c)

g\cap P(\mathfrak{T})
witnesses
\mathcal{M}
is a
(\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T}),V)
-generic model of
\mathfrak{T}
, and

(d)

f:\beta\longrightarrow U
is a bijection.

Proof.

Construct
\mathfrak{T}^{*}
from
\mathfrak{T}
as per the proof of Lemma
5.39
.

Proposition 5.41
.

\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})\lessdot\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T}^{*})
.

Proof.

Observe that, if
\mathcal{M}
is a model of
\mathfrak{T}
is some outer model
W
of
V
, then
\mathcal{M}
extends to a model of
\mathfrak{T}^{*}
in an outer model
W^{\prime}
of
W
. As a result,
\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})
is a suborder of
\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T}^{*})
. To show the regularity of
\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})
as a suborder of
\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T}^{*})
, let
p\in\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T}^{*})
. Define

q_{0}:=\{\ulcorner\dot{\mathcal{U}}(j)\urcorner:\ulcorner\dot{F}(i)=j\urcorner\in p\}

and let

q:=(p\cap\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}})\cup q_{0}.

Obviously
q\in\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})
. Consider any
\Sigma

\Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}},\mathfrak{A}_{\mathfrak{T}})
-certifying
q
in some outer model of
V
. Since the set
U
defined by
\dot{\mathcal{U}}
in
\Sigma
is guaranteed to be infinite following our assumptions on
\mathfrak{T}
, the finitely many restrictions imposed by
p
on the relationship between (the function interpreting)
\dot{F}
and
U
can be circumvented with ease. In other words,
\Sigma
can be extended to some
\Sigma^{*}

\Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}^{*}}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}^{*}},\mathfrak{A}_{\mathfrak{T}^{*}})
-certifying
p
. But this means every
q^{\prime}\leq_{\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})}q
is compatible with
p
in
\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T}^{*})
.
∎

By Fact
2.54
, Proposition
5.41
, and the identity

\bigcup(g\cap P(\mathfrak{T}))=(\bigcup g)\cap\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}}

which holds for every
\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T}^{*})
-generic filter
g
over
V
, we are done.
∎

Models of a TCI
\mathfrak{T}
across all outer models of
V
can be very complicated. However, when a model of
\mathfrak{T}
is finitely determined, its atomic diagram can be easily read off
\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})
.

Lemma 5.42
.

Let
\mathfrak{T}
be a TCI and
\mathcal{M}
be a finitely determined model of
\mathfrak{T}
in some outer model of
V
. Then for some atom
p
of
\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})
,
\Sigma(\mathfrak{T},\mathcal{M})=g_{p}(\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T}))
. In particular,
\mathcal{M}\in V
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be finitely determined by
\varphi
. Without loss of generality, we can assume
\varphi
is the conjunction of a set of literals
\{l_{i}:i<n\}
for some
n<\omega
. This means

p:=\{\ulcorner E(l_{i})\urcorner:i<n\}

is an atom of
\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})
. Lemma
2.49
tells us that
g_{p}(\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T}))
is
\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})
-generic over
V
, so necessarily
\Sigma(\mathfrak{T},\mathcal{M})=g_{p}(\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T}))
by Theorem
5.36
. Then according to Lemma
5.22
,
\mathcal{M}\in V
because
g_{p}(\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T}))\in V
.
∎

It is possible to have an analogue of Lemma
5.42
for models that are “close to being finitely determined”.

Definition 5.43
.

Let
\mathfrak{T}
be a TCI. Inductively define
\Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}}^{(\alpha)}
,
P(\mathfrak{T})^{(\alpha)}
and
\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})^{(\alpha)}
for all ordinals
\alpha\leq|[\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}}]^{<\omega}|^{+}
as follows:

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}}^{(0)}:=\

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}}\text{,}

\displaystyle P(\mathfrak{T})^{(0)}:=\

\displaystyle P(\mathfrak{T})\text{,}

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}}^{(\alpha)}:=\

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}}^{(\alpha-1)}\cup\{\ulcorner\bigvee_{x\in p}(\neg E(x))\urcorner:p\text{ is an atom of }\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})^{(\alpha-1)}\}

\displaystyle\text{if }\alpha\text{ is a successor ordinal,}

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}}^{(\alpha)}:=\

\displaystyle\bigcup_{\beta<\alpha}\Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}}^{(\beta)}

\displaystyle\text{if }\alpha\text{ is a limit ordinal,}

\displaystyle P(\mathfrak{T})^{(\alpha)}:=\

\displaystyle\{p\in[\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}}]^{<\omega}:\ \Vdash_{Col(\omega,|trcl(\mathfrak{A}_{\mathfrak{T}})|)}\exists\Sigma\ (``\Sigma\ \Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}}^{(\alpha)}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}},\mathfrak{A}_{\mathfrak{T}})\text{-certifies }p")\}\text{,}

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})^{(\alpha)}:=\

\displaystyle(P(\mathfrak{T})^{(\alpha)},\leq_{\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})})\text{.}

By a simple cardinality argument, there must exist some
\alpha<|[\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}}]^{<\omega}|^{+}
for which
\Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}}^{(\alpha)}=\Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}}^{(\alpha+1)}
, whence
\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})^{(\alpha)}=\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})^{(\alpha+1)}
.

Definition 5.44
.

Let
\Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\top}
denote the unique
\Gamma
such that
\Gamma=\Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}}^{(\alpha)}=\Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}}^{(\alpha+1)}
for some
\alpha<|[\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}}]^{<\omega}|^{+}
. Similarly,
\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})^{\top}
shall denote the unique
\mathbb{P}
such that
\mathbb{P}=\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})^{(\alpha)}=\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})^{(\alpha+1)}
for some
\alpha<|[\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}}]^{<\omega}|^{+}
.

It is not hard to see that
P(\mathfrak{T})^{\top}
is an atomless upward closed subset of
\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})
and
\Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}}\subset\Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\top}
.

Remark 5.45
.

In constructing the
\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})^{(\alpha)}
’s, we are inductively removing atoms of
\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})
. These atoms are representatives of isolated models of a TCI. By looking at Definition
5.43
in this way, we can draw obvious parallels between
\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})^{(\alpha)}
and the
\alpha
-th-order Cantor-Bendixson derivative of a set. Such parallels culminate in
\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})^{\top}
being analogous to the “perfect core” of
\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})
.

Definition 5.46
.

Given a TCI
\mathfrak{T}
and any
\mathcal{M}
, we say
\mathcal{M}
is an
almost finitely determined model of

\mathfrak{T}
iff
\mathcal{M}\models^{*}\mathfrak{T}
and for some
\alpha<|[\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}}]^{<\omega}|^{+}
and an atom
p
of
\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})^{(\alpha)}
,

p\subset\Sigma(\mathfrak{T},\mathcal{M}).

We have as our next lemma, the promised analogue of Lemma
5.42
.

Lemma 5.47
.

Let
\mathfrak{T}
be a TCI and
\mathcal{M}
be an almost finitely determined model of
\mathfrak{T}
in some outer model of
V
. Then for some
\alpha<|[\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}}]^{<\omega}|^{+}
and some atom
p
of
\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})^{(\alpha)}
,
\Sigma(\mathfrak{T},\mathcal{M})=g_{p}(\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})^{(\alpha)})
. In particular,
\mathcal{M}\in V
.

Proof.

Choose any model
\mathcal{M}
of
\mathfrak{T}
in an outer model of
V
. It suffices to prove by induction on
\alpha\leq|[\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}}]^{<\omega}|^{+}
that

\displaystyle\forall q\ \exists\beta\leq\alpha\ \exists p\ (

\displaystyle(q\text{ is an atom of }\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})^{(\alpha)}\text{ and }q\subset\Sigma(\mathfrak{T},\mathcal{M}))

\displaystyle\implies(p\text{ is an atom of }\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})^{(\beta)}\text{ and }\Sigma(\mathfrak{T},\mathcal{M})=g_{p}(\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})^{(\beta)})))\text{.}

The base case where
\alpha=0
is just Lemma
5.42
. For the inductive case, assume
0<\alpha\leq|[\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}}]^{<\omega}|^{+}
. and let
q
be an atom of
\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})^{(\alpha)}
with
q\subset\Sigma(\mathfrak{T},\mathcal{M})
. Then by Lemma
2.49
and the definition of
\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})^{(\alpha)}
, either
\Sigma(\mathfrak{T},\mathcal{M})=g_{q}(\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})^{(\alpha)})
or there is
\beta^{\prime}<\alpha
and an atom
q^{\prime}
of
\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})^{(\beta^{\prime})}
such that
q^{\prime}\subset\Sigma(\mathfrak{T},\mathcal{M})
. In the latter case, the inductive hypothesis gives us
\beta\leq\beta^{\prime}
and an atom
p
of
\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})^{(\beta)}
for which
\Sigma(\mathfrak{T},\mathcal{M})=g_{p}(\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})^{(\beta)})
. Either way we are done.
∎

The way
\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})
and
\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})^{\top}
are defined from a TCI
\mathfrak{T}
allows us to establish a nice dichotomy on the
(\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T}),V)
-generic models of
\mathfrak{T}
when
\mathfrak{T}
is
\Pi_{2}
.

Lemma 5.48
.

Let
\mathfrak{T}
be a
\Pi_{2}
TCI and
\mathcal{M}
be a
(\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T}),V)
-generic model of
\mathfrak{T}
. Then one of the following must hold:

(1)

\mathcal{M}
is almost finitely determined.

(2)

\mathcal{M}
is a
(\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})^{\top},V)
-generic model of
\mathfrak{T}
.

Proof.

Let
g
be a
\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})
-generic filter over
V
and assume
\mathcal{A}\cap g=\emptyset
, where

\mathcal{A}:=\{p:\exists\alpha\ (\alpha<|[\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}}]^{<\omega}|^{+}\text{ and }p\text{ is an atom of }\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})^{(\alpha)})\}.

This latter assumption is equivalent to saying that the unique model
\mathcal{M}
of
\mathfrak{T}
for which
\bigcup g=\Sigma(\mathfrak{T},\mathcal{M})
is not almost finitely determined. By Theorem
5.36
, it suffices to show that
g
is a
\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})^{\top}
-generic filter over
V
. Clearly,
\bigcup g

\Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\top}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}},\mathfrak{A}_{\mathfrak{T}})
-certifies
p
, so
g\subset\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})^{\top}
. That
\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})^{\top}
is a suborder of
\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})
means
g
is a filter on
\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})^{\top}
.

To see
g
is
\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})^{\top}
-generic over
V
, let
E
be predense in
\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})^{\top}
. Note that if
p\in\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})
is incompatible in
\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})
with every member of
\mathcal{A}
, then
p\in\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})^{\top}
. As such,
E\cup\mathcal{A}
must be predense in
\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})
. But this implies
E\cap g\neq\emptyset
because
g
is
\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})
-generic and
\mathcal{A}\cap g=\emptyset
.
∎

The following is a stronger version of Theorem
5.36
.

Theorem 5.49
.

Let
\mathfrak{T}
be a
\Pi_{2}
TCI. If not all models of
\mathfrak{T}
are almost finitely determined, then
\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})^{\top}
is non-empty and every
\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})^{\top}
-generic filter over
V
witnesses
\mathcal{M}
is a
(\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})^{\top},V)
-generic model of
\mathfrak{T}
for some
\mathcal{M}
.

Proof.

Assume not all models of
\mathfrak{T}
are almost finitely determined, and let
\mathcal{M}
be a model of
\mathfrak{T}
not almost finitely determined in some outer model of
V
. Then
\Sigma(\mathfrak{T},\mathcal{M})

\Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\top}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}},\mathfrak{A}_{\mathfrak{T}})
-certifies
\emptyset
, so
\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})^{\top}
is non-empty.

Check that the hypothesis of Lemma
3.56
are satisfied when we have

•

\mathfrak{A}_{\mathfrak{T}}
in place of
\mathfrak{A}
,

•

|\mathfrak{A}_{\mathfrak{T}}|=|trcl(\mathfrak{A}_{\mathfrak{T}})|
in place of
\lambda
,

•

\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}}
in place of
\mathcal{L}
,

•

[\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}}]^{<\omega}
in place of
B
,

•

P(\mathfrak{T})^{\top}
in place of
P
,

•

\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})^{\top}
in place of
\mathbb{P}
,

•

\Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\top}
in place of
\Gamma
,

•

g
a
\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})^{\top}
-generic filter over
V
, and

•

V[g]
in place of
W
.

A direct application of said lemma, coupled with the knowledge that
\Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}}\subset\Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\top}
, completes the proof.
∎

Two important, yet perhaps surprising, properties of
\Pi_{2}
TCIs follow from the dichotomy in Theorem
5.49
. We state these properties in the next corollary.

Corollary 5.50
.

The following statements hold.

(1)

Let
\mathfrak{T}
be a
\Pi_{2}
TCI. Every model of
\mathfrak{T}
can be found in
V
iff every model of
\mathfrak{T}
is almost finitely determined (see also Lemma
5.47
).

(2)

There is a procedure to uniformly decide in
V
, whether every model of any given
\Pi_{2}
TCI
\mathfrak{T}
is definable in
V
with parameters from

\mathrm{PS}(\mathfrak{T}):=trcl(\mathfrak{T})\cup|trcl(\mathfrak{T})|^{+}\cup H(\omega)\text{,}

or equivalently, whether every model of
\mathfrak{T}
can be found in
V
.

Moreover, for some
n<\omega
there is a
(n+2)
-ary formula
\psi
in the language of set theory such that, given any
\Pi_{2}
TCI
\mathfrak{T}
,

•

whenever
\vec{z}\in\mathrm{PS}(\mathfrak{T})^{n}
, if there exists
x
for which
\psi(x,\mathfrak{T},\vec{z})
holds, then said
x
is unique, and

•

if every model of
\mathfrak{T}
can be found in
V
, then in
V
,

\displaystyle\{x:\exists\vec{z}\ (\vec{z}\in\mathrm{PS}(\mathfrak{T})^{n}\wedge\psi(x,\mathfrak{T},\vec{z}))\}

\displaystyle=\{\mathcal{M}:\mathcal{M}\models^{*}\mathfrak{T}\}

\displaystyle\subset H(|trcl(\mathfrak{T})|^{+})\text{.}

Notice that the first sentence to appear in
(2)
of Corollary
5.50
is similar in form to Corollary
5.26
. Through Remark
5.37
, this points further to Theorem
5.49
being an improvement upon Theorem
5.36
.

For a countable TCI
\mathfrak{T}
, the consistency of
\mathfrak{T}
implies the existence of a model of
\mathfrak{T}
in
V
.

Lemma 5.51
.

Let
\mathfrak{T}=(T,\sigma,\dot{\mathcal{U}},\vartheta)
be a TCI such that

|\sigma\cup y|\leq\aleph_{0}

whenever
\vartheta(\dot{\mathcal{U}})=(y,z)
for some
z
. If
\mathfrak{T}
is consistent then
\mathfrak{T}
has a model in
V
.

Proof.

Fix
f_{1}
a bijection between
y
and
|y|
, and
f_{2}
a form-preserving signature embedding from
\sigma\cup\{\dot{\mathcal{U}}\}
into
H(\omega)
. Then
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
naturally induce

•

a TCI
\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}:=(T^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime},\dot{\mathcal{U}},\vartheta^{\prime})
in
V
such that
\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}
has a countable transitive closure, and

•

a bijection between
\{\mathcal{M}:\mathcal{M}\models^{*}\mathfrak{T}\}
and
\{\mathcal{M}:\mathcal{M}\models^{*}\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}\}
in every weak outer model of
V
.

As a consequence, we can assume
\mathfrak{T}
has a countable transitive closure without loss of generality. By Lemma
2.15
,
\mathfrak{T}
can be coded as a real. Besides, if
\mathfrak{T}
has a model
\mathcal{M}
in an outer model
W
of
V
, then
M
has a real code. By a routine check while unfurling the definition of
\models^{*}
(see e.g. the proof of Lemma
3.55
for an argument of the satisfaction relation being
\Sigma_{1}
), we get that the statement

\exists\mathcal{M}\ (\mathcal{M}\models^{*}\mathfrak{T})

is equivalent to a
\mathbf{\Sigma^{1}_{1}}
sentence involving a real code of
\mathfrak{T}
found in
V
, so it is absolute for
V
and any of its weak outer models. If
\mathfrak{T}
is consistent, it must have a model in some outer model of
V
, whence it has a model in
V
.
∎

Ideally, in the spirit of Lemmas
5.39
and
5.40
, we want to prove a generic version of Lemma
5.51
. This can be done through a relatively effective version of Theorem
5.49
for a certain class of countable TCIs, so as to kill two birds with one stone. Some definitions and facts are prerequisites.

For the rest of this subsection,

•

fix a bijection

\displaystyle f^{\dagger}:\

\displaystyle\mathrm{Var}\cup\{x:x\text{ is a first-order logical symbol}\}\cup\{\ulcorner\in\urcorner\}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\{n<\omega:n\text{ is odd}\}\text{,}

and

•

interpret
\Delta_{n}
,
\Pi_{n}
and
\Sigma_{n}
formulas the way they are defined in Definition
2.7
.

Definition 5.52
.

For any countable set
X
, we say
(r,f)

witnesses

(X;\in)

is computable
iff

•

f
is a bijection from
X
into
\{n<\omega:n\text{ is even}\}
,

•

​
\begin{aligned} r=\{\langle(f\cup f^{\dagger})^{*}(\varphi)\rangle:\varphi\text{ is a member of the }\Delta_{0}\text{-elementary diagram of }(X;\in)\},\end{aligned}

where

\circ

\langle\cdot\rangle
is the standard computable Gödel numbering of strings over
\omega
, and

\circ

(f\cup f^{\dagger})^{*}
is the canonical bijection from the set of finite strings over
dom(f\cup f^{\dagger})
into the set of finite strings over
\omega
, induced by
f\cup f^{\dagger}
, and

•

r
is computable.

We say
r
is a
nicely computable code of

(X;\in)
iff there is
f
for which
(r,f)
witnesses
(X;\in)
is computable.

Fact 5.53
.

There is a nicely computable code of
(H(\omega);\in)
.

Fact 5.54
.

If
r
is a nicely computable code of
(H(\omega);\in)
, then there is a unique
f
for which
(r,f)
witnesses
(H(\omega);\in)
is computable.

Let
\mathfrak{T}=(T,\sigma,\dot{\mathcal{U}},\vartheta)
be a TCI, and
y
be such that
\vartheta(\dot{\mathcal{U}})=(y,z)
for some
z
.

Assume
|\sigma\cup y|\leq\aleph_{0}
. Then we can find
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
such that

•

f_{1}
is a bijection from
y
into
|y|
, and

•

f_{2}
is a form-preserving signature embedding from
\sigma\cup\{\dot{\mathcal{U}}\}
into
H(\omega)
.

Together,
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
naturally induce a TCI
\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}
with its associated
\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}}
being a subset of
H(\omega)
. Moreover, in every model
\mathfrak{A}
of
\mathsf{ZFC-Powerset}
containing
\{\mathfrak{T},f_{1},f_{2}\}
,
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
also induce a bijection
h^{\mathfrak{A}}
from

\{\mathcal{M}:\mathcal{M}\models^{*}\mathfrak{T}\}

into

\{\mathcal{M}^{\prime}:\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\models^{*}\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}\},

such that for all
\mathcal{M}\in dom(h^{\mathfrak{A}})
,
\mathcal{M}\cong h^{\mathfrak{A}}(\mathcal{M})
.

Hence, if we only care about models of
\mathfrak{T}
up to isomorphism, we can without loss of generality, assume
y
is an ordinal at most
\omega
and
\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}}
is a subset of
H(\omega)
.

Definition 5.55
.

A TCI
\mathfrak{T}=(T,\sigma,\dot{\mathcal{U}},\vartheta)
is
code-friendly
iff

•

\vartheta(\dot{\mathcal{U}})=(y,z)\in(\omega+1)\times 2
, and

•

\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}}\subset H(\omega)
.

Code-friendly TCIs are relatively well-behaved and easy to reason about, especially when it comes to things like absoluteness. Notice that given any code-friendly TCI
\mathfrak{T}=(T,\sigma,\dot{\mathcal{U}},\vartheta)
and any ordinal
\alpha
,

(CF1)

\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}}
and
\Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}}
are definable subsets of
H(\omega)
over the structure

\mathfrak{A}^{*}_{\mathfrak{T}}:=\mathfrak{(}H(\omega);\in,T,\sigma,\dot{\mathcal{U}},\vartheta),

(CF2)

P(\mathfrak{T})^{(\alpha)}=\{p\in[\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}}]^{<\omega}:\exists\Sigma\ (``\Sigma\ \Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}}^{(\alpha)}(\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}},\mathfrak{A}^{*}_{\mathfrak{T}})\text{-certifies }p")\}
by straightforward induction incorporating an argument similar to that which proved Lemma
3.55
, and hence

(CF3)

P(\mathfrak{T})^{\top}
is
\Delta_{1}
-definable in
\mathfrak{A}^{*}_{\mathfrak{T}}
.

This means that the definition of
\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})^{\top}
from a code-friendly TCI
\mathfrak{T}
is absolute for transitive models of
\mathsf{ZFC-Powerset}
.

Recall Cohen forcing
\mathbb{C}=(C;\leq_{\mathbb{C}})
. We will use this labelling in the statements and proofs of the subsequent lemmas.

Lemma 5.56
.

Let
\mathfrak{T}
be a code-friendly
\Pi_{2}
TCI, and
(r,f)
witness
(H(\omega);\in)
is computable. Then one of the following must hold.

(1)

All models of
\mathfrak{T}
are almost finitely determined.

(2)

There is an oracle machine
\Psi
and a countable structure
\mathfrak{A}
in the language of set theory, such that whenever
g
is a
\mathbb{C}
-generic filter over
\mathfrak{A}
, there is a unique model
\mathcal{M}_{g}
of
\mathfrak{T}
satisfying

\Psi^{(f"g)\oplus(f"(P(\mathfrak{T})^{\top}))}=f"(\Sigma(\mathfrak{T},\mathcal{M}_{g}))\text{.}

Moreover, the function
g\mapsto\mathcal{M}_{g}
defined as such is injective.

Proof.

Assume not all models of
\mathfrak{T}
are almost finitely determined. For brevity, let us write

\displaystyle s:=\

\displaystyle f"(P(\mathfrak{T})^{\top})\text{ and}

\displaystyle\leq_{s}\ :=\

\displaystyle f"(\leq_{\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})^{\top}})\text{.}

We shall identify
s
with
(s,\leq_{s})
whenever contextually necessary. This can be done without loss of generality because
\leq_{s}
is computable in
s
.

Going forward, even beyond this proof, we would often argue about things in
H(\omega)
even though our intended domain of discourse is the set of natural numbers. This is because first-order truths about
(H(\omega);\in)
are uniformly propagated by
f
onto its range, so that specific versions of them hold there as well. If one such truth is sufficiently simple, then
r
knows the version of it on
ran(f)
and can then relay that to the appropriate machines for further processing.

Let
\mathfrak{A}=(A;\in)
be any countable elementary substructure of
\mathfrak{A}_{\mathfrak{T}}
with
P(\mathfrak{T})^{\top}\in A
. Then
A
is finitely transitive because
\mathfrak{A}_{\mathfrak{T}}
is a transitive model of
\mathsf{ZFC-Powerset}
. Since
\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}}
is just the closure of
\bigcup P(\mathfrak{T})^{\top}
under negation in
\mathfrak{A}_{\mathfrak{T}}
, we have
\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}}\in A
. That
\mathfrak{T}
is code-friendly and
H(\omega)\subset A
implies
\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}}\subset A
too, so
\mathfrak{A}
is
\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}}
-suitable. The members of
\Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\top}
are
(\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}})^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}_{\mathfrak{T}}}
-
\Pi_{2}
sentences with a single parameter
P(\mathfrak{T})^{\top}
and quantification exclusively over
H(\omega)
, entailing that
\Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\top}
is also a set of
(\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}})^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}}
-
\Pi_{2}
sentences.

In this vein, similar to what we did in the proof of Theorem
5.49
, check that all except the last two points in the hypothesis of Lemma
3.56
are satisfied with

•

\mathfrak{A}
as defined,

•

|\mathfrak{A}_{\mathfrak{T}}|=|trcl(\mathfrak{A}_{\mathfrak{T}})|
in place of
\lambda
,

•

\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}}
in place of
\mathcal{L}
,

•

[\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}}]^{<\omega}
in place of
B
,

•

P(\mathfrak{T})^{\top}
in place of
P
,

•

\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})^{\top}
in place of
\mathbb{P}
, and

•

\Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\top}
in place of
\Gamma
.

Following the proof of Theorem
5.49
, while bearing in mind

•

\Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}}\subset\Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\top}
,

•

Remark
5.23
and how its invocation is justified by the preceding paragraph, as well as

•

the injectivity of the function
\mathcal{M}\mapsto\Sigma(\mathfrak{T},\mathcal{M})
,

we apply Lemma
3.56
with
V
in place of
W
to give us

\displaystyle\begin{split}\forall\bar{g}\ \exists!\mathcal{M}_{\bar{g}}\ (&\bar{g}\text{ is a }\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})^{\top}\text{-}\Sigma_{1}\text{-generic filter over }\mathfrak{A}\\
&\implies(\mathcal{M}_{\bar{g}}\models^{*}\mathfrak{T}\text{ and }\bigcup\bar{g}=\Sigma(\mathfrak{T},\mathcal{M}_{\bar{g}})))\text{.}\end{split}

(5.4)

In particular,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\forall\bar{g}\ \exists!\mathcal{M}_{\bar{g}}\ (&\bar{g}\text{ is a }\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})^{\top}\text{-generic filter over }\mathfrak{A}\\
&\implies(\mathcal{M}_{\bar{g}}\models^{*}\mathfrak{T}\text{ and }\bigcup\bar{g}=\Sigma(\mathfrak{T},\mathcal{M}_{\bar{g}})))\text{.}\end{split}

(5.5)

Passing (
5.5
) through
f
leads us to the presence of an oracle machine
\bar{\Phi}
fulfilling

\displaystyle\begin{split}\forall\bar{g}\ \exists!\mathcal{M}_{\bar{g}}\ (&\bar{g}\text{ is a }s\text{-generic filter over }\mathfrak{A}\\
&\implies(\mathcal{M}_{\bar{g}}\models^{*}\mathfrak{T}\text{ and }\bar{\Phi}^{\bar{g}}=f"(\Sigma(\mathfrak{T},\mathcal{M}_{\bar{g}}))))\text{.}\end{split}

(5.6)

Next, note that

\displaystyle u:=\

\displaystyle f"C\text{ and}

\displaystyle\leq_{u}\ :=\

\displaystyle f"(\leq_{\mathbb{C}})

are computable subsets of
\omega
. We shall, without loss of generality, identify
u
with
(u,\leq_{u})
whenever contextually necessary.

Proposition 5.57
.

There is a dense embedding
\pi
of
\mathbb{C}
into
\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})^{\top}
such that
\pi
is
\Delta_{1}
-definable over the structure
(H(\omega);\in,P(\mathfrak{T})^{\top})
.

Proof.

First,
C
is a
\Delta_{0}
subset of
H(\omega)
. That
(r,f)
witnesses
H(\omega)
is computable means
f
and
g:=f^{-1}
are functions of which graphs are
\Delta_{1}
-definable over

\mathfrak{B}:=(H(\omega);\in,P(\mathfrak{T})^{\top}).

Also,
\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}}
is
\Delta_{1}
-definable over
\mathfrak{B}
because

x\in\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}}\iff\{x\}\in P(\mathfrak{T})^{\top}\text{ or }\{\neg x\}\in P(\mathfrak{T})^{\top}.

Inductively define sequences
\{a_{n}:n<\omega\}
and
\{k_{n}:n<\omega\}
as follows:

\displaystyle a_{0}:=\

\displaystyle\emptyset\text{,}

\displaystyle k_{n}:=\

\displaystyle min((f"\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}})\setminus(f"a_{n}))\text{, and}

\displaystyle a_{n+1}:=\

\displaystyle a_{n}\cup\{g(k_{n})\}\cup\{\neg(g(k_{n}))\}\text{.}

Note that
\{a_{n}\}
and
\{k_{n}\}
are
\Delta_{1}
-definable over
\mathfrak{B}
. Next, let
P^{*}
be such that

\displaystyle x\in P^{*}\iff x\in P(\mathfrak{T})^{\top}\text{ and }x\subset a_{|x|}\text{,}

and have
x

split in

P^{*}
iff

\displaystyle x\in P^{*}\text{ and }\forall y\ (y\in a_{|x|+1}\setminus a_{|x|}\implies x\cup\{y\}\in P^{*})\text{,}

so that both
P^{*}
and the set of all its members that split in
P^{*}
are
\Delta_{1}
-definable over
\mathfrak{B}
. We say
x
is a
P^{*}
-least split above

z
iff

\displaystyle z\subset x\text{ and }x\text{ splits in }P^{*}\text{ and }\forall y\ (z\subset y\subsetneq x\implies y\text{ does not split in }P^{*})\text{.}

Clearly,
P^{*}
is dense in
P(\mathfrak{T})^{\top}
, so
P(\mathfrak{T})^{\top}
being atomless entails
P^{*}
is too. This yields the existence of a — necessarily unique —
P^{*}
-least split above
z
for every
z\in P^{*}
.

Finally, we can inductively define
\pi
on
C
as such:

\displaystyle\pi(\emptyset):=\

\displaystyle\text{the }P^{*}\text{-least split above }\emptyset\text{,}

\displaystyle\pi(x^{\frown}\langle 0\rangle):=\

\displaystyle\text{the }P^{*}\text{-least split above }\pi(x)\cup\{\varphi_{|x|,0}\}\text{, and}

\displaystyle\pi(x^{\frown}\langle 1\rangle):=\

\displaystyle\text{the }P^{*}\text{-least split above }\pi(x)\cup\{\varphi_{|x|,1}\}\text{,}

where

\displaystyle\varphi_{n,0}:=\

\displaystyle\text{the unique member of }a_{n+1}\setminus a_{n}\text{ with leading symbol }\ulcorner\neg\urcorner\text{, and}

\displaystyle\varphi_{n,1}:=\

\displaystyle\text{the unique member of }a_{n+1}\setminus a_{n}\text{ with leading symbol not }\ulcorner\neg\urcorner\text{.}

It in not difficult to see that
ran(\pi)
is dense in
P^{*}
, and thus in
P(\mathfrak{T})^{\top}
. Moreover, since for each
x\in C
, the definition of
\pi(x)
depends only on the finite set

\{\pi(x\!\restriction_{n}):n<|x|\}

and finitely many parameters which are
\Delta_{1}
-definable over
\mathfrak{B}
,
\pi
must be
\Delta_{1}
-definable over
\mathfrak{B}
as well.
∎

Proposition
5.57
, via
f
, implies the existence of a dense embedding
\pi
of
\mathbb{C}
into
\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})^{\top}
with
f"\pi
computable in
s
, which is all we need to proceed. Fix any such
\pi
. It is not difficult to verify that
f\circ\pi=(f"\pi)\circ f
on domain
C
and taking upward closure of a set in a forcing notion commutes with
f
. As such,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\forall g\ (g\text{ is a }\mathbb{C}\text{-generic filter over }\mathfrak{A}\implies(&\mathrm{UC}(s,(f"\pi)"(f"g))\text{ is a filter on }s\text{ and}\\
&\Phi^{(f"g)\oplus s}=\mathrm{UC}(s,(f"\pi)"(f"g))))\end{split}

(5.7)

for some oracle machine
\Phi
.

Proposition 5.58
.

Let
g
be a
\mathbb{C}
-generic subset over
\mathfrak{A}
. Then
\mathrm{UC}(\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})^{\top},\pi"g)
is a
\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})^{\top}
-generic subset over
\mathfrak{A}
.

Proof.

Let
h
denote
\mathrm{UC}(\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})^{\top},\pi"g)
, and
D
be a dense subset of
\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})^{\top}
definable in the language associated with
\mathfrak{A}
. Then the set

\displaystyle D^{\prime}:=\{p\in C:\exists q\ (q\in D\text{ and }\pi(p)\leq_{\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})^{\top}}q)\}

is also definable in the language associated with
\mathfrak{A}
. Choose any
p_{0}\in C
. By the density of
D
in
\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})^{\top}
, there is
q_{0}\in D
such that
q_{0}\leq_{\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})^{\top}}\pi(p_{0})
. That
\pi
is a dense embedding tells us there exists
q_{1}\in ran(\pi)
with
q_{1}\leq_{\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})^{\top}}q_{0}
. Now, for some
p_{1}\leq_{\mathbb{C}}p_{0}
,
q_{1}=\pi(p_{1})
and
p_{1}\in D^{\prime}
. We can therefore conclude that
D^{\prime}
is dense in
\mathbb{C}
.

As
g
is
\mathbb{C}
-generic over
\mathfrak{A}
, we can find
p\in g\cap D^{\prime}
. Seeing that
\pi(p)\in h
and
h
is upward closed, we have by the definition of
D^{\prime}
,
h\cap D\neq\emptyset
.
∎

Passing Proposition
5.58
through
f
strengthens (
5.7
) to

\displaystyle\begin{split}\forall g\ (g\text{ is a }\mathbb{C}\text{-generic filter over }\mathfrak{A}\implies(&\mathrm{UC}(s,(f"\pi)"(f"g))\text{ is a }s\text{-generic}\\
&\text{filter over }\mathfrak{A}\text{ and}\\
&\Phi^{(f"g)\oplus s}=\mathrm{UC}(s,(f"\pi)"(f"g))))\text{.}\end{split}

(5.8)

Now (
5.6
) and (
5.8
) in conjunction tells us that we can combine
\bar{\Phi}
and
\Phi
into an oracle machine
\Psi
such that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\forall g\ \exists!\mathcal{M}_{g}\ (&g\text{ is a }\mathbb{C}\text{-generic filter over }\mathfrak{A}\\
&\implies(\mathcal{M}_{g}\models^{*}\mathfrak{T}\text{ and }\Psi^{(f"g)\oplus s}=f"(\Sigma(\mathfrak{T},\mathcal{M}_{g}))))\end{split}

(5.9)

and

\displaystyle F_{\Psi}:=((g\text{ a }\mathbb{C}\text{-generic filter over }\mathfrak{A})\mapsto\mathcal{M}_{g}\text{ as per (\ref{number10p})})=F_{\bar{\Phi}}\circ F_{\Phi}\text{,}

where

\displaystyle F_{\bar{\Phi}}:=\

\displaystyle(\bar{g}\text{ a }s\text{-generic filter over }\mathfrak{A})\mapsto\mathcal{M}_{\bar{g}}\text{ as per (\ref{marker2}), and}

\displaystyle F_{\Phi}:=\

\displaystyle(g\text{ a }\mathbb{C}\text{-generic filter over }\mathfrak{A})\mapsto\mathrm{UC}(s,(f"\pi)"(f"g))\text{ as per (\ref{number8p})}

are both injective.
∎

Remark 5.59
.

Observe that we derived
\pi
in a uniform way from the parameters given in Proposition
5.57
. Turning our attention to the proof of Lemma
5.56
, said observation passes through
f
to imply
F_{\Phi}
is derivable uniformly in
\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})^{\top}
, and thus in
\mathfrak{T}
. Obviously,
F_{\bar{\Phi}}
is derivable uniformly in
\mathfrak{T}
, so
F_{\Psi}
is too. As a result, this same
\Psi
works uniformly in
\mathfrak{T}
to witness Lemma
5.56
for all
\mathfrak{T}
and
g
as given in said lemma.

In addition to Remark
5.59
, we can also strengthen Lemma
5.56
by lowering the requirement on the genericity of
g
and omitting
(A;\in)
altogether. We formulate a strengthened version below in the nomenclature of computability theory.

Lemma 5.60
.

Let
(r,f)
witness
(H(\omega);\in)
is computable. Then there is an oracle machine
\Psi
such that whenever
\mathfrak{T}
is a code-friendly
\Pi_{2}
TCI, one of the following must hold.

(1)

All models of
\mathfrak{T}
are almost finitely determined.

(2)

For every
(f"(P(\mathfrak{T})^{\top}))
-
1
-generic real
t
, there is a unique model
\mathcal{M}_{t}
of
\mathfrak{T}
satisfying

\Psi^{t\oplus(f"(P(\mathfrak{T})^{\top}))}=f"(\Sigma(\mathfrak{T},\mathcal{M}_{t})).

Moreover for every
\mathfrak{T}
, the function
t\mapsto\mathcal{M}_{t}
defined as such is injective.

Proof.

Choose an arbitrary a code-friendly
\Pi_{2}
TCI
\mathfrak{T}
with not all models almost finitely determined. Adopt the abbreviations

\displaystyle s:=\

\displaystyle f"(P(\mathfrak{T})^{\top})\text{ and}

\displaystyle\leq_{s}\ :=\

\displaystyle f"(\leq_{\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})^{\top}})\text{,}

and identify
s
with
(s,\leq_{s})
whenever contextually necessary. We will modify the proof of Lemma
5.56
to get an oracle machine
\Psi
witnessing
(2)
, before checking that a very slightly modified version of Remark
5.59
applies to
\Psi
.

Let
\mathfrak{A}=(H(\omega);\in,P(\mathfrak{T})^{\top})
, so that

•

(H(\omega);\in)
is a transitive model of a sufficiently strong set theory, and

•

\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}}
is a
\Delta_{1}
-definable subset of
H(\omega)
over the
\mathfrak{A}
,

whence
\mathfrak{A}
is
\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}}
-suitable. We argue as in the proof of Lemma
5.56
to conclude

(1)

all but the last two points in the hypothesis of Lemma
3.56
hold with

\circ

\mathfrak{A}
as defined,

\circ

|\mathfrak{A}_{\mathfrak{T}}|=|trcl(\mathfrak{A}_{\mathfrak{T}})|
in place of
\lambda
,

\circ

\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}}
in place of
\mathcal{L}
,

\circ

[\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}}]^{<\omega}
in place of
B
,

\circ

P(\mathfrak{T})^{\top}
in place of
P
,

\circ

\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})^{\top}
in place of
\mathbb{P}
, and

\circ

\Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\top}
in place of
\Gamma
;

(2)

in particular,
\Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}}^{\top}
is a set of
(\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}})^{*}_{\mathfrak{A}}
-
\Pi_{2}
sentences.

Now apply Lemma
3.56
with
V
in place of
W
, as well as the substitutions in
(1)
, to arrive at (
5.4
).

Notice that

•

H(\omega)
is closed under the function
\chi_{\mathfrak{T}}
as defined in Remark
5.18
, and

•

the
f
-image of
\chi_{\mathfrak{T}}
is
\Delta^{0}_{0}
(i.e. computable),

from which we deduce the following:

(3)

the
f
-image of each
\Sigma_{1}
-definable subset of
P(\mathfrak{T})^{\top}
over
\mathfrak{A}
is
\Sigma^{0,s}_{1}
.

Passing (
5.4
) and
(3)
through
f
the way (
5.5
) was passed through
f
in the proof of Lemma
5.56
, for some oracle machine
\bar{\Phi}
we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\forall\bar{g}\ \exists!\mathcal{M}_{\bar{g}}\ (&\bar{g}\text{ is a filter on }s\text{ meeting all }\Sigma^{0,s}_{1}\text{ subsets of }s\\
&\implies(\mathcal{M}_{\bar{g}}\models^{*}\mathfrak{T}\text{ and }\bar{\Phi}^{\bar{g}}=f"(\Sigma(\mathfrak{T},\mathcal{M}_{\bar{g}}))))\text{.}\end{split}

(5.10)

Adopt the abbreviations

\displaystyle u:=\

\displaystyle f"C\text{ and}

\displaystyle\leq_{u}\ :=\

\displaystyle f"(\leq_{\mathbb{C}})\text{,}

and identify
u
with
(u,\leq_{u})
whenever contextually necessary.

To deal with generic reals instead of generic filter, we first fix an oracle machine
\ddot{\Phi}
that computes the set

f"\{t\!\restriction_{n}:n<\omega\}

when given any real
t
as oracle. Next, we follow an argument similar to the one used to derive (
5.7
) in the proof of Lemma
5.56
, so that for some

•

dense embedding
\pi
of
\mathbb{C}
into
\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})^{\top}
with
f"\pi
computable in
s
, and

•

oracle machine
\Phi
,

we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\forall t\ \exists!c\ (t\text{ is a }s\text{-}1\text{-generic real}\implies(&\mathrm{UC}(s,(f"\pi)"c)\text{ is a filter on }s\text{ and}\\
&\ddot{\Phi}^{t}=c\text{ and }\Phi^{c\oplus s}=\mathrm{UC}(s,(f"\pi)"c)))\text{.}\end{split}

(5.11)

The upcoming proposition is an analogue of Proposition
5.58
, formulated to restrict the universe of discourse to the (even) natural numbers.

Proposition 5.61
.

Let
c
be a subset of
u
meeting all
\Sigma^{0,s}_{1}
subsets of
u
. Then

\mathrm{UC}(s,(f"\pi)"c)
meets all
\Sigma^{0,s}_{1}
subsets of
s
.

Proof.

Let
z
be the subset of
s
defined by a
\Sigma^{0,s}_{1}
formula
\varphi(x)
in one free variable. Define

a_{z}:=\{p\in u:\exists q\ (\varphi(q)\text{ and }(f"\pi)(p)\leq_{s}q)\}\text{.}

That
u
,
f"\pi
and
\leq_{s}
are all computable in
s
(
u
is even outright computable) gives us the
\Sigma^{0,s}_{1}
-definability of
a_{z}
as a subset of
u
. Consequently,
c
must meet
a_{z}
.

If
c\cap a_{z}\neq\emptyset
, then by the definition of
a_{z}
, there are conditions
p\in c
and
q\in z
for which
(f"\pi)(p)\leq_{s}q
, so
\mathrm{UC}(s,(f"\pi)"c)
meets
z
. Otherwise, there is
p\in c
that cannot be extended in
u
to a member of
a_{z}
. Consider any
q\leq_{s}(f"\pi)(p)
. As
f"\pi
densely embeds
u
into
s
, we can find conditions
p^{\prime}\in u
and
q^{\prime}\in s
for which
q^{\prime}\leq_{s}q
and
(f"\pi)(p^{\prime})=q^{\prime}
. Now
p^{\prime}\leq_{u}p
, which according to our choice of
p
, means
p^{\prime}\not\in a_{z}
. Unfurling the definition of
a_{z}
gives us
q\not\in z
. Having thus shown that
(f"\pi)(p)
cannot be extended in
s
to a member of
z
, we are done.
∎

We can passing the definition of a
s
-
1
-generic real through
f
to conclude that for every such real
t
,
\ddot{\Phi}^{t}
is a subset of
u
meeting all
\Sigma^{0,s}_{1}
subsets of
u
. With (
5.10
) and (
5.11
) in mind, Proposition
5.61
then tells us that we can combine
\bar{\Phi}
,
\ddot{\Phi}
and
\Phi
to get an oracle machine
\Psi
fulfilling the requirements

\displaystyle\forall t\ \exists!\mathcal{M}_{t}\ (t\text{ is a }s\text{-}1\text{-generic}\implies(\mathcal{M}_{t}\models^{*}\mathfrak{T}\text{ and }\Psi^{t\oplus s}=f"(\Sigma(\mathfrak{T},\mathcal{M}_{t}))))

(5.12)

and

\displaystyle F_{\Psi}:=((t\text{ a }s\text{-}1\text{-generic real})\mapsto\mathcal{M}_{t}\text{ as per (\ref{lastreq})})=F_{\bar{\Phi}}\circ F_{\Phi}\circ F_{\ddot{\Phi}}\text{,}

where all of

\displaystyle F_{\bar{\Phi}}:=\

\displaystyle(\bar{g}\text{ a filter on }s\text{ meeting all }\Sigma^{0,s}_{1}\text{ subsets of }s)\mapsto\mathcal{M}_{\bar{g}}\text{ as per (\ref{number9}),}

\displaystyle F_{\Phi}:=\

\displaystyle(c\text{ a filter on }u\text{ meeting all }\Sigma^{0,s}_{1}\text{ subsets of }u)

\displaystyle\mapsto\mathrm{UC}(s,(f"\pi)"c)\text{ as per (\ref{number10}), and}

\displaystyle F_{\ddot{\Phi}}:=\

\displaystyle(t\text{ a }s\text{-}1\text{-generic real})\mapsto f"\{t\!\restriction_{n}:n<\omega\}

are injective.

The argument in Remark
5.59
applies here to net us the uniformity of deriving
F_{\bar{\Phi}}
and
F_{\Phi}
from
\mathfrak{T}
, and clearly
F_{\ddot{\Phi}}
does not depend on
\mathfrak{T}
at all. As in Remark
5.59
, we can then conclude that
\Psi
is the required witness to the lemma.
∎

Fix any nicely computable code
r
of
H(\omega)
. Check that

•

the function
f
given in Lemma
5.60
is a definable subset of
H(\omega)
over the structure
(H(\omega);\in,r)
, in light of Fact
5.54
,

•

the oracle machine
\Psi
constructed in the proof of Lemma
5.60
is a definable element of
H(\omega)
over the structure
(H(\omega);\in,r,f)
, and

•

the injective function
t\mapsto f"\Sigma(\mathfrak{T},\mathcal{M}_{t})
defined in the proof of Lemma
5.60
always has a left inverse computable using only
f_{r}"(P(\mathfrak{T})^{\top})
as parameter. Further, said left inverse is uniformly computable over all relevant code-friendly
\Pi_{2}
TCIs
\mathfrak{T}
.

As such, we have actually proven a more general version of Lemma
5.60
, which we formally present as our final theorem below.

Theorem 5.62
.

There is a formula
\psi_{gmc}
in two free variables, absolute for transitive models of
\mathsf{ZFC-Powerset}
, such that
\psi_{gmc}(r,(f_{r},\Psi_{r},\bar{\Psi}_{r}))
defines a function

r\mapsto(f_{r},\Psi_{r},\bar{\Psi}_{r})

on the set of all nicely computable codes of
H(\omega)
, wherewith

(1)

(r,f_{r})
witnesses
(H(\omega);\in)
is computable,

(2)

\Psi_{r}
and
\bar{\Psi}_{r}
are oracle machines, and

(3)

whenever
\mathfrak{T}
is a code-friendly
\Pi_{2}
TCI, one of the following must hold:

(a)

All models of
\mathfrak{T}
are almost finitely determined.

(b)

For every
(f_{r}"(P(\mathfrak{T})^{\top}))
-
1
-generic real
t
, there is a unique model
\mathcal{M}_{t}
of
\mathfrak{T}
satisfying

\displaystyle\Psi_{r}^{t\oplus(f_{r}"(P(\mathfrak{T})^{\top}))}=f_{r}"(\Sigma(\mathfrak{T},\mathcal{M}_{t}))\text{ and}

\displaystyle\bar{\Psi}_{r}^{(f_{r}"(\Sigma(\mathfrak{T},\mathcal{M}_{t})))\oplus(f_{r}"(P(\mathfrak{T})^{\top}))}=t.

In particular, for each such pair
(t,\mathcal{M}_{t})
,

t\oplus(f_{r}"(P(\mathfrak{T})^{\top}))\equiv_{T}(f_{r}"(\Sigma(\mathfrak{T},\mathcal{M}_{t})))\oplus(f_{r}"(P(\mathfrak{T})^{\top})).

From Theorem
5.62
, we can prove that certain
T
-substructures of a countable structure have the prefect set property.

Corollary 5.63
.

Let
\mathfrak{A}=(A;\mathcal{I})
and
T
be a countable structure and a first-order
\Pi_{2}
theory respectively, over the same signature
\sigma
. Then
|\mathrm{Sub}(\mathfrak{A},T)|\leq\aleph_{0}
or
|\mathrm{Sub}(\mathfrak{A},T)|=2^{\aleph_{0}}
.

Proof.

Clearly
\mathrm{Sub}(\mathfrak{A},T)
is invariant under isomorphisms, so without loss of generality, we can assume
A
is some ordinal
\alpha
with
\alpha\leq\omega
, and
dom(\mathcal{I})\subset H(\omega)\setminus\omega
. By Example
5.5
, there is a
\Pi_{2}
TCI
\mathfrak{T}
such that

\{\text{models of }\mathfrak{T}\}=\mathrm{Sub}(\mathfrak{A},T).

Our assumptions on
\mathfrak{A}
allow us to choose
\mathfrak{T}
satisfying
\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}}\subset H(\omega)
, so that
\mathfrak{T}
is also code-friendly. Note that
|\mathrm{Sub}(\mathfrak{A},T)|\leq 2^{|A|}\leq 2^{\aleph_{0}}
.

If all models of
\mathfrak{T}
are almost finitely generated, then Lemma
5.47
tells us that the number of models of
\mathfrak{T}
is bounded above by

max\{|P(\mathfrak{T})|,|[\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}}]^{<\omega}|\}\leq\aleph_{0},

which means
|\mathrm{Sub}(\mathfrak{A},T)|\leq\aleph_{0}
. Otherwise, by Theorem
5.62
, for some real
X
there is an injection from the set of
X
-
1
-generic reals into the set of models of
\mathfrak{T}
. As there are continuum many
X
-
1
-generic reals,
|\mathrm{Sub}(\mathfrak{A},T)|=2^{\aleph_{0}}
.
∎

Corollary
5.63
also follows from a well-known fact in descriptive set theory (see e.g.
[
8
]
), via the Cantor-Bendixson theorem.

Fact 5.64
.

Let
\mathfrak{A}=(A;\mathcal{I})
and
T
be a countable structure and a first-order theory respectively, over the same signature
\sigma
. Then
\mathrm{Sub}(\mathfrak{A},T)
is a closed set in some Polish space.

Precisely because Theorem
5.62
establishes a very strong version of perfect set property, it makes sense to treat it like a souped-up variant of the Cantor-Bendixson theorem, subject to restrictions on use cases. Instead of being applicable to arbitrary closed subsets of some Polish space, Theorem
5.62
only applies to sets which contain exactly all the models of some
\Pi_{2}
TCI. This treatment comes off as a natural extension of the parallels we drew in Remark
5.45
.

5.4
Open Questions

The study of how abundant generic models of a TCI are, can be approached from another direction: by comparing them with arbitrary models of the same TCI. As such, the propensity for a model of a TCI to be isomorphic to a generic model becomes of fundamental interest. In view of much of the work done in this section, the following is a most natural question.

(Q3)

Is there a consistent
\Pi_{2}
TCI
\mathfrak{T}
such that every model of
\mathfrak{T}
found in some outer model of
V
is isomorphic to a
V
-generic model of
\mathfrak{T}
?

Consider any consistent first-order
\Pi_{2}
theory
T
with only finite models (there are many such theories with the empty signature).
T
can be used to define a
\Pi_{2}
TCI
\mathfrak{T}
such that every model of
T
is isomorphic to some model of
\mathfrak{T}
, and vice versa. This relation between
T
and
\mathfrak{T}
remains true in all outer models of
V
. Hence,
(Q3)
can be answered in the affirmative.

Even if we require
\mathfrak{T}
to have an infinite model in each of these questions, the same answers apply when we choose
\mathfrak{T}
to be a
\Pi_{2}
TCI such that all models of
\mathfrak{T}
are isomorphic to the unique (up to isomorphism)
\aleph_{0}
-sized model of a
\aleph_{0}
-categorical first-order
\Pi_{2}
theory
T
(say, the theory of dense linear orders without endpoints). As the
\aleph_{0}
-categoricity of a theory is absolute for transitive models of
\mathsf{ZFC}
with the same ordinals, said relation between
T
and
\mathfrak{T}
is preserved across outer models of
V
.

However, the question dual to
(Q3)
appears more difficult.

(Q4)

Is there a
\Pi_{2}
TCI
\mathfrak{T}
with a model
\mathcal{M}
in some outer model of
V
such that
\mathcal{M}
is not isomorphic to any
V
-generic model of
\mathfrak{T}
?

Since our impetus for studying TCIs stems from our interest in uncovering links between forcing/genericity and the semantics of first-order logic, it is perhaps fitting that we ask for a similar example of a first-order theory.

(Q5)

Is there a first-order
\Pi_{2}
theory
T
with a model
M
in some outer model of
V
such that for no
\Pi_{2}
TCI
\mathfrak{T}
is
M
isomorphic to a
V
-generic model of
\mathfrak{T}
?

By Lemma
5.30
, it seems that
(Q4)
and
(Q5)
cannot be solved using set forcing alone. Thankfully, class forcing has been developed sufficiently to answer them. Essentially, we “cheat” by choosing a close-to-trivial TCI, only possible models of which are of the form
(\omega;\in,A)
, where
A
can be any real. By way of Jensen’s coding-the-universe forcing, we can get to an outer model of
V
with a new non-generic real
r
. Now the model of our TCI with
r
as the predicate cannot be isomorphic to
any
member of
any
forcing extension of
V
.

So, for TCIs with very simple theories, we can construct a non-generic model. We cannot do the same for all
\Pi_{2}
TCIs because of Lemma
5.34
. Together, they make us wonder if a clear line can be drawn in
V
. Let

\displaystyle\mathrm{NG}_{1}:=\{\mathfrak{T}\in V:\

\displaystyle\mathfrak{T}\text{ is a }\Pi_{2}\text{ TCI and }\exists W\ \exists\mathcal{M}\!\in\!W\ \forall x\!\in\!W\ (W\text{ is an outer model of }V

\displaystyle\text{and }\mathcal{M}\models^{*}\mathfrak{T}\text{ and }x\not\cong\mathcal{M}\text{ whenever }x\text{ is a }V\text{-generic model of }\mathfrak{T})\}

\displaystyle\mathrm{NG}_{2}:=\{T\in V:\

\displaystyle T\text{ is a }\Pi_{2}\text{ theory and }

\displaystyle\exists W\ \exists\mathcal{M}\!\in\!W\ \forall\mathfrak{T}\!\in\!V\ \forall x\!\in\!W

\displaystyle(W\text{ is an outer model of }V\text{ and }\mathcal{M}\models T\text{ and }

\displaystyle x\not\cong\mathcal{M}\text{ whenever }\mathfrak{T}\text{ is a TCI and }x\text{ is a }V\text{-generic model of }\mathfrak{T})\}\text{.}

Question 5.65
.

Is
\mathrm{NG}_{1}
definable in
V
?

Question 5.66
.

Is
\mathrm{NG}_{2}
definable in
V
?

Our current line of questioning can be extended to the paradigm of relative effectiveness.

Fix
\psi_{gmc}
to be as in Theorem
5.62
. Let
r
be a nicely computable code of
H(\omega)
. Define

\displaystyle f_{r}:=\

\displaystyle\text{the unique }f\text{ for which there are }\Psi\text{ and }\bar{\Psi}\text{ satisfying }\psi_{gmc}(r,(f,\Psi,\bar{\Psi}))\text{,}

\displaystyle\Psi_{r}:=\

\displaystyle\text{the unique }\Psi\text{ for which there are }f\text{ and }\bar{\Psi}\text{ satisfying }\psi_{gmc}(r,(f,\Psi,\bar{\Psi}))\text{, and}

\displaystyle\bar{\Psi}_{r}:=\

\displaystyle\text{the unique }\bar{\Psi}\text{ for which there are }f\text{ and }\Psi\text{ satisfying }\psi_{gmc}(r,(f,\Psi,\bar{\Psi}))\text{.}

Analogous to Question
5.65
, we want to pick out every code-friendly TCI
\mathfrak{T}
with a model
M
that neither almost finitely determined nor isomorphic to any
\mathcal{M}_{t}
born from a
(f_{r}"(P(\mathfrak{T})^{\top}))
-
1
-generic real
t
à la Theorem
5.62
. As turns out, because
f_{r}\circ f_{s}^{-1}
is computable for any other nicely computable code
s
of
H(\omega)
, the answer to this question is independent of
the choice of
r
.

Let
\mathrm{NGE}^{\prime}_{1}
be the set containing exactly all the code-friendly
\Pi_{2}
TCIs
\mathfrak{T}
with a model
\mathcal{M}
such that

(a)

\mathcal{M}
is not almost finitely determined, and

(b)

for every nicely computable code
r
of
H(\omega)
and every
(f_{r}"(P(\mathfrak{T})^{\top}))
-
1
-generic real
t
, if
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
is a model of
\mathfrak{T}
satisfying

\Psi_{r}^{t\oplus(f_{r}"(P(\mathfrak{T})^{\top}))}=f_{r}"(\Sigma(\mathfrak{T},\mathcal{M}^{\prime}))\text{,}

then
\mathcal{M}\not\cong\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
.

We are interested in representing
\mathrm{NGE}^{\prime}_{1}
as a set of reals, so fix a nicely computable code
r
of
H(\omega)
and set

\mathrm{NGE}_{1}:=\{(f_{r}"\Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}})\oplus(f_{r}"\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}}):\mathfrak{T}\in\mathrm{NGE}^{\prime}_{1}\}\text{.}

Notice that the pair
(\Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}},\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}})
completely determines
\mathfrak{T}
. Further, both
f_{r}"\Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}}
and
f_{r}"\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}}
are well-defined by
(CF1)
.

Question 5.67
.

Is
\mathrm{NGE}_{1}
a
\mathbf{\Delta^{1}_{0}}
set of reals?

We can replace
\mathrm{NGE}_{1}
Question
5.67
with another set to get an analogue of Question
5.66
in the same spirit of relative effectiveness.

Let
\mathrm{NGE}^{\prime}_{2}
be the set containing exactly all the first-order
\Pi_{2}
theories
T
with a model
M
such that whenever
\mathfrak{T}
is a code-friendly
\Pi_{2}
TCI and
\mathcal{M}\models^{*}\mathfrak{T}
,

(a)

if
\mathcal{M}
is almost finitely determined then
M\not\cong\mathcal{M}
, and

(b)

if
r
is a nicely computable code of
H(\omega)
and
t
is a
(f_{r}"(P(\mathfrak{T})^{\top}))
-
1
-generic real for which

\Psi_{r}^{t\oplus(f_{r}"(P(\mathfrak{T})^{\top}))}=f_{r}"(\Sigma(\mathfrak{T},\mathcal{M}))\text{,}

then
M\not\cong\mathcal{M}
.

Fix a nicely computable code
r
of
H(\omega)
and set

\mathrm{NGE}_{2}:=\{(f_{r}"\Gamma_{\mathfrak{T}})\oplus(f_{r}"\mathcal{L}_{\mathfrak{T}}):\mathfrak{T}\in\mathrm{NGE}^{\prime}_{2}\}\text{.}

Question 5.68
.

Is
\mathrm{NGE}_{2}
a
\mathbf{\Delta^{1}_{0}}
set of reals?

In search of further evidence that
\hat{\mathbb{P}}
is a useful lens through which one can classify the reach of forcing as a technique, we ask the next few questions following the directions of Remarks
5.35
and
5.38
.

Question 5.69
.

Let
\mathfrak{T}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{T}_{2}
be
\Pi_{2}
TCIs such that
\mathfrak{T}_{1}\trianglelefteq\mathfrak{T}_{2}
.

(1)

If
\mathcal{M}\models^{*}\mathfrak{T}_{2}
in an outer model of
V
, must
V[\mathcal{M}]
contain a model of
\mathfrak{T}_{1}
?

(2)

If
\mathcal{M}\models^{*}\mathfrak{T}_{1}
in an outer model of
V
, must there be
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}
is some outer model of
V
such that
\mathcal{M}^{\prime}\models^{*}\mathfrak{T}_{2}
and
V[\mathcal{M}]\subset V[\mathcal{M}^{\prime}]
?

Question 5.70
.

Is there a “naturally definable” class
\mathcal{C}
such that

(a)

\mathcal{C}\subsetneq\{\mathfrak{T}:\mathfrak{T}\text{ is a }\Pi_{2}\text{ TCI}\}
, and

(b)

every member of
\trianglelefteq/\sim_{T}
contains a member
\mathfrak{T}
of
\mathcal{C}
for which

\displaystyle\{V[\mathcal{M}]:\mathcal{M}\models^{*}\mathfrak{T}\text{ in an outer model of }V\}

\displaystyle=\{V[g]:g\text{ is }\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})\text{-generic over }V\}\text{?}

Question 5.71
.

Is there a “naturally definable” class
\mathcal{C}
of TCIs such that

(a)

\mathcal{C}\supsetneq\{\mathfrak{T}:\mathfrak{T}\text{ is a }\Pi_{2}\text{ TCI}\}
, and

(b)

for every
\mathfrak{T}\in\mathcal{C}
,

\displaystyle\{V[\mathcal{M}]:\mathcal{M}\models^{*}\mathfrak{T}\text{ in an outer model of }V\}

\displaystyle\supset\{V[g]:g\text{ is }\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{T})\text{-generic over }V\}\text{?}
